  3. A page shows 50 products and, for each one, its number of reviews, loaded in a loop. How many queries is that, and what is the fix?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Changing the schema by handMachines differ; nobody knows the real schemaMigrations, in the repository
Editing a migration that ranDatabases silently divergeA new migration
A down that never ranNo way back when you need itUp, down, up in CI
Breaking changes in one stepErrors while old and new code run togetherExpand, migrate data, contract
Storing what can be computedTwo truths that disagreeStore facts; cache later, on purpose
Types written by handTypes that lie about the schemaGenerate them after every migration
Queries in loops1 + N round tripsJoins, IN, or JSON aggregation

Summary

  • Model entities and relationships; the many side holds the foreign key; many-to-many uses a joining table with its own facts.
  • Store facts, compute the rest; put every unbreakable rule in the schema; decide what deleting does.
  • Migrations are ordered, recorded, reversible changes in code. Never edit one that ran; test up, down, up.
  • Expand and contract changes a live schema in compatible steps.
  • kysely-codegen generates types from the real database; map bigint deliberately.
  • Kysely writes typed SQL; result types follow the selection; JSON aggregation shapes nested data in one query.
  • N+1: one query per row of a list. Count queries, and load related data together.

Quick check answers: 1. Other databases have already run it and will never run it again, so the change would exist only on some machines; fix mistakes with a new migration. 2. The existing rows have no value, so the database refuses the constraint (or, with a default, every row gets a meaningless value), and the old code still running does not send the column; add it as optional, fill it, then require it. 3. 1 + 50 = 51 queries; load the counts in the same query with a join and GROUP BY or a subquery, or in one second query with WHERE product_id IN (…).

Practice projects

Each project continues this chapter’s Kysely project. Every schema change is a migration with a working down, followed by regenerated types.

Project 5.1 · Easy

Addresses

Give customers several delivery addresses, one of them the default, and attach an address to each order.

You will practise: one-to-many design, migrations with foreign keys, partial unique indexes, and typed queries.

  1. Write a migration for addresses (customer, label, street, city, is_default) and its down.
  2. Allow at most one default address per customer with a partial unique index on customer_id where is_default is true.
  3. Add orders.address_id with expand and contract: optional first, then required once old orders are backfilled.
  4. Write a typed query that returns a customer with all their addresses, default first, in one query.

Hint: should an order point to the address, or copy it? If a customer later edits the address, an old order’s delivery address must not change. That is the same reasoning as the price in order_items.

Project 5.2 · Medium

Migrations in CI

Make CI prove on every pull request that migrations run up and down on an empty database, and that the generated types are up to date.

You will practise: GitHub Actions service containers, scripted migrations, and detecting drift.

  1. In a workflow, start PostgreSQL as a service container, and run npm run migrate.
  2. Add a migrate:reset script that migrates down until nothing is left, then run it, then migrate up again.
  3. Regenerate the types, and fail the job if git diff --exit-code src/db-types.ts finds a difference.
  4. Open a pull request that changes a migration without regenerating the types, and check that CI stops it.

Hint: Kysely’s migrateDown() undoes one migration at a time; loop until results is empty.

Project 5.3 · Challenge

A rename without downtime

Rename customers.name to full_name on a running API, step by step, with traffic flowing the whole time.

You will practise: expand and contract, dual writes, backfilling in batches, and proving compatibility.

  1. Start the API from Chapter 3 reading customers from this database, and a script that creates and reads customers in a loop, reporting any error.
  2. Expand: add full_name. Deploy code that writes both columns and still reads name.
  3. Backfill full_name in batches of 1,000 rows, outside the migration.
  4. Deploy code that reads full_name; then contract: make it NOT NULL and drop name.
  5. Keep the loop running through every step, and check it never reported an error.

Hint: a database trigger can keep the two columns in step instead of the application, which helps when several applications write the table. Either way, write down which step you are at: a rename in progress is a state the team must know about.

Exercises

  1. Easy Write a migration that adds an optional city column to customers, with its down. Run it, undo it, and run it again.
  2. Easy The generated type of orders.status is string, although the database allows only four values. Why, and how can the generator produce the union type instead?
  3. Medium Plan the steps to rename products.name to title without downtime. For each step, say which code versions can run safely.
  4. Medium A report shows every product with the total quantity sold, written as one query for the products and one per product for the sum. Rewrite it as one Kysely query.
  5. Challenge Write a script that checks that all migrations work in both directions: on an empty database, run them all up, then all down, then all up again, and fail on any error.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 6

Two customers order the last robot kit at the same moment. Next we see what really happens inside the database when requests run at the same time, and use transactions, isolation levels and locks to keep the data correct.

6

Chapter 6

Transactions, Locks and Consistency

In this chapter you will learn to

  • group changes into transactions that happen completely or not at all
  • see a race condition sell one item ten times, and fix it three different ways
  • choose an isolation level, knowing what each lets a transaction see
  • recognise deadlocks, and prevent them with a consistent lock order
  • retry the transactions the database refuses, safely
  • keep side effects such as emails and messages out of transactions that might roll back

Everything in Part 1 quietly assumed one request at a time. A real shop serves many customers at once, and the database runs their requests side by side. Most of the time they touch different rows and nothing interesting happens. Then two customers buy the last robot kit at the same moment, and the shop sells it twice. These bugs never show in development, where you are the only user; they appear under load, rarely, and they cost money. This chapter makes them happen on purpose, so you can see them, and then makes them impossible.

Bit

A bug report said: “we sold 103 tickets for a room of 100”. I could not reproduce it. Every test passed, every read of the code looked right. It only happened when three people pressed “buy” in the same tenth of a second. It took me a week to understand that my code was correct for one user and wrong for two.

6.1 Transactions: all or nothing

A transaction groups statements so that they act as one. It has four guarantees, known as ACID:

  • Atomicity: either every change is kept, or none is.
  • Consistency: every rule of the schema holds at the end.
  • Isolation: transactions running at the same time do not see each other’s unfinished work (exactly how much they see is the isolation level, 6.3).
  • Durability: once committed, a change survives a crash.

This chapter uses two small tables:

schema.sql
CREATE TABLE products (
  id    bigint PRIMARY KEY,
  name  text NOT NULL,
  price integer NOT NULL CHECK (price > 0),
  stock integer NOT NULL CHECK (stock >= 0)
);
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id         bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  product_id bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES products (id),
  buyer      integer NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO products VALUES (1, 'Robot kit', 45000, 1), (2, 'Spare wheels', 6000, 10);

BEGIN starts a transaction, COMMIT keeps its changes, ROLLBACK throws them away. Inside the transaction, the change is visible; after the rollback, it never happened:

Terminal
# A transaction: all or nothing
$ psql shop -a -f basics.sql 2>&1
BEGIN;
BEGIN
UPDATE products SET stock = stock + 5 WHERE id = 2;
UPDATE 1
SELECT name, stock FROM products WHERE id = 2;
     name     | stock 
--------------+-------
 Spare wheels |    15
(1 row)

ROLLBACK;
ROLLBACK
SELECT name, stock FROM products WHERE id = 2;
     name     | stock 
--------------+-------
 Spare wheels |    10
(1 row)

When one step fails, the transaction is aborted: PostgreSQL ignores every later statement, and even COMMIT turns into a ROLLBACK. The first update, which succeeded, is undone with the rest:

Terminal
# One step fails, so nothing happens
$ psql shop -a -f atomic.sql 2>&1
BEGIN;
BEGIN
UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 2;
UPDATE 1
UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 100 WHERE id = 1;
psql:atomic.sql:3: ERROR:  new row for relation "products" violates check constraint "products_stock_check"
DETAIL:  Failing row contains (1, Robot kit, 45000, -99).
SELECT name, stock FROM products;
psql:atomic.sql:4: ERROR:  current transaction is aborted, commands ignored until end of transaction block
COMMIT;
ROLLBACK
SELECT name, stock FROM products;
     name     | stock 
--------------+-------
 Robot kit    |     1
 Spare wheels |    10
(2 rows)

That is exactly what an order with several items needs: if the third item is out of stock, the stock of the first two must not be taken. Every statement you run outside BEGIN is a transaction of its own, committed at once.

6.2 A race: one kit, ten buyers

Here is the obvious way to sell a product: read the stock, check it in code, write the new stock, record the order. To see what happens under load, the program starts ten buyers at the same moment, each with its own database connection, for a product with one unit left. A short pause between reading and writing stands for what a real application does there, such as calling a payment service:

node/race.ts
async function buy(buyer: number) {
  const db = new pg.Client(url);
  await db.connect();
  try {
    for (;;) {
      try {
        await db.query(mode === "serializable" ? "BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE" : "BEGIN");
        if (mode === "atomic") {
          // one statement: the database checks and changes the stock together
          const taken = await db.query(
            "UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 1 AND stock > 0",
          );
          if (taken.rowCount === 1) {
            await db.query("INSERT INTO orders (product_id, buyer) VALUES (1, $1)", [buyer]);
          }
        } else {
          // read, decide in code, write: the classic race
          // FOR UPDATE locks the row: other buyers wait here until this transaction ends
          const lock = mode === "lock" ? " FOR UPDATE" : "";
          const { rows } = await db.query(`SELECT stock FROM products WHERE id = 1${lock}`);
          // the time a real app spends between reading and writing: a call to a payment service, say
          await db.query("SELECT pg_sleep(0.1)");
          if (rows[0].stock > 0) {
            await db.query("UPDATE products SET stock = $1 WHERE id = 1", [rows[0].stock - 1]);
            await db.query("INSERT INTO orders (product_id, buyer) VALUES (1, $1)", [buyer]);
          }
        }
        await db.query("COMMIT");
        return;
      } catch (error) {
        await db.query("ROLLBACK");
        // serialization failure: start the whole transaction again
        if ((error as { code?: string }).code !== "40001") throw error;
        retries++;
      }
    }
  } finally {
    await db.end();
  }
}
Terminal
# Ten buyers, one robot kit, four ways to sell it
$ node node/race.ts naive
naive        1 in stock, 10 sold, stock now 0
$ node node/race.ts atomic
atomic       1 in stock, 1 sold, stock now 0
$ node node/race.ts lock
lock         1 in stock, 1 sold, stock now 0
$ node node/race.ts serializable
serializable 1 in stock, 1 sold, stock now 0, 9 retries

The naive version sold one kit ten times. Every buyer read “1 in stock” before anyone had written, every buyer decided to sell, and every buyer wrote “0”. The transaction did not help: it makes changes all-or-nothing, but it does not stop two transactions from reading the same value. This is a race condition, a check-then-act bug: the check was true when it ran and false by the time the action happened.

The other three versions each sold it once. They are the three standard fixes:

  • An atomic update. UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 1 AND stock > 0 checks and changes in one statement. PostgreSQL locks the row while it updates, so the second buyer’s update waits, then re-checks stock > 0 against the new value and changes nothing. The number of changed rows tells the code whether it sold. When the rule fits in a WHERE, this is the simplest and fastest fix.
  • A row lock. SELECT … FOR UPDATE reads the row and locks it until the transaction ends. Every other buyer waits at that line, then reads the stock after the first one committed. Use it when the decision needs code between the read and the write (a price calculation, a call to another service), and keep such transactions short, because everyone waits.
  • Serializable isolation. The code stays naive, and the database guarantees that the result equals running the transactions one after another. When it detects that this is impossible, it refuses a transaction with error 40001, and the code runs it again from the start: here, nine of the ten buyers were refused once and found the stock empty on their retry.

Watch out: the check in code is never enough

Any rule of the form “only if …” (enough stock, a seat still free, a balance high enough, a username not taken) is a race when it is checked in code and then written. Put the rule where the database can enforce it at the moment of the write: in a conditional update, a unique constraint, a CHECK, or a lock. Chapter 4’s CHECK (stock >= 0) would also have stopped the oversale, with an error instead of a quiet second order.

6.3 Isolation levels

The isolation level decides what a transaction sees of other transactions that commit while it is running. Two connections show the difference: A reads a price, B changes it and commits, A reads again and then tries to change it:

node/isolation.ts
// A reads, B changes and commits, A reads again, then A tries to change it
await a.query(`BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL ${level}`);
console.log("A reads the price:", await price());
await b.query("UPDATE products SET price = 50000 WHERE id = 1");
console.log("B sets the price to 50000 and commits");
console.log("A reads it again:", await price());
try {
  await a.query("UPDATE products SET price = price + 1000 WHERE id = 1");
  await a.query("COMMIT");
  console.log("A adds 1000 to the price:", (await a.query("SELECT price FROM products WHERE id = 1")).rows[0].price);
} catch (error) {
  await a.query("ROLLBACK");
  console.log("A adds 1000 to the price: ERROR", (error as Error).message);
}
Terminal
# READ COMMITTED (PostgreSQL's default)
$ node node/isolation.ts
A reads the price: 45000
B sets the price to 50000 and commits
A reads it again: 50000
A adds 1000 to the price: 51000
# REPEATABLE READ
$ node node/isolation.ts 'REPEATABLE READ'
A reads the price: 45000
B sets the price to 50000 and commits
A reads it again: 45000
A adds 1000 to the price: ERROR could not serialize access due to concurrent update
  • READ COMMITTED, PostgreSQL’s default: every statement sees everything committed before it started. A’s second read saw B’s new price, so two reads in one transaction disagreed, and A’s update built on B’s value.
  • REPEATABLE READ: the transaction sees a snapshot taken at its first statement. A’s second read still saw 45000, so its reads are consistent with each other. And when A tried to update a row that B had changed since that snapshot, PostgreSQL refused: A was working from an old picture. The application must retry.
  • SERIALIZABLE adds detection of every pattern that could not happen if the transactions ran one after another, as in the race above. It is the safest level and needs retries the most.

A practical rule: keep the default, use atomic statements and constraints for the rules they can express, take row locks when code must decide between a read and a write, and use SERIALIZABLE (with retries) for complicated multi-row rules where it is hard to see every race. Reports that read many tables at once benefit from REPEATABLE READ, so their numbers come from one moment.

Quick check

  1. Why didn’t wrapping the naive version in a transaction stop the oversale?
  2. What does FOR UPDATE do to other transactions that read the same row?
  3. A transaction fails with error 40001. What should the application do?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

6.4 Deadlocks

Locks have their own trap. Transaction A locks product 1 and then wants product 2; transaction B has locked product 2 and wants product 1. Each waits for the other, forever:

node/deadlock.ts
// A locks product 1 then 2; B locks 2 then 1
async function run(name: string, first: number, second: number) {
  const db = new pg.Client(url);
  await db.connect();
  try {
    await db.query("BEGIN");
    await take(db, first);
    await sleep(200);
    await take(db, second);
    await db.query("COMMIT");
    console.log(`${name}: done`);
  } catch (error) {
    await db.query("ROLLBACK");
    console.log(`${name}: failed, ${(error as { code?: string }).code}, ${(error as Error).message}`);
  } finally {
    await db.end();
  }
}
Terminal
# Two transactions, the same two rows, in opposite orders
$ node node/deadlock.ts
B: failed, 40P01, deadlock detected
A: done

PostgreSQL checks for such cycles after a second of waiting (deadlock_timeout), picks one transaction, and aborts it with error 40P01, so the other can finish. Which one loses depends on timing. Two habits keep deadlocks rare: lock rows in a consistent order (for an order with several products, lock or update them sorted by id), and keep transactions short, with no network calls or user waits inside them. Like serialization failures, a deadlock victim can simply be retried.

6.5 Transactions in the framework

With Kysely from Chapter 5, a transaction is a callback; it commits when the callback returns and rolls back when it throws. Row locks and isolation levels are methods:

  • await db.transaction().setIsolationLevel("serializable").execute(async (trx) => { … }), using trx, not db, for every query inside.
  • trx.selectFrom("products").selectAll().where("id", "=", id).forUpdate().executeTakeFirst() for a row lock.

Kysely does not retry for you: wrap the call in a small loop that retries on codes 40001 and 40P01, a few times, with a short random pause between attempts. The race program above has exactly that loop.

6.6 Side effects and the outbox

A transaction can be rolled back; an email cannot be unsent. If the code sends “your order is confirmed” inside the transaction and the commit then fails (a deadlock, a constraint, a crash), the customer has an email for an order that does not exist. Sending after the commit has the opposite problem: if the process crashes between the commit and the send, the order exists and the email never goes.

The reliable pattern is the outbox: inside the same transaction as the order, insert a row into an outbox table describing the message to send. The order and its message commit together or not at all. A separate worker reads the outbox, sends each message, and marks it sent; if it crashes, it sends again later, so the receiver must tolerate duplicates (idempotency again). Chapter 10 builds the worker with a queue.

Professional habit: test races on purpose

A race does not appear in a test that runs one request at a time. For every “only if” rule (stock, seats, balances, unique names), write one test that starts many requests at the same moment, as this chapter’s race program does, and asserts on the final state: one sale, one booking, a balance never below zero. Such a test fails reliably on the naive code, which is the proof that it tests the right thing.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Check in code, then writeTwo requests both pass the checkAtomic conditional update, constraint, or row lock
Believing a transaction prevents racesOversales inside perfectly good transactionsLocks, constraints or SERIALIZABLE
No retry on 40001 and 40P01Errors for users at busy momentsRetry the whole transaction a few times
Locking rows in random orderDeadlocksAlways the same order, such as by id
Network calls inside a transactionLong locks, everyone waitsShort transactions; call before or after
Emails sent inside a transactionMessages about changes that rolled backAn outbox committed with the change
Testing concurrency one request at a timeRaces found by customersTests that start many requests together

Summary

  • A transaction makes several statements atomic: one failure, and COMMIT becomes ROLLBACK.
  • Transactions do not stop races: “read, check in code, write” sells one item many times under load.
  • Three fixes: an atomic conditional update, a row lock with FOR UPDATE, or SERIALIZABLE with retries.
  • READ COMMITTED sees each statement’s latest commits; REPEATABLE READ sees one snapshot and refuses updates to rows changed since.
  • Deadlocks are cycles of locks; PostgreSQL breaks them with 40P01. Lock in a consistent order, keep transactions short, retry.
  • Side effects belong after the commit, reliably through an outbox.

Quick check answers: 1. A transaction makes its own changes all-or-nothing, but under the default isolation level it does not stop other transactions from reading the same row at the same time, so all ten read “1” before any of them wrote. 2. Other SELECT … FOR UPDATE statements, and updates, on that row wait until the locking transaction commits or rolls back; plain SELECTs still read the last committed version without waiting. 3. Roll back and run the whole transaction again from the beginning, reading the data afresh, a limited number of times, ideally after a short random pause.

Practice projects

Each project is about correctness under concurrency. Prove each one with a test or a script that starts many requests at the same moment and checks the final state.

Project 6.1 · Easy

A safe stock reservation

Turn the race program into a reusable function that reserves several products for one order, safely, and prove it under load.

You will practise: atomic updates, transactions with several statements, consistent lock order, and concurrency tests.

  1. Write reserve(items): inside one transaction, take each product’s quantity with an atomic conditional update, and roll back the whole order if any item is short.
  2. Sort the items by product id before updating, and explain why.
  3. Start 50 concurrent orders for random baskets on a few products with small stocks, and check afterwards that no stock is negative and that the stock taken equals the quantities in the successful orders.
  4. Remove the sorting, run it again, and count the deadlocks.

Hint: the final check is the real test: the sum of quantities in successful orders, plus the remaining stock, must equal the starting stock, for every product.

Project 6.2 · Medium

Money between wallets

Build customer wallets with transfers that never lose or create money, whatever the concurrency.

You will practise: double-entry thinking, row locks in order, CHECK constraints, and invariants.

  1. Create wallets (customer, balance in piastres, CHECK (balance >= 0)) and transfers (from, to, amount, time).
  2. Write transfer(from, to, amount): lock both wallets with FOR UPDATE in id order, check the balance, move the money, and record the transfer, in one transaction.
  3. Run 1,000 random transfers concurrently between 20 wallets, and check that the total of all balances never changed.
  4. Rewrite it with SERIALIZABLE and no explicit locks, with retries, and compare the number of retries and the time taken.

Hint: real systems rarely store a balance alone; they keep a ledger of entries and compute or cache the balance from it. Your transfers table is the start of that ledger.

Project 6.3 · Challenge

An outbox that never loses a message

Send an order confirmation exactly when the order is committed, even if processes crash at the worst moments.

You will practise: the outbox pattern, FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, idempotent consumers, and crash testing.

  1. Create an outbox table (id, type, payload as JSON, created, sent). Insert a message in the same transaction as each order.
  2. Write a worker that, in a transaction, takes up to 10 unsent messages with FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, “sends” them (write them to a file), and marks them sent.
  3. Run three workers at once and check that no message is sent twice by different workers in normal operation.
  4. Kill a worker in the middle of a batch (after writing to the file, before committing) and show that the message is sent again: make the receiver ignore duplicates by message id.

Hint: SKIP LOCKED lets each worker skip the rows another worker has locked, so several workers share the queue without waiting for each other. It is how PostgreSQL can serve as a simple job queue.

Exercises

  1. Easy A transaction has updated three rows when the application crashes before COMMIT. What happens to the three changes, and to the locks on those rows?
  2. Easy Choose a fix for each, and say why: counting page views; booking a numbered seat; a username that must be unique.
  3. Medium Change the deadlock program so that both transactions update the two products without ever deadlocking, and run it.
  4. Medium Why is it wrong to call a payment service inside a transaction that holds a FOR UPDATE lock on the product? Propose a better order of steps for “reserve stock, charge the card, confirm the order”.
  5. Challenge Write a helper that runs a transaction function with retries on 40001 and 40P01: at most five attempts, a random pause that grows with each attempt, and the original error if all attempts fail.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 7

The database is now correct; next we make it fast for the questions asked thousands of times a minute. Redis keeps answers in memory: caching, invalidation, rate limits shared between servers, and locks across processes.

7

Chapter 7

Redis: Caching, Rate Limits and Locks

In this chapter you will learn to

  • use Redis from the command line: strings, counters, expiry, hashes and sorted sets
  • put a cache in front of a slow query, and keep it correct when the data changes
  • stop a stampede of requests from hitting the database at once, with a lock
  • take and release a lock across processes and servers safely
  • build a rate limit that several servers share
  • decide what belongs in Redis and what must stay in the database

PostgreSQL is careful: it writes to disk, checks constraints and keeps every transaction safe. That care has a price, measured in milliseconds, and some questions are asked thousands of times a minute with the same answer. Redis is the opposite kind of tool: a server that keeps everything in memory and answers in well under a millisecond. Almost every production backend has one beside its database, for three jobs this chapter builds for real: a cache, a lock that many processes respect, and counters that several servers share.

Bit

The first time I put a cache in front of our slowest page, its response time went from 900 milliseconds to 4. I felt like a magician for a week. Then a customer complained that a price they had changed an hour earlier still showed the old value, and I learned the other half of caching: knowing when the copy is wrong.

7.1 Redis in five minutes

Redis is a server that stores keys and their values in memory. Clients talk to it over the network, like a database, and redis-cli is its command-line client. The simplest values are strings, and a key can carry an expiry time:

Terminal
# Strings, counters and expiry
$ redis-cli SET greeting hello
OK
$ redis-cli GET greeting
"hello"
$ redis-cli GET nothing
(nil)
$ redis-cli INCR visits
(integer) 1
$ redis-cli INCR visits
(integer) 2
$ redis-cli SET session:7f3a 42 EX 1800
OK
$ redis-cli TTL session:7f3a
(integer) 1800
$ redis-cli SET once yes NX
OK
$ redis-cli SET once again NX
(nil)
  • GET of a key that does not exist answers (nil).
  • INCR adds 1 and returns the new value, in one step: two clients incrementing at the same moment get 1 and 2, never 1 and 1. Redis runs one command at a time, so every single command is atomic.
  • EX 1800 makes the key disappear after 1,800 seconds, and TTL says how long it has left. Sessions, caches and rate limits all depend on this.
  • NX sets the key only if it does not exist yet: the second SET … NX changed nothing. This one flag is the base of a lock (section 7.3).

Values can also be structures. A hash is a small table of fields under one key; a sorted set keeps members ordered by a score, which makes leaderboards and time windows easy:

Terminal
# Hashes and sorted sets
$ redis-cli HSET product:2 name 'Spare wheels' price 6000
(integer) 2
$ redis-cli HGET product:2 price
"6000"
$ redis-cli ZADD scores 120 sara 95 omar 140 lina
(integer) 3
$ redis-cli ZINCRBY scores 30 omar
"125"
$ redis-cli ZREVRANGE scores 0 2 WITHSCORES
1) "lina"
2) "140"
3) "omar"
4) "125"
5) "sara"
6) "120"

Key names are free text; the convention is parts separated by colons, from general to specific: product:2, session:7f3a, rate:203.0.113.7. It keeps keys readable and lets you find them by prefix when debugging.

7.2 A cache in front of a slow query

The most common pattern is cache-aside: the application looks in Redis first; on a cache miss it asks the database and keeps a copy in Redis for next time; on a cache hit the database is not touched. Here a pg_sleep(0.2) makes the query take 200 milliseconds, like a heavy report:

node/cache.ts
// the slow query: pg_sleep stands for a heavy report or a join over big tables
async function productFromDb(id: number): Promise<Product> {
  const sql = "SELECT id, name, price FROM products, pg_sleep(0.2) WHERE id = $1";
  return (await db.query(sql, [id])).rows[0];
}

// cache-aside: look in Redis first; on a miss, ask the database and keep the answer
async function product(id: number) {
  const key = `product:${id}`;
  const cached = await redis.get(key);
  if (cached !== null) return { product: JSON.parse(cached) as Product, from: "Redis" };
  const fresh = await productFromDb(id);
  // forgotten after 60 seconds, even if nobody deletes it
  await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(fresh), { EX: 60 });
  return { product: fresh, from: "PostgreSQL" };
}

// changing the data: update the database first, then delete the cached copy
async function setPrice(id: number, price: number) {
  await db.query("UPDATE products SET price = $1 WHERE id = $2", [price, id]);
  await redis.del(`product:${id}`);
}
Terminal
# A cache in front of a slow query
$ node node/cache.ts
first request    Robot kit, 45000, from PostgreSQL in 216 ms
second request   Robot kit, 45000, from Redis in 1 ms
third request    Robot kit, 45000, from Redis in 1 ms
price changed to 47000, cached copy deleted
after the change Robot kit, 47000, from PostgreSQL in 204 ms
seconds left in Redis: 60

The first request paid the full 200 milliseconds; the next two came from memory in about one. That is the reward. The cost is that there are now two copies of the data, and the copy can be wrong. Two rules keep it right:

  • Invalidate on change. Whatever changes the data deletes the cached copy, after the database write has succeeded. The next read misses and fetches the new value: the price 47000 appeared at once. Deleting is safer than writing the new value into the cache, because two concurrent writers could otherwise leave the older value there.
  • Always set an expiry. Some change will one day forget to invalidate: a direct SQL fix, a new code path, a bug. A TTL limits how long a wrong copy can live. Choose it from the question “how stale may this be?”: seconds for stock levels, minutes for a product page, hours for a list of countries.

Watch out: cache what is shared, not what is private

A cache key must contain everything the answer depends on. A product page in two languages needs product:2:ar and product:2:en, and anything that depends on the user (their basket, their prices, their permissions) needs the user in the key, or no cache at all. Serving one customer’s cached data to another is a security incident, not a performance bug.

7.3 Stampedes and locks

A cache has a dangerous moment: when a popular key expires. Every request that arrives in the next 200 milliseconds misses, and every one of them asks the database the same heavy question. This is a cache stampede. The program sends twenty requests at once for a product that is not cached, first without protection, then with a lock that lets only one request rebuild the cache while the others wait and look again:

node/stampede.ts
// delete the lock only if it is still ours, in one step on the server
const RELEASE = `if redis.call("GET", KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1]
  then return redis.call("DEL", KEYS[1]) else return 0 end`;

async function product(id: number) {
  const key = `product:${id}`;
  for (;;) {
    const cached = await redis.get(key);
    if (cached !== null) return JSON.parse(cached);
    if (mode === "naive") break;
    // only the request that gets the lock asks the database; the others wait and look again
    const token = randomUUID(); // a random token: only the owner of the lock may release it
    if (await redis.set(`lock:${key}`, token, { NX: true, PX: 5000 })) {
      try {
        const fresh = await productFromDb(id);
        await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(fresh), { EX: 60 });
        return fresh;
      } finally {
        await redis.eval(RELEASE, { keys: [`lock:${key}`], arguments: [token] });
      }
    }
    await sleep(50);
  }
  const fresh = await productFromDb(id);
  await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(fresh), { EX: 60 });
  return fresh;
}
Terminal
# Twenty requests on a cold cache
$ node node/stampede.ts naive
naive   20 requests, database queries: 20, 440 ms
$ node node/stampede.ts locked
locked  20 requests, database queries: 1, 259 ms

Without the lock, twenty requests made twenty identical queries; with it, one. With a real report that takes two seconds and a page that a thousand visitors open in that time, this is the difference between a slow page and a database that falls over. The lock is three ideas:

  • Take it with SET key token NX PX 5000. NX means only one client can create the key; the others get null and know someone else is working. PX 5000 makes the lock expire after 5 seconds, so a process that crashes while holding it cannot block everyone forever.
  • A random token as the value. If the work took longer than 5 seconds, the lock expired and another process may hold it now. Deleting the key blindly would release their lock.
  • Release with a script. “Read the value, compare it, delete the key” must happen in one step, or the lock could change hands between the read and the delete. Redis runs a Lua script atomically, like a single command, so the release checks the token and deletes in one step.

A Redis lock is good for efficiency: avoiding duplicate work, as here. It is not a guarantee of correctness, because a lock that expires while its owner is still working (a long pause, a slow network) lets two processes in. When two processes doing the work at once would corrupt data, the last line of defence stays in the database: a constraint, a conditional update or a row lock, from Chapter 6.

Quick check

  1. Why does the cache-aside code delete the cached copy after an update instead of writing the new value into it?
  2. What would go wrong if the lock had no expiry time?
  3. Why does the lock need a random token instead of the value 1?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

7.4 A rate limit that servers share

Chapter 3 limited requests with @fastify/rate-limit, which counts in the server’s own memory.

With counters in each server’s memory, a client who spreads requests across two servers gets twice the limit, and a restart resets everyone. The counter belongs in Redis, where every server sees the same number:

node/ratelimit.ts
import { createClient } from "redis";

const LIMIT = 5;
const WINDOW = 10; // seconds

// one counter per client; the first request starts a 10-second window
async function allow(redis: ReturnType<typeof createClient>, client: string) {
  const key = `rate:${client}`;
  // MULTI/EXEC: both commands run together, nothing in between
  const [count] = await redis.multi().incr(key).expire(key, WINDOW, "NX").exec();
  return { allowed: Number(count) <= LIMIT, count: Number(count), key };
}

// two servers, two connections, one Redis: the limit is shared
const servers = {
  A: await createClient({ url: "redis://localhost:6379" }).connect(),
  B: await createClient({ url: "redis://localhost:6379" }).connect(),
};
await servers.A.del("rate:203.0.113.7");
for (let i = 1; i <= 8; i++) {
  const name = i % 2 === 1 ? "A" : "B";
  const redis = servers[name];
  const { allowed, count, key } = await allow(redis, "203.0.113.7");
  const answer = allowed
    ? `allowed (${count} of ${LIMIT})`
    : `refused, 429, try again in ${await redis.ttl(key)} s`;
  console.log(`request ${i} via server ${name}: ${answer}`);
}
await servers.A.close();
await servers.B.close();
Terminal
# Five requests per 10 seconds, shared by two servers
$ node node/ratelimit.ts
request 1 via server A: allowed (1 of 5)
request 2 via server B: allowed (2 of 5)
request 3 via server A: allowed (3 of 5)
request 4 via server B: allowed (4 of 5)
request 5 via server A: allowed (5 of 5)
request 6 via server B: refused, 429, try again in 10 s
request 7 via server A: refused, 429, try again in 10 s
request 8 via server B: refused, 429, try again in 10 s

The requests alternated between servers A and B, and the sixth was refused whichever server received it. Two details make it right:

  • EXPIRE … NX sets the expiry only if the key has none, so the first request starts the 10-second window and later requests do not extend it.
  • MULTI and EXEC send both commands as one transaction: Redis runs them together, with no other client’s command in between. With two separate commands, a process that crashed after INCR and before EXPIRE would leave a counter that never expires, and that client blocked forever.

This is a fixed window: simple and cheap, but a client can send 5 requests at the end of one window and 5 more at the start of the next. A sliding window counts the requests of the last 10 seconds at every moment, with a sorted set of timestamps; it is exercise 5.

7.5 Redis in the framework

In a Fastify application, create one Redis client when the server starts, share it with app.decorate("redis", client), and close it in an onClose hook, exactly like the database pool. The ready-made pieces accept it:

  • @fastify/rate-limit takes a redis option, and then keeps its counters there instead of in memory, shared by every server.
  • Caching stays a few lines of your own code, like product() above, often wrapped in a small helper cached(key, ttl, load).
  • For locks with automatic renewal and for queues, there are packages built on these same commands; Chapter 10 uses Redis for a job queue.

7.6 What belongs in Redis

Redis keeps everything in memory, and memory is small and expensive compared with disk. Three settings decide how it behaves when things go wrong:

  • maxmemory limits the memory Redis may use, and maxmemory-policy decides what happens at the limit. For a pure cache, allkeys-lru evicts the least recently used keys, which is exactly what a cache should forget. The default, noeviction, refuses new writes instead.
  • Persistence (RDB snapshots and the AOF log) can save the data to disk, so a restart does not empty it. A cache can do without; sessions and queues need it.
  • One Redis for a cache that may forget, and another (or another configuration) for data that must not: a queue whose jobs vanish under memory pressure loses orders.

The rule that follows: Redis holds copies and short-lived state; the database holds the truth. If Redis were emptied right now, the application should get slower, log users out at worst, but lose nothing that matters.

Professional habit: measure before you cache

A cache adds a second copy of the data, invalidation code, and a new way to be wrong. Before adding one, measure: which query is slow, how often it runs, and whether an index (Chapter 4) or a better query would fix it with no copy at all. Then cache the few hot, expensive, shared answers, and watch the hit rate: a cache that is hit 20% of the time is mostly overhead.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Caching without a TTLA missed invalidation lasts foreverEvery key gets an expiry
Writing the new value into the cache on updateConcurrent writers leave the old valueDelete the key; the next read reloads
A key missing part of what the answer depends onOne user sees another’s dataLanguage, user and filters in the key
A lock without an expiryA crash blocks everyone foreverSET … NX PX
Releasing a lock with a plain DELReleasing someone else’s lockToken, and a Lua compare-and-delete
INCR then EXPIRE as two commandsCounters that never expireMULTI/EXEC, or EXPIRE … NX together
Rate limits in each server’s memoryLimits multiplied by the number of serversCounters in Redis
Redis as the only copy of important dataData lost on eviction or restartThe database holds the truth

Summary

  • Redis keeps keys in memory and answers in under a millisecond; every single command is atomic.
  • Cache-aside: read Redis, on a miss read the database and keep a copy with a TTL; on change, delete the copy.
  • A stampede sends every request to the database when a hot key expires; a lock lets one rebuild it.
  • A safe lock: SET key token NX PX, and release with a Lua script that checks the token. Redis locks save work; correctness stays in the database.
  • Rate limits belong in Redis so that servers share them; MULTI/EXEC keeps counter and expiry together.
  • Redis holds copies and short-lived state; the database holds the truth.

Quick check answers: 1. Two updates can happen at nearly the same time; if each writes its value into the cache, the slower one can overwrite the newer value with the older, and the cache stays wrong until the TTL. Deleting is the same whatever the order, and the next read loads the current value from the database. 2. If the process holding the lock crashed or hung, the key would never disappear, and every later request would wait forever for a cache that nobody rebuilds. 3. When the lock expires while its owner is still working, another process can take it; a fixed value would let the first process, when it finishes, delete the second one’s lock. The token lets each process delete only the lock it took.

Practice projects

Each project adds Redis to the shop from the earlier chapters. Measure before and after, and prove the correctness claims with concurrent requests.

Project 7.1 · Easy

A cached product list

Cache the paginated product list of the shop’s API, and keep it correct when any product changes.

You will practise: cache-aside, cache keys that include every parameter, invalidation and TTLs.

  1. Cache the response of the product list for 60 seconds, with a key that includes the page cursor, the page size and the language.
  2. When any product is created, updated or deleted, the list must change at once. Deleting every list key is awkward, so keep a version number in products:version, put it in every list key, and INCR it on every change.
  3. Measure the response time of the list with and without the cache, and the hit rate after a hundred random requests.

Hint: after the version changes, the old keys are never read again and simply expire with their TTL; nothing needs to find and delete them.

Project 7.2 · Medium

A live leaderboard

Build a “best sellers this week” board that updates on every order and answers instantly.

You will practise: sorted sets, keys per time period, atomic increments, and rebuilding from the database.

  1. On every order, ZINCRBY the product’s score by the quantity in a key for the current week, such as bestsellers:2026-W47, with an expiry a few weeks away.
  2. Add GET /products/bestsellers that returns the top 10 with ZREVRANGE … WITHSCORES, joined to the names from the database in one query.
  3. Write a command that rebuilds the current week’s board from the orders table, for the day Redis is emptied, and check that its result equals the live board.

Hint: the rebuild is what makes this safe: the board in Redis is a copy computed from orders, so losing it costs a minute of work, not data.

Project 7.3 · Challenge

A cache that never stampedes

Write a reusable cached(key, ttl, load) helper that is fast, correct under load and safe when Redis is down.

You will practise: locks with tokens, stale-while-revalidate, graceful degradation and load testing.

  1. Store each value with its own “fresh until” time inside the JSON, and a Redis TTL much longer than that.
  2. When the value is stale but present, return it at once, and let one request (the one that gets the lock) reload it in the background.
  3. When Redis cannot be reached, log a warning and call load() directly, so the application is slower but still works.
  4. Load test it with 200 concurrent requests on an expired key, and show that the database receives one query and no request waits for it.

Hint: this is the idea behind the stale-while-revalidate directive of HTTP caching.

Exercises

  1. Easy Choose a TTL for each and say why: a product’s price on its page; the exchange rate shown next to prices; the list of countries in the address form; a user’s login session.
  2. Easy What is wrong with this rate limit: count = INCR key, then if count == 1: EXPIRE key 60, as two separate commands?
  3. Medium A product page is cached under product:2. The page also shows the product’s category name. What must happen when a category is renamed? Propose two solutions and compare them.
  4. Medium A lock taken with PX 5000 protects a job that usually takes 1 second, but once took 7. Describe exactly what happened, and three ways to protect the data.
  5. Challenge Write a sliding-window rate limit with a sorted set: at most 3 requests in any 2 seconds. Show that the fourth request in a burst is refused and that a request 2 seconds later is allowed.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 8

The shop is correct and fast; now it needs to know who is calling. Authentication: passwords stored properly, sessions and tokens, and the rules that decide what each user may do.

8

Chapter 8

Authentication and Authorization

In this chapter you will learn to

  • tell authentication (who are you?) from authorization (may you do this?)
  • store passwords so that a stolen database does not reveal them
  • register and sign users in, without telling attackers which emails exist
  • issue session tokens you can revoke, and understand when a JWT is the better choice
  • protect routes by role and by ownership
  • slow down password guessing, and test all of it automatically

Until now, anyone could call every route of the shop’s API. A real shop needs to know who is calling: a customer sees their own orders and no one else’s, and only staff may see the list of customers. Getting this right is not hard, but almost every mistake in it is a security incident, and the mistakes are invisible until someone exploits them. This chapter builds it step by step with Node’s own crypto module, PostgreSQL and Fastify, and runs every request against the real server.

Bit

Years ago a site I used was breached, and the attackers published its user table. My password was right there, in plain text, next to my email. It was the same password I used on two other sites. That afternoon I changed seven passwords, and I promised myself that no table I build would ever hold one.

8.1 Two questions

Every protected request answers two separate questions, in order:

  • Authentication: who is calling? The client proves an identity, with a password once and then with a token on every request. If it cannot, the answer is 401 Unauthorized (a confusing name: it means “not authenticated”).
  • Authorization: may this user do this? The identity is known; the rules decide. If they say no, the answer is 403 Forbidden, or 404 Not Found when even the existence of the thing is private.

Keeping them apart keeps the code clear: one function finds the user, and the rules about what each user may do live next to the routes they protect.

8.2 Storing passwords

A password must never be stored as it is, and never with an ordinary hash such as SHA-256 either. A fast hash is the problem: a graphics card computes billions of SHA-256 hashes a second, so an attacker with a stolen table tries every common password against every row in minutes. Password storage needs a hash that is slow on purpose and needs a lot of memory, so that each guess costs real time: scrypt, Argon2 or bcrypt. Node has scrypt built in:

src/password.ts
import { randomBytes, scrypt, timingSafeEqual } from "node:crypto";

// N = 2^15: tens of milliseconds and 32 MB per hash, slow on purpose; raise it as computers get faster
const COST = 2 ** 15;

function derive(password: string, salt: Buffer, cost: number) {
  return new Promise<Buffer>((resolve, reject) => {
    const options = { N: cost, r: 8, p: 1, maxmem: 64 * 1024 * 1024 };
    scrypt(password.normalize("NFKC"), salt, 32, options, (error, key) =>
      error ? reject(error) : resolve(key),
    );
  });
}

export async function hashPassword(password: string) {
  // a new random salt for every hash: the same password never gives the same hash
  const salt = randomBytes(16);
  const key = await derive(password, salt, COST);
  // algorithm, cost, salt and key in one string: old hashes still verify after the cost changes
  return [
    "scrypt",
    COST,
    salt.toString("base64url"),
    key.toString("base64url"),
  ].join("$");
}

export async function verifyPassword(password: string, stored: string) {
  const [, cost, salt, key] = stored.split("$");
  const expected = Buffer.from(key ?? "", "base64url");
  const actual = await derive(
    password,
    Buffer.from(salt ?? "", "base64url"),
    Number(cost),
  );
  // compares every byte, so the time taken does not reveal where the first difference is
  return timingSafeEqual(actual, expected);
}
  • The salt is 16 random bytes, different for every hash, stored next to it. Two users with the same password get different hashes, so an attacker must attack each row separately, and precomputed tables of hashes are useless.
  • The cost is stored inside the string. When computers get faster you raise COST; old hashes still verify with the cost they were made with, and can be upgraded when the user next signs in (exercise 3).
  • timingSafeEqual compares all 32 bytes every time. An ordinary comparison stops at the first different byte, and the time it takes can leak information.
  • normalize("NFKC") makes passwords typed on different keyboards compare equal when they look the same, which matters for Arabic and other non-Latin passwords.
Terminal
# Two hashes of the same password
$ node demos/hash.ts
hash 1 scrypt$32768$h1DPlXc_E5z0hNDZOH1U2g$yZQql2677IvnTVPLIFiU7-Sq8zzhgGWk-D0kCEYPPDw
hash 2 scrypt$32768$AdpZgg4H03YAcnFf6owdEg$U52yHx90PRGHdwiV_TjQS5wk46p2ySCOWQzGQTc8xcc
right password: true
wrong password: false
one hash took 111 ms

The same password gave two different strings, both verify, and one hash took tens of milliseconds: nothing for a user signing in, a great deal for an attacker trying a billion guesses.

Watch out: never write your own crypto

This chapter uses scrypt, HMAC and random bytes from Node’s crypto module, all written and reviewed by specialists. Combining well-known pieces in the standard way is fine; inventing your own hashing scheme, your own encryption or your own random token generator is not. Math.random() is never acceptable for anything secret: it is predictable. Use randomBytes.

8.3 Registering and signing in

The users table follows Chapter 5’s migrations. Emails are compared without their capitals, so the unique index is on lower(email), not on the column:

migrations/2026_11_08_001_users.ts
  await db.schema
    .createTable("users")
    .addColumn("id", "bigint", (c) => c.primaryKey().generatedAlwaysAsIdentity())
    .addColumn("email", "text", (c) => c.notNull())
    .addColumn("name", "text", (c) => c.notNull())
    .addColumn("password_hash", "text", (c) => c.notNull())
    .addColumn("role", "text", (c) =>
      c
        .notNull()
        .defaultTo("customer")
        .check(sql`role IN ('customer', 'admin')`),
    )
    .addColumn("created_at", "timestamptz", (c) =>
      c.notNull().defaultTo(sql`now()`),
    )
    .execute();
  // one account per email, whatever its capitals
  await db.schema
    .createIndex("users_email_key")
    .on("users")
    .unique()
    .expression(sql`lower(email)`)
    .execute();

Registration validates the body, hashes the password and inserts the user. The unique index, not a check in code, decides whether the email is taken: ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING returns no row when it is, and two simultaneous registrations with the same email cannot both succeed (the lesson of Chapter 6):

src/routes/auth.ts
const Email = z.email().max(254).toLowerCase();
const Login = z.strictObject({ email: Email, password: z.string().max(128) });
// an unknown email still costs one hash, so the response time does not reveal which emails exist
const DUMMY = await hashPassword("not anyone's password");

export const authRoutes: FastifyPluginAsyncZod = async (app) => {
  app.post(
    "/auth/register",
    {
      schema: {
        body: z.strictObject({
          email: Email,
          name: z.string().trim().min(1).max(100),
          password: z.string().min(12).max(128),
        }),
        response: { 201: UserOut },
      },
    },
    async (request, reply) => {
      const { email, name, password } = request.body;
      const user = await db
        .insertInto("users")
        .values({ email, name, password_hash: await hashPassword(password) })
        .onConflict((c) => c.expression(sql`lower(email)`).doNothing())
        .returning(["id", "email", "name", "role"])
        .executeTakeFirst();
      if (!user) throw problems.emailTaken();
      return reply.code(201).send(user);
    },
  );
Terminal

  "title": "Email already registered",
  "status": 409,
  "instance": "/auth/register"
}
# A password that is too short
$ curl -s localhost:3000/auth/register -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"sam@example.com","name":"Sam","password":"short"}' | jq
{
  "title": "Invalid body",
  "status": 422,
  "instance": "/auth/register",
  "errors": [
    {
      "field": "password",
      "message": "Too small: expected string to have >=12 characters"
    }
  ]
}

Signing in is where attackers probe. Two details stop the login route from telling them which emails have accounts:

src/routes/auth.ts
  app.post(
    "/auth/login",
    {
      // 5 attempts a minute per address and email, counted before the password is checked
      config: {
        rateLimit: {
          max: 5,
          timeWindow: "1 minute",
          hook: "preHandler",
          keyGenerator: (request) =>
            `${request.ip}:${(request.body as { email?: string }).email}`,
        },
      },
      schema: {
        body: Login,
        response: { 200: z.object({ token: z.string(), expires_in: z.number() }) },
      },
    },
    async (request) => {
      const { email, password } = request.body;
      const user = await db
        .selectFrom("users")
        .select(["id", "password_hash"])
        .where(sql`lower(email)`, "=", email)
        .executeTakeFirst();
      const ok = await verifyPassword(password, user?.password_hash ?? DUMMY);
      // the same answer for a wrong email and a wrong password
      if (!user || !ok) throw problems.badLogin();
      return { token: await startSession(user.id), expires_in: 30 * 24 * 3600 };
    },
  );

  app.post("/auth/logout", { onRequest: authenticate }, async (request, reply) => {
    await endSession(request.token ?? "");
    return reply.code(204).send();
  });
  • The same answer for an unknown email and a wrong password: “email or password is wrong”, never “no such user”.
  • The same time. Without the DUMMY hash, an unknown email would answer at once and a known one after a slow hash, and the time alone would reveal which emails exist. Verifying against a dummy hash makes both cost one hash.
Terminal
# Signing in
$ curl -s localhost:3000/auth/login -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"lina@example.com","password":"a long and silly sentence"}' | jq
{
  "token": "d1Yb2eg4GpvgtBzashBAhPQuFWOR-rAoULstx3_BFmQ",
  "expires_in": 2592000
}
# A wrong password, and an unknown email: the same answer
$ curl -s localhost:3000/auth/login -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"lina@example.com","password":"a wrong sentence"}' | jq
{
  "title": "Email or password is wrong",
  "status": 401,
  "instance": "/auth/login"
}
$ curl -s localhost:3000/auth/login -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"nobody@example.com","password":"a wrong sentence"}' | jq
{
  "title": "Email or password is wrong",
  "status": 401,
  "instance": "/auth/login"
}

(Registration still reveals that an email is taken; that is usually accepted for usability. Sites that must hide it send an email in both cases instead of answering differently.)

8.4 Sessions and tokens

After a successful sign-in the server gives the client a session token: proof of identity for the next requests, so the password is sent once. Our tokens are 32 random bytes, and the database stores only their SHA-256 hash:

src/sessions.ts
const digest = (token: string) => createHash("sha256").update(token).digest("hex");

export async function startSession(userId: number) {
  // 32 random bytes: impossible to guess, and meaningless without the database
  const token = randomBytes(32).toString("base64url");
  await db
    .insertInto("sessions")
    // only the hash is stored: a leaked sessions table opens no account
    .values({
      token_hash: digest(token),
      user_id: userId,
      expires_at: sql`now() + interval '30 days'`,
    })
    .execute();
  return token;
}

export async function userForToken(token: string) {
  return db
    .selectFrom("sessions")
    .innerJoin("users", "users.id", "sessions.user_id")
    .select(["users.id", "users.email", "users.name", "users.role"])
    .where("sessions.token_hash", "=", digest(token))
    .where("sessions.expires_at", ">", sql<Date>`now()`)
    .executeTakeFirst();
}

export async function endSession(token: string) {
  await db.deleteFrom("sessions").where("token_hash", "=", digest(token)).execute();
}

A fast hash is right here, unlike for passwords: the token is 32 random bytes, not a word a person chose, so there is nothing to guess, and the hash only ensures that someone who reads the sessions table cannot use what they read. On every request, a preHandler reads the Authorization header and finds the user:

src/auth.ts
// Authentication: who is calling? (Authorization: Bearer <token>)
export async function authenticate(request: FastifyRequest) {
  const [scheme, token] = (request.headers.authorization ?? "").split(" ");
  const user = scheme === "Bearer" && token ? await userForToken(token) : undefined;
  if (!user) throw problems.unauthenticated();
  request.user = user;
  request.token = token;
}

// authorization: the user is known; may they do this?
export function requireRole(role: Role) {
  return async (request: FastifyRequest) => {
    if (request.user?.role !== role) throw problems.forbidden(role);
  };
}

export function currentUser(request: FastifyRequest): User {
  if (!request.user) throw problems.unauthenticated();
  return request.user;
}
Terminal
# Without a token
$ curl -s localhost:3000/me | jq
{
  "title": "Not signed in",
  "status": 401,
  "detail": "Send a valid token: Authorization: Bearer <token>.",
  "instance": "/me"
}
# With the token
$ TOKEN=$(curl -s localhost:3000/auth/login -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"lina@example.com","password":"a long and silly sentence"}' | jq -r .token)
$ curl -s localhost:3000/me -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" | jq
{
  "id": 4,
  "email": "lina@example.com",
  "name": "Lina",
  "role": "customer"
}

Because sessions live in the database, signing out is a DELETE, and the token stops working at once. The database keeps only hashes, of passwords and of tokens:

Terminal
# Signing out
$ curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}\n" -X POST localhost:3000/auth/logout -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN"
204
# The same token afterwards
$ curl -s localhost:3000/me -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" | jq .title
"Not signed in"
# What the database keeps
$ psql shop -c 'SELECT email, left(password_hash, 40) AS password_hash FROM users ORDER BY id'
       email       |              password_hash               
-------------------+------------------------------------------

$ psql shop -c 'SELECT user_id, left(token_hash, 16) AS token_hash, expires_at::date FROM sessions'

---------+------------------+------------

What about JWTs?

A JSON Web Token takes the opposite approach: instead of a random key into a table, the token contains the data (the user id, the role, the expiry), signed with the server’s secret so that it cannot be changed. Here is one made by hand, to see what is inside:

demos/jwt.ts
// a JWT: header and payload in base64url, then an HMAC of both with the server's secret
const header = b64({ alg: "HS256", typ: "JWT" });
const payload = b64({ sub: 2, role: "customer", exp: 1795000000 });
const token = `${header}.${payload}.${sign(`${header}.${payload}`)}`;
console.log("token:", token);

// anyone can read the payload: it is encoded, not encrypted
const read = (part: string) =>
  JSON.parse(Buffer.from(part, "base64url").toString());
console.log("payload, read without the key:", read(token.split(".")[1] ?? ""));

const valid = (jwt: string) => {
  const [h, p, signature = ""] = jwt.split(".");
  const expected = Buffer.from(sign(`${h}.${p}`));
  const given = Buffer.from(signature);
  return given.length === expected.length && timingSafeEqual(given, expected);
};
console.log("signature valid:", valid(token));
// change the payload, keep the signature
const forged = b64({ sub: 2, role: "admin", exp: 1795000000 });
console.log(
  "tampered token valid:",
  valid(`${header}.${forged}.${token.split(".")[2]}`),
);
Terminal
# Inside a JWT
$ node demos/jwt.ts
token: eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9.eyJzdWIiOjIsInJvbGUiOiJjdXN0b21lciIsImV4cCI6MTc5NTAwMDAwMH0.FIARINURub56u1m-5b1qFNKR31jo58HiVsulPiTmtkU
payload, read without the key: { sub: 2, role: 'customer', exp: 1795000000 }
signature valid: true
tampered token valid: false

Changing the role to admin broke the signature, so a JWT cannot be forged. But anyone can read it, and the server checks it without looking anything up, which is both its strength and its weakness: it cannot be revoked. A user who signs out, changes their password or loses their role keeps a valid token until it expires. JWTs fit messages between your own services and short-lived tokens (minutes); for users of your own apps, opaque tokens in a table, or in Redis from Chapter 7, are simpler and safer. Never put a secret in a JWT payload.

Note: tokens in browsers

A mobile app keeps the token in the phone’s secure storage and sends it in the Authorization header, as curl does here. A website should not keep it in localStorage, where any injected script can read it. Instead the server sets it in a cookie with HttpOnly (scripts cannot read it), Secure (HTTPS only) and SameSite=Lax (not sent with most requests from other sites), and reads the cookie instead of the header. Cookies bring a risk of their own, cross-site request forgery, which Chapter 9 deals with.

8.5 Authorization: roles and ownership

Authentication found the user; now the routes decide what that user may do. Two kinds of rule cover most applications: a role (customer or admin) for whole areas, and ownership for single records:

src/routes/orders.ts
export const orderRoutes: FastifyPluginAsyncZod = async (app) => {
  // every route in this plugin needs a signed-in user
  app.addHook("onRequest", authenticate);

  app.get("/orders", async (request) =>
    db
      .selectFrom("orders")
      .select(["id", "total"])
      .where("user_id", "=", currentUser(request).id)
      .orderBy("id")
      .execute(),
  );

  app.get(
    "/orders/:id",
    { schema: { params: z.object({ id: z.coerce.number().int() }) } },
    async (request) => {
      const user = currentUser(request);
      const order = await db
        .selectFrom("orders")
        .selectAll()
        .where("id", "=", request.params.id)
        .executeTakeFirst();
      // someone else's order is 'not found', not 'forbidden': its existence is not revealed
      if (!order || (order.user_id !== user.id && user.role !== "admin")) {
        throw problems.noOrder(request.params.id);
      }
      return order;
    },
  );

  app.get("/admin/users", { preHandler: requireRole("admin") }, async () =>
    db.selectFrom("users").select(["id", "email", "role"]).orderBy("id").execute(),
  );
};
Terminal
# Omar signs in
$ OMAR=$(curl -s localhost:3000/auth/login -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"omar@example.com","password":"omar-password-2026"}' | jq -r .token)
# His own orders
$ curl -s localhost:3000/orders -H "authorization: Bearer $OMAR" | jq -c
[{"id":3,"total":12000}]
# Sara's order
$ curl -s localhost:3000/orders/1 -H "authorization: Bearer $OMAR" | jq
{
  "title": "Not found",
  "status": 404,
  "detail": "There is no order 1.",
  "instance": "/orders/1"
}
# The admin list, as Omar
$ curl -s localhost:3000/admin/users -H "authorization: Bearer $OMAR" | jq
{
  "title": "Not allowed",
  "status": 403,
  "detail": "This needs the admin role.",
  "instance": "/admin/users"
}
# The admin list, as an admin
$ ADMIN=$(curl -s localhost:3000/auth/login -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"admin@example.com","password":"admin-password-2026"}' | jq -r .token)
$ curl -s localhost:3000/admin/users -H "authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" | jq -c ".[]"
{"id":1,"email":"sara@example.com","role":"customer"}
{"id":2,"email":"omar@example.com","role":"customer"}
{"id":3,"email":"admin@example.com","role":"admin"}
{"id":4,"email":"lina@example.com","role":"customer"}
  • addHook("onRequest", authenticate) protects every route of the plugin, including routes added to it later. A rule that each route must remember will one day be forgotten.
  • The list of orders is filtered in the query: WHERE user_id = …. Fetching everything and filtering in code risks returning too much the day someone changes it.
  • Omar asking for Sara’s order gets 404, exactly as for an order that does not exist. A 403 would confirm that order 1 exists, and ids that count up would let him learn how many orders the shop has.
  • The admin list returns 403: Omar knows the route exists, and he is told he lacks the role.

Quick check

  1. Why is SHA-256 fine for session tokens but not for passwords?
  2. A signed-in customer asks for /admin/users. Which status, and why not 401?
  3. Why does the login route verify a dummy hash when the email does not exist?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

8.6 Slowing down guessing

A slow hash protects a stolen table; the running server needs protection too, against a script that tries passwords one after another. The login route has its own rate limit (from Chapter 3’s plugin), keyed on the client’s address and the email, so one attacker cannot try thousands of passwords on one account:

Terminal
# Six wrong passwords in a row
$ for i in 1 2 3 4 5 6; do curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code} ' localhost:3000/auth/login -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"sara@example.com","password":"guess number one"}'; done; echo
401 401 401 401 401 429 
$ curl -s localhost:3000/auth/login -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"sara@example.com","password":"guess number seven"}' | jq
{
  "title": "Too many attempts",
  "status": 429,
  "detail": "Try again in 60 seconds.",
  "instance": "/auth/login"
}

The sixth attempt was refused before its password was even checked. In production the counters belong in Redis, shared by every server. Locking the account after a few failures is tempting but lets anyone lock out any user by typing wrong passwords for them; rate limits slow the attacker without punishing the owner. Adding a second factor (a code from an app) is the strongest protection of all.

8.7 Testing it

Security rules are exactly the kind of code that breaks silently, so each rule gets a test. Fastify’s app.inject sends requests to the application without a network, so the tests run fast:

src/auth.test.ts
async function signIn(email: string, password: string) {
  const res = await app.inject({
    method: "POST",
    url: "/auth/login",
    payload: { email, password },
  });
  return res.json().token as string;
}
const as = (token: string) => ({ authorization: `Bearer ${token}` });

test("registers, signs in and reads /me", async () => {
  const email = `test-${Date.now()}@example.com`;
  const password = "a long test password";
  const created = await app.inject({
    method: "POST",
    url: "/auth/register",
    payload: { email, name: "Test", password },
  });
  assert.equal(created.statusCode, 201);
  const me = await app.inject({
    url: "/me",
    headers: as(await signIn(email, password)),
  });
  assert.equal(me.json().email, email);
});

test("refuses /me without a token", async () => {
  assert.equal((await app.inject({ url: "/me" })).statusCode, 401);
});
Terminal
# The tests
$ npm test
✔ registers, signs in and reads /me (…ms)
✔ refuses /me without a token (…ms)
✔ refuses the admin list to a customer (…ms)
✔ hides other customers' orders (…ms)
✔ a token stops working after sign-out (…ms)
ℹ tests 5
ℹ suites 0
ℹ pass 5
ℹ fail 0
ℹ cancelled 0
ℹ skipped 0
ℹ todo 0

Note what is tested: not only that the right user gets in, but that the wrong ones are kept out. A test suite that checks only the happy path would pass for an API with no security at all.

Professional habit: test the “no” for every route

For every protected route, write at least two tests: one without a token (401) and one as a user who must not have access (403 or 404). Add them in the same pull request as the route. The day someone removes a hook or changes a query, these tests fail, which is far cheaper than finding out from a customer who saw someone else’s order.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Passwords in plain text, or with SHA-256 or MD5A stolen table reveals most passwordsscrypt, Argon2 or bcrypt, with a salt
“No such user” on loginAttackers learn which emails have accountsOne message, and a dummy hash for the same time
Tokens from Math.random() or a counterTokens can be guessedrandomBytes(32)
Tokens stored as they areA leaked table opens every accountStore their hash
Long-lived JWTs for sign-inNo way to sign a user outRevocable tokens, or JWTs of minutes
Checking the user in some routes onlyThe forgotten route is openA hook on the whole plugin
Loading a record, then checking the owner in codeEasy to forget; 403 reveals the record existsFilter by owner in the query; 404
No limit on login attemptsPassword guessing at full speedA rate limit per address and account

Summary

  • Authentication finds out who is calling (401 if unknown); authorization decides what they may do (403, or 404 to hide existence).
  • Passwords are stored with a slow, salted hash (scrypt here), compared in constant time.
  • Sign-in gives the same answer and the same time for a wrong email and a wrong password.
  • Session tokens are random bytes, stored only as a hash, and revoked by deleting the row.
  • A JWT carries signed data that anyone can read and nobody can revoke: good between services and for minutes, not for user sessions.
  • Protect whole plugins with a hook, filter records by owner in the query, rate-limit login, and test every “no”.

Quick check answers: 1. A session token is 32 random bytes: there are far too many possibilities to try, so the hash only needs to stop someone who reads the table from using the values directly, and a fast hash does that. A password is chosen by a person from a small set of likely words, so an attacker can try the likely ones quickly unless each try is made deliberately slow. 2. 403 Forbidden: the server knows exactly who is calling (the token is valid), so the problem is not authentication; the user lacks the admin role. 3. So that the response takes the same time whether or not the email exists; otherwise an unknown email would be answered much faster, and the time would reveal which emails have accounts.

Practice projects

Each project extends this chapter’s API. Every new rule comes with tests for the users who must be refused.

Project 8.1 · Easy

Change your password

Let a signed-in user change their password safely.

You will practise: verifying the current password, hashing, and revoking sessions.

  1. Add POST /me/password with current_password and new_password (at least 12 characters).
  2. Refuse with 401 if the current password is wrong, and with 422 if the new one equals the old one.
  3. After the change, delete every other session of the user, keeping the one making the request.
  4. Test the three cases, and that a second device’s token stops working.

Hint: asking for the current password stops someone who finds an unlocked, signed-in laptop from taking over the account.

Project 8.2 · Medium

Staff with permissions

Replace the two fixed roles with permissions that can be combined.

You will practise: modelling permissions, migrations, and a reusable authorization hook.

  1. Add tables for roles, permissions and the links between them and users, in a migration.
  2. Write requirePermission("orders.refund"), loading the user’s permissions once per request.
  3. Create a “support” role that may read every order and refund, but not see the user list.
  4. Test each route with a user who has the permission and one who does not.

Hint: check permissions, not role names, in the routes. Roles are only a convenient way to give a set of permissions to many users at once.

Project 8.3 · Challenge

Forgotten password

Build a password reset that cannot be used to take over someone else’s account.

You will practise: single-use tokens, expiry, not leaking account existence, and side effects through an outbox.

  1. POST /auth/forgot takes an email and always answers 202, whether or not the account exists.
  2. For an existing account, store the hash of a random token with a 30-minute expiry, and put the reset link in the outbox from Chapter 6 instead of sending it inside the request.
  3. POST /auth/reset takes the token and a new password, works once, and deletes all the user’s sessions.
  4. Test an expired token, a reused token, and that the answer for an unknown email is identical.

Hint: the reset token is a password for 30 minutes. Treat it exactly like a session token: random, hashed in the database, and deleted when used.

Exercises

  1. Easy A colleague proposes storing sha256(salt + password) with a random salt per user. What does the salt protect against, and what does it not?
  2. Easy Choose the status for each: no Authorization header; an expired token; a customer deleting a product; a customer reading another customer’s address.
  3. Medium You raise COST from 2 ** 15 to 2 ** 16. How do the existing users’ hashes get the new cost, without asking anyone to reset their password?
  4. Medium Add “sign out on every device”, and show that a token from a second device stops working.
  5. Challenge Design (without code) an access token of 10 minutes as a JWT plus a refresh token in the database. What does each request check, what happens at sign-out, and what is gained compared with this chapter’s sessions?

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 9

Users can sign in; now the API must survive people who try to break it. Security: injection, cross-site attacks, secrets, headers, dependencies, and the OWASP list of what goes wrong most often.

9

Chapter 9

Security: Attacks and Defences

In this chapter you will learn to

  • think like an attacker, using the OWASP Top 10 as a map
  • see SQL injection steal password hashes, and make it impossible with parameters
  • stop clients from writing fields they must not touch
  • keep user input out of file paths and other dangerous places
  • protect browser users with security headers, CORS and safe cookies
  • handle limits, secrets, configuration and dependencies like a professional

Every API on the internet is probed by scripts within hours of going online. Most of what they try is old and well known, and most successful attacks still use the same few mistakes: input treated as code, missing checks, secrets in the wrong place. This chapter commits those mistakes on purpose. Each attack gets two routes side by side, the obvious version under /unsafe and the safe one, and every attack is run for real with curl. Seeing your own server hand over its password hashes is the fastest way to never write that code again.

Bit

The first time I ran a security scanner against an API I had built, it found a search box that accepted SQL. I had used string templates because they were “just for a search”. Within a minute, the scanner had listed every table in my database. Nothing was stolen, because it was a test server. It was the most useful minute of my career.

9.1 Thinking like an attacker

An attacker reads your API differently from a user: every input is a chance to make the server do something it was not meant to do. The OWASP Top 10, a list kept by the Open Worldwide Application Security Project from data on real breaches, names the most common ways this succeeds. Its 2025 edition, and where this book deals with each:

RiskIn shortWhere
Broken access controlUsers reach data or actions that are not theirsChapter 8, and 9.3
Security misconfigurationDefaults, debug output, missing headers9.5 and 9.7
Software supply chain failuresVulnerable or malicious dependencies9.8
Cryptographic failuresWeak hashing, no HTTPS, secrets in clearChapter 8, and 9.7
InjectionInput executed as SQL, commands or HTML9.2 and 9.5
Insecure designThe flaw is in the plan, not the codeEvery chapter’s “professional habit”
Authentication failuresGuessable passwords, unlimited attemptsChapter 8
Software or data integrity failuresTrusting updates or data that were not checked9.8
