Hint: most of a worker’s behaviour can be tested as ordinary functions; keep the tests that need Redis few and focused.

Exercises

  1. Easy Run the pricing tests with BUG=1. Which tests fail, which pass, and what does that tell you about where the bug is?
  2. Easy The coverage report shows 90.91% of branches in pricing.ts. Which branch never runs, and should a test cover it?
  3. Medium A colleague’s test fails about once in twenty runs with “expected 2 orders, got 3”. List three likely causes and how to find which it is.
  4. Medium The order route sends the email after the transaction. Write the test that proves a refused order sends no email, and explain why it would not catch an email sent inside a transaction that later rolls back.
  5. Challenge Design the tests for “an order is cancelled automatically if it is not paid within 15 minutes”: which levels, which doubles, and how to control time in each.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 13

Tests prove the code works before it ships; production needs proof that it keeps working. Observability: structured logs, metrics, traces and alerts that tell you what is wrong before the customers do.

13

Chapter 13

Observability: Logs, Metrics and Traces

In this chapter you will learn to

  • write structured logs that machines can search and people can read
  • follow one request across services with a trace id
  • count requests, errors and durations as metrics, and read them like Prometheus
  • answer health and readiness checks so platforms know what to do with each server
  • turn signals into alerts that wake people only when users are affected

Tests prove the code worked on the day it shipped. In production, things go wrong that no test imagined: a partner’s API slows down, a disk fills, one query becomes slow when a table grows. Observability is the ability to answer “what is happening, and why?” from the outside, without adding code and redeploying first. It rests on three kinds of signal: logs (what happened, event by event), metrics (how much and how fast, as numbers over time) and traces (where the time of one request went, across services). This chapter adds all three to a small shop service, written with Fastify.

Bit

The worst night of my first job began with a customer email: “checkout has been broken for three hours”. Our logs were lines like Error occurred, with no order, no user and no time zone. We spent two hours finding which server, and one minute fixing the bug. Since then I log as if I will be reading it at three in the morning, tired, in a hurry. Because I will.

13.1 Structured logs

A log line like Order failed for sara is easy to write and hard to use: you cannot count them, filter them by order or join them to other lines. A structured log writes each event as one JSON object per line, with named fields. Tools such as Loki, Elasticsearch or a cloud provider’s log service can then answer questions like “all errors for this order in the last hour”, and people can still read it.

Fastify logs with pino, one of the fastest JSON loggers for Node, built in. A few options make its lines shorter and safer:

node/server.ts
const app = Fastify({
  // one JSON object per line: machines can search it, people can still read it
  logger: {
    base: { service: `shop:${port}` },
    timestamp: () => `,"time":"${new Date().toISOString().slice(11, 23)}"`,
    formatters: {
      level: (label) => ({ level: label }),
      log: (line) => ({ ...line, responseTime: round(line.responseTime) }),
    },
    // only what is useful: the method and the address, not every header
    serializers: {
      req: (req) => ({ method: req.method, url: req.url }),
      res: (res) => ({ status: res.statusCode }),
      err: (err) => ({ type: err.name, message: err.message, at: where(err) }),
    },
  },
  // the trace id comes from the caller's traceparent header, or starts here
  genReqId: (req) => String(req.headers.traceparent ?? "").split("-")[1] ?? hex(16),
});

Three requests, one of which brings its own trace id from a caller:

Terminal
# A few requests, one with its own trace id
$ curl -s localhost:3000/products/1 | jq -c
{"id":1,"name":"Robot kit","price":45000}
$ curl -s localhost:3000/products/9 | jq -c
{"title":"Not found"}
$ curl -s localhost:3000/products/1 -H 'traceparent: 00-4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736-00f067aa0ba902b7-01' | jq -c
{"id":1,"name":"Robot kit","price":45000}
Server terminal
$ node node/server.ts
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:30.657","service":"shop:3000","msg":"Server listening at http://127.0.0.1:3000"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:31.639","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"837a507a862af4306439fb442f43368b","req":{"method":"GET","url":"/products/1"},"msg":"incoming request"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:31.652","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"837a507a862af4306439fb442f43368b","res":{"status":200},"responseTime":11.87,"msg":"request completed"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:31.665","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"0e22cce747ffb29093705d5bf617c7dd","req":{"method":"GET","url":"/products/9"},"msg":"incoming request"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:31.667","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"0e22cce747ffb29093705d5bf617c7dd","res":{"status":404},"responseTime":1.68,"msg":"request completed"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:31.677","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736","req":{"method":"GET","url":"/products/1"},"msg":"incoming request"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:31.679","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736","res":{"status":200},"responseTime":1.73,"msg":"request completed"}

Every line carries the same fields: the time, the level (debug, info, warn, error), the service that wrote it, and the request’s trace id. The start and the end of each request are logged with its method, address, status and response time. Good habits for what goes into logs:

  • Log events, with context. “order placed” with the order id and total, not “done”. Every line of a request carries its id, so one search shows the whole story.
  • Choose levels honestly. error for something that needs a person; warn for something odd that the code handled; info for normal events. Production usually keeps info and above.
  • Never log secrets or personal data. No passwords, tokens, card numbers or whole request bodies. The serializers above log the method and address only, not the headers with their Authorization.
  • Write to standard output, and let the platform collect, store and search. The application should not manage log files.

13.2 Errors

When something fails, two audiences need different things. The client needs a short answer that reveals nothing (Chapter 9); the team needs every detail. The trace id connects them:

node/server.ts
app.setErrorHandler((err, request, reply) => {
  request.log.error({ err }, err.message);
  return reply.code(500).send({ title: "Server error", trace: request.id });
});
Terminal
# A request that fails
$ curl -s localhost:3000/boom | jq -c
{"title":"Server error","trace":"84335d96a3e9f93b6f45d0cf859c7bde"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:31.890","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"84335d96a3e9f93b6f45d0cf859c7bde","req":{"method":"GET","url":"/boom"},"msg":"incoming request"}
{"level":"error","time":"14:15:31.892","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"84335d96a3e9f93b6f45d0cf859c7bde","err":{"type":"Error","message":"payment provider timed out","at":"server.ts:84"},"msg":"payment provider timed out"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:31.893","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"84335d96a3e9f93b6f45d0cf859c7bde","res":{"status":500},"responseTime":2.44,"msg":"request completed"}

The customer sees “Server error” and a trace id they can quote to support; the log has the message, the type and the line, under the same id. With an error tracker (Sentry, or the free GlitchTip), errors are also grouped, counted and sent to the team with the full stack, which is better than reading logs for them.

13.3 Traces across services

Once a request crosses from one service to another, a request id is no longer enough: each service would make up its own. The W3C Trace Context standard defines a header, traceparent, that carries a trace id from service to service: 00-, 32 hex characters of trace id, 16 of the caller’s span id, and flags. Each service takes the trace id from the incoming header (or starts a new trace), logs with it, and passes it on:

node/server.ts
app.get("/checkout", async (request) => {
  request.log.info("checkout started");
  // the next service gets the same trace id, so its logs join this request's
  const res = await fetch(`http://localhost:${process.env.STOCK_PORT}/stock/1`, {
    headers: { traceparent: `00-${request.id}-${hex(8)}-01` },
  });
  const { stock } = (await res.json()) as { stock: number };
  request.log.info({ stock }, "stock checked");
  return { ok: stock > 0 };
});
Terminal
# One checkout, two services, one trace id
$ curl -s localhost:3000/checkout | jq -c
{"ok":true}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:32.104","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"db944d137cf47cda9d75aeab67e0a62a","req":{"method":"GET","url":"/checkout"},"msg":"incoming request"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:32.104","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"db944d137cf47cda9d75aeab67e0a62a","msg":"checkout started"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:32.125","service":"shop:3001","reqId":"db944d137cf47cda9d75aeab67e0a62a","req":{"method":"GET","url":"/stock/1"},"msg":"incoming request"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:32.137","service":"shop:3001","reqId":"db944d137cf47cda9d75aeab67e0a62a","res":{"status":200},"responseTime":11.15,"msg":"request completed"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:32.142","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"db944d137cf47cda9d75aeab67e0a62a","stock":3,"msg":"stock checked"}
{"level":"info","time":"14:15:32.143","service":"shop:3000","reqId":"db944d137cf47cda9d75aeab67e0a62a","res":{"status":200},"responseTime":38.83,"msg":"request completed"}

The two services wrote to different terminals; merged by time, their lines form one story under one trace id: the checkout started, called the stock service, which answered in a few milliseconds, and the checkout finished. This is the core idea of distributed tracing. Full tracing systems go further: each step becomes a span with a start, a duration and a parent, sent to a tracing backend (Jaeger, Tempo, or a cloud service) that draws the whole request as a timeline. OpenTelemetry, the open standard for this, provides libraries for every language that create spans for HTTP calls, database queries and queue jobs automatically, using the same traceparent header.

Quick check

  1. Why is a JSON log line more useful than Order 1001 failed for sara@example.com?
  2. The client of the failing request received a trace id. What is it for?
  3. Why does the checkout send traceparent to the stock service instead of letting it create its own id?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

13.4 Metrics

Logs tell you about individual events; metrics are numbers about all of them, cheap to store for months and fast to chart: requests per second, errors per minute, how slow the slowest requests are. For a web service, three numbers per route cover most needs, often called RED: the Rate of requests, the Errors among them, and their Duration. Prometheus, the most common open-source metrics system, collects them by reading a /metrics page from each server every few seconds.

The prom-client library keeps the numbers in the process’s memory and writes the page:

node/server.ts
const requests = new client.Counter({
  name: "http_requests_total",
  help: "Requests by route and status",
  labelNames: ["method", "route", "status"],
});
const duration = new client.Histogram({
  name: "http_request_duration_seconds",
  help: "How long requests took",
  labelNames: ["route"],
  buckets: [0.01, 0.05, 0.1, 0.5, 1],
});
app.addHook("onResponse", async (request, reply) => {
  // the route's pattern, not the address: /products/1 and /products/2 are one series
  const route = request.routeOptions.url ?? "unknown";
  if (["/health", "/ready", "/metrics"].includes(route)) return;
  requests.inc({ method: request.method, route, status: reply.statusCode });
  duration.observe({ route }, reply.elapsedTime / 1000);
});
Terminal
# The metrics, as Prometheus reads them
$ curl -s localhost:3000/metrics | grep -E '^http_requests_total|duration_seconds_(bucket|sum).*products'
http_requests_total{method="GET",route="/products/:id",status="200"} 2
http_requests_total{method="GET",route="/products/:id",status="404"} 1
http_requests_total{method="GET",route="/boom",status="500"} 1
http_requests_total{method="GET",route="/checkout",status="200"} 1
http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{le="0.01",route="/products/:id"} 2
http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{le="0.05",route="/products/:id"} 3
http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{le="0.1",route="/products/:id"} 3
http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{le="0.5",route="/products/:id"} 3
http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{le="1",route="/products/:id"} 3
http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{le="+Inf",route="/products/:id"} 3
http_request_duration_seconds_sum{route="/products/:id"} 0.015281654000000117
  • A counter only goes up: http_requests_total by method, route and status. Prometheus computes rates from it (“errors per minute”) by comparing readings over time.
  • A histogram counts durations into buckets: how many requests took at most 10 ms, 50 ms, and so on. From it, Prometheus estimates percentiles: the 95th percentile (p95) is the time under which 95% of requests finish. Percentiles matter more than averages, because an average hides the customers who waited ten seconds.
  • Labels use the route pattern, never the real address or a user id: /products/:id is one series, while one series per product would multiply without end (high cardinality) and overload the metrics system.

13.5 Health and readiness

Load balancers and container platforms keep asking each server whether it is well, and act on the answer. Two different questions deserve two endpoints:

node/server.ts
app.get("/health", { logLevel: "silent" }, async () => ({ status: "ok" }));
// ready: it can do its job, so send it traffic (stop sending if not)
app.get("/ready", { logLevel: "silent" }, async (request, reply) => {
  try {
    await db.query({ text: "SELECT 1", query_timeout: 1000 });
    return { status: "ready" };
  } catch {
    return reply.code(503).send({ status: "database unavailable" });
  }
});
Terminal
# Health and readiness, with the database up
$ curl -s -w ' %{http_code}\n' localhost:3000/health
{"status":"ok"} 200
$ curl -s -w ' %{http_code}\n' localhost:3000/ready
{"status":"ready"} 200
# The database stops
$ docker stop b3-pg
$ curl -s -w ' %{http_code}\n' localhost:3000/health
{"status":"ok"} 200
$ curl -s -w ' %{http_code}\n' localhost:3000/ready
{"status":"database unavailable"} 503
  • Liveness (/health): is the process alive and answering? If not, the platform restarts it. It must not check the database: when the database is down, restarting every web server makes things worse, not better.
  • Readiness (/ready): can this server do useful work right now? If not, the load balancer stops sending it traffic until it can. Here it checks the database with a short timeout, and answered 503 as soon as the database stopped, while liveness stayed 200.

Note the line that keeps the server alive when the database goes away. A pool of connections emits an error event when an idle connection is cut; without a listener, Node treats it as an unhandled error and the whole process crashes, which is exactly what happened in the first version of this example:

node/server.ts
db.on("error", (err) => app.log.warn({ err }, "database connection lost"));

13.6 From signals to alerts

Dashboards are for looking; alerts are for being told. The art is alerting on what users feel, not on every cause:

  • Service level objectives. Decide what “working” means, in numbers: “99.5% of checkout requests succeed, and 95% finish within 500 ms, over 30 days”. That is an SLO; the 0.5% of allowed failures is the error budget. Alert when the budget is burning fast, not at every error.
  • Symptoms over causes. “Checkout errors above 2% for 5 minutes” wakes someone; “CPU at 80%” goes to a dashboard. High CPU with happy users is not an emergency; a broken checkout at 20% CPU is.
  • Every alert is actionable. It says what is wrong, links to the dashboard and to a short guide (a runbook) for what to check first. An alert that people learn to ignore is worse than none.
  • Watch the background too. Queue length and failed jobs (Chapter 10), disk space, certificate expiry, and the time since the last successful backup.

13.7 Going further in Node

  • Logs. pino writes to standard output; in development, pipe it through pino-pretty for colours. Use request.log in routes so that every line carries the request id, and pino’s redact option to remove fields such as password wherever they appear.
  • Errors. Sentry and GlitchTip have Node SDKs that hook into Fastify.
  • Tracing. OpenTelemetry’s Node SDK, with its automatic instrumentation, creates spans for incoming requests, fetch calls, PostgreSQL queries and Redis commands without changing the routes, and propagates traceparent for you.
  • Metrics. prom-client’s collectDefaultMetrics() adds the process’s memory, CPU and event loop delay, which is the first sign of blocking code (Chapter 14).

Professional habit: debug production from the signals first

When something goes wrong, resist adding console.log and redeploying. Start from the alert, open the dashboard, find the trace id of a failing request, and read its logs. Each time the signals were not enough to answer the question, add the missing field, metric or span, so that next time they are. Observability is built one incident at a time.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Free-text logsNothing can be searched, counted or joinedOne JSON object per line, with named fields
No request or trace idThe lines of one request cannot be found togetherAn id on every line, passed in traceparent
Secrets and personal data in logsLogs become a data leakLog ids, not bodies; redact sensitive fields
Stack traces sent to clientsAttackers learn the codeA title and a trace id outside; details in the log
Averages instead of percentilesSlow requests hidden behind fast onesHistograms and p95/p99
User ids or full URLs as metric labelsMillions of series; the metrics system falls overRoute patterns and small fixed sets
Liveness that checks the databaseEvery server restarted when the database blipsLiveness for the process, readiness for dependencies
Alerts on causes, for everythingAlert fatigue; real alerts ignoredAlerts on symptoms and SLOs, with runbooks

Summary

  • Logs say what happened, metrics how much and how fast, traces where one request’s time went.
  • Structured logs are one JSON object per line, with a level, the service and a trace id, and no secrets.
  • Errors give the client a title and the trace id; the details go to the log or an error tracker.
  • The traceparent header carries the trace id between services; OpenTelemetry turns it into full traces.
  • RED metrics per route: rate, errors and duration as a histogram, read by Prometheus; labels stay low-cardinality.
  • Liveness and readiness are different checks; alerts follow SLOs and symptoms, each with a runbook.

Quick check answers: 1. Its fields can be searched and counted by tools (“all errors for order 1001”, “errors per minute by route”), it carries the request’s trace id so it can be joined with the other lines of the same request, and it can leave out personal data such as the email, which the free-text line puts in every copy of the logs. 2. It is the key to the logs: when the customer or the support team quotes it, the team can find every line about that request, across services, without guessing from times and addresses, while the client learns nothing about the code. 3. So that all the lines written about one checkout, in both services, share one id and can be shown together; with separate ids nobody could tell which stock request belonged to which checkout.

Practice projects

Each project makes the shop easier to run. Prove each one by breaking something on purpose and finding it with the signals.

Project 13.1 · Easy

Logs that answer questions

Make the shop’s API from the earlier chapters log like this chapter’s service.

You will practise: structured logging, trace ids, redaction, and searching logs with jq.

  1. Log in JSON with a trace id taken from traceparent or created, returned to clients in a response header.
  2. Log one info line for each business event (order placed, order cancelled) with the order id and total, and make sure passwords and tokens never appear.
  3. Generate a hundred requests, then answer with jq alone: how many orders were placed, which routes returned errors, and the full story of one trace id.

Hint: jq -c 'select(.level == "error")' and jq -s 'group_by(.msg) | map({msg: .[0].msg, count: length})' answer most questions about a log file.

Project 13.2 · Medium

A dashboard with Prometheus and Grafana

Collect the shop’s metrics with a real Prometheus, and chart them in Grafana.

You will practise: running Prometheus, PromQL, percentiles from histograms, and dashboards.

  1. Run Prometheus and Grafana in Docker, with Prometheus reading the service’s /metrics every 5 seconds.
  2. Chart requests per second by route: sum by (route) (rate(http_requests_total[1m])).
  3. Chart the error ratio and the p95 duration with histogram_quantile(0.95, …).
  4. Add a slow route, send traffic to it, and watch the p95 move while the average barely does.

Hint: rates are always computed over a window ([1m]): a counter by itself only grows, and its slope is what matters.

Project 13.3 · Challenge

Full tracing with OpenTelemetry

See one checkout as a timeline of spans across two services, the database and Redis.

You will practise: OpenTelemetry SDKs, automatic instrumentation, context propagation, and reading traces.

  1. Run Jaeger in Docker, and add the OpenTelemetry SDK with automatic instrumentation to both services.
  2. Call the checkout and find its trace in Jaeger: the incoming request, the call to the stock service, and each SQL query.
  3. Add one manual span around a business step (“price the basket”) with the order id as an attribute.
  4. Make the stock service slow for one product, and use the traces to prove where the time goes.

Hint: put the trace id in every log line as well; then you can jump from a trace to its logs and back, which is where the two signals become more than the sum of their parts.

Exercises

  1. Easy Rewrite these as structured log events, with a level and fields: “Starting…”; “User sara@example.com logged in with password hunter2”; “ERROR!!! order failed”.
  2. Easy A histogram says that of 1,000 requests, 940 took at most 100 ms, 990 at most 500 ms, and all at most 1 s. Roughly what are the p95 and p99?
  3. Medium Someone adds a label customer_id to http_requests_total. What happens to the metrics system after a month, and how should the question they wanted to answer be answered instead?
  4. Medium The readiness check queries the database with a one-second timeout. What could go wrong if it had no timeout, and what if it also checked a slow partner API?
  5. Challenge Write the SLO and two alerts for the shop’s checkout: one that pages someone at night and one that only opens a ticket. Explain the difference.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 14

The signals show where the shop is slow; now we make it fast. Performance: measuring under load, finding the bottleneck, indexes and query plans, and the event loop.

14

Chapter 14

Performance

In this chapter you will learn to

  • measure performance under load instead of guessing
  • read percentiles and throughput, and know which matters when
  • find the bottleneck, which is usually the database, and fix it with the right index
  • understand the concurrency model of Node’s event loop, and its limits
  • choose between faster code, caching, background work and more servers

“Make it fast” is not a task until it has a number: how many requests a second, how slow the slowest ones may be, measured how. This chapter follows the order that works in practice. First measure, under realistic load. Then find the one thing that limits the system, the bottleneck. Fix that, and measure again. Almost every time, the bottleneck is not where intuition points, and the fix is smaller than feared. Here a single line of SQL makes the shop’s product query almost a hundred times faster.

Bit

I once spent a week rewriting a JSON serialiser to make an API faster. It became 30% quicker at serialising, and the API became 1% faster, because 95% of each request was spent waiting for one query without an index. The index took a minute to add. I have measured before optimising ever since.

14.1 Measuring

Performance has two numbers that matter, and they are different:

  • Latency: how long one request takes, as seen by the client. Report it as percentiles (Chapter 13): the median (p50) is a typical request, the p99 is the slowest one in a hundred, which on a busy site is someone every second.
  • Throughput: how many requests per second the system completes. It matters for capacity: how many users one server can carry.

A load test measures both by sending many requests at once. autocannon, a load-testing tool written in Node, opens a number of connections and keeps them busy for a few seconds. The shop has 500,000 products in 2,000 categories:

schema.sql
-- 500,000 products in 2,000 categories of 250 each
CREATE TABLE products (
  id       bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  name     text NOT NULL,
  category text NOT NULL,
  price    integer NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO products (name, category, price)
SELECT 'Product ' || n,
       (ARRAY['kits', 'wheels', 'cables', 'sensors', 'motors', 'tools', 'lights', 'cases'])[1 + n % 8]
         || '-' || (n % 250),
       (n * 37) % 100000 + 100
FROM generate_series(1, 500000) AS n;
ANALYZE products;

The product listing asks for the 20 cheapest products in one category. Twenty clients, five seconds:

Terminal
# 20 clients for 5 seconds, without an index
$ npx autocannon -c 20 -d 5 'localhost:3000/products?category=cables-42'
{"requests_per_s":93.6,"p50_ms":205,"p99_ms":320,"errors":0}

About 90 requests a second, and the typical client waits 200 milliseconds. For a listing, that is slow. The obvious suspects are Node, Fastify, JSON. The right move is to look.

Watch out: measure like production

A load test on a laptop with ten rows tells you nothing. Use production-like data volumes (the bottleneck here appears only because the table is big), the same database, a server configured as in production (no debug logging), and a load generator on a different machine when the numbers matter. Warm up first, run several times, and change one thing at a time.

14.2 Finding the bottleneck

The observability of Chapter 13 usually points straight at it: the histogram of the listing route is slow, and its trace shows nearly all the time inside one SQL query. PostgreSQL can explain how it runs a query, and with ANALYZE it runs the query and reports the real times:

query.sql
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, COSTS OFF)
SELECT id, name, price FROM products
WHERE category = 'cables-42'
ORDER BY price
LIMIT 20;
Terminal
# The query plan, without an index
$ psql shop -f query.sql
                                           QUERY PLAN                                            
-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Limit (actual time=20.416..28.366 rows=20.00 loops=1)
   Buffers: shared hit=4170
   ->  Sort (actual time=20.413..28.360 rows=20.00 loops=1)
         Sort Key: price
         Sort Method: top-N heapsort  Memory: 27kB
         Buffers: shared hit=4170
         ->  Gather (actual time=0.333..28.204 rows=500.00 loops=1)
               Workers Planned: 2
               Workers Launched: 2
               Buffers: shared hit=4167
               ->  Parallel Seq Scan on products (actual time=0.036..17.474 rows=166.67 loops=3)
                     Filter: (category = 'cables-42'::text)
                     Rows Removed by Filter: 166500
                     Buffers: shared hit=4167
 Planning:
   Buffers: shared hit=86 read=4
 Planning Time: 0.412 ms
 Execution Time: 28.420 ms
(18 rows)

Read a plan from the inside out. The innermost step is a Parallel Seq Scan: three processes read the whole table, checking every row against the category, and “Rows Removed by Filter” says that 166,500 rows per process were read and thrown away to find 250. Then they sort and merge the matches, and keep 20. About 20 milliseconds per query, and with twenty clients the database is busy reading the same table over and over: that is the bottleneck. (Chapter 4 introduced indexes; here is what the lack of one costs.)

14.3 The right index

An index on category alone would find the 250 rows quickly, but they would still need sorting. An index on (category, price) keeps the rows of each category already sorted by price, so the database can read the first 20 entries of one category and stop:

Terminal
# An index that matches the query
$ psql shop -c 'CREATE INDEX products_category_price ON products (category, price)'
CREATE INDEX
# The same query plan, with the index
$ psql shop -f query.sql
                                                QUERY PLAN                                                
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 Limit (actual time=0.066..0.185 rows=20.00 loops=1)
   Buffers: shared hit=20 read=3
   ->  Index Scan using products_category_price on products (actual time=0.065..0.182 rows=20.00 loops=1)
         Index Cond: (category = 'cables-42'::text)
         Index Searches: 1
         Buffers: shared hit=20 read=3
 Planning:
   Buffers: shared hit=123 read=1
 Planning Time: 0.450 ms
 Execution Time: 0.209 ms
(10 rows)

An Index Scan reading 20 entries, no sort, and about a quarter of a millisecond instead of 20: almost a hundred times less work. And under the same load:

Terminal
# The same load, with the index
$ npx autocannon -c 20 -d 5 'localhost:3000/products?category=cables-42'
{"requests_per_s":7076.6,"p50_ms":2,"p99_ms":13,"errors":0}

From about 90 to about 7,000 requests a second, and the median from 200 milliseconds to 2. Nothing in Node changed.

Indexes are not free: each one takes disk space and slows down every insert and update a little, because it must be kept up to date. Add them for the queries that run often or are slow, matching their WHERE and ORDER BY columns in order, and check with EXPLAIN that they are used. The same habit finds the other database problems: the N+1 queries of Chapter 5, SELECT * on wide tables, and offset pagination that reads thousands of rows to skip them.

Quick check

  1. Why is the p99 latency more important than the average for users?
  2. In the first plan, what did “Rows Removed by Filter: 166500” tell you?
  3. Why (category, price) and not (price, category)?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

14.4 The event loop

Node runs your JavaScript on a single thread, the event loop. That is why one process can serve thousands of requests: while one request waits for the database, the loop runs others. It also means that any JavaScript that runs for a long time without waiting blocks every request in the process. Two routes do the same expensive work, hashing a password with 100,000 rounds, in two ways:

node/server.ts
app.get("/hash/sync", async () => {
  // DANGER: 100,000 rounds of hashing on the event loop: nothing else runs meanwhile
  const key = pbkdf2Sync("password", "salt", 100_000, 32, "sha256");
  return { key: key.toString("hex").slice(0, 8) };
});

const pbkdf2Async = promisify(pbkdf2);
app.get("/hash/async", async () => {
  // the same work on libuv's thread pool: the event loop stays free
  const key = await pbkdf2Async("password", "salt", 100_000, 32, "sha256");
  return { key: key.toString("hex").slice(0, 8) };
});

While four clients call one of them, ten others call /ping, which does nothing at all:

Terminal
# /ping, while other clients call /hash/sync
$ npx autocannon -c 10 -d 4 localhost:3000/ping
{"requests_per_s":65.75,"p50_ms":137,"p99_ms":595,"errors":0}
# /ping, while other clients call /hash/async
$ npx autocannon -c 10 -d 4 localhost:3000/ping
{"requests_per_s":20223,"p50_ms":0,"p99_ms":2,"errors":0}
# /ping alone, for comparison
$ npx autocannon -c 10 -d 4 localhost:3000/ping
{"requests_per_s":34970,"p50_ms":0,"p99_ms":1,"errors":0}

With the synchronous version, /ping fell from about 35,000 requests a second to under 100, and its median rose to over 100 milliseconds: it waited in line behind each hash. With the asynchronous version, the hashing runs on libuv’s thread pool, outside the event loop, and /ping stayed fast. The lessons:

  • Never use the …Sync functions of fs, crypto and zlib in request handlers.
  • Heavy computation in JavaScript (image processing, big JSON, complex reports) belongs in a worker thread, a separate process, or a background job (Chapter 10).
  • Watch the event loop delay (prom-client’s default metrics, Chapter 13): when it grows, something is blocking.
  • One process uses one CPU core for JavaScript. Run one process per core (several containers, or Node’s cluster module) to use the whole machine.

14.5 The order of fixes

When the measurements show a real problem, try the fixes in this order, cheapest first:

  1. Do less work. An index, a better query, fewer queries, smaller responses, no work the client did not ask for. Usually the biggest win.
  2. Do the work later. Emails, reports and calls to partners in background jobs, so the request returns at once.
  3. Do the work once. Cache answers that many requests share (Chapter 7), and let HTTP caches and CDNs serve what does not change (Cache-Control, ETag).
  4. Do the work in parallel. More processes or servers behind a load balancer. This works only for the parts that can be shared out: the database behind them does not get faster by adding web servers, which is why the database was fixed first.
  5. Get a bigger machine. Sometimes the cheapest option of all, and always worth pricing before a month of engineering.

Professional habit: a performance budget, checked automatically

Write down the targets (“the listing: p95 under 100 ms at 200 requests a second”), keep a load test for the main routes in the repository, and run it before releases against production-like data. A regression caught by a test costs a revert; one caught by customers costs their patience. And keep the before-and-after numbers of every optimisation: they are the proof that the work was worth it.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Optimising without measuringWeeks spent on code that was not the problemLoad test, profile, then fix the bottleneck
Testing with tiny dataMissing indexes invisible until productionProduction-like volumes
Looking at averagesSlow requests hiddenp50, p95 and p99
Queries without matching indexesFull table scans under loadEXPLAIN ANALYZE and composite indexes
Indexes on everythingSlow writes, wasted space, unused indexesIndexes for real, frequent queries
Synchronous work on the event loopEvery request slows down togetherAsync APIs, worker threads, jobs
Adding servers for a database bottleneckMore cost, same speed, a busier databaseFix the queries first
Caching to hide a slow queryStale data and stampedes, the query still slowFix the query; cache what is truly shared

Summary

  • Measure first: latency as percentiles, throughput as requests per second, under realistic load with realistic data.
  • Find the bottleneck with metrics, traces and EXPLAIN ANALYZE; it is usually a query.
  • A composite index matching WHERE and ORDER BY turned a full scan into reading 20 entries.
  • Node’s event loop runs one thing at a time: never block it; offload heavy work.
  • Fix in order: less work, later work, work once, work in parallel, a bigger machine.
  • Keep a performance budget and a load test, and record before-and-after numbers.

Quick check answers: 1. Each user makes many requests, so most users meet the slow ones regularly; and a fast majority pulls the average down, hiding a minority that waits for seconds. The p99 describes what the unluckiest regular experiences look like. 2. That the database read 166,500 rows per process and discarded them, to find the few hundred it needed: a full table scan, the sign of a missing index. 3. An index is sorted by its first column, then the second within it. With (category, price), the rows of one category are together and already ordered by price, so the query reads the first 20. With (price, category), the rows of one category are scattered through the whole index, and the database would have to scan most of it.

Practice projects

Each project starts from a measurement and ends with one. Keep the before-and-after numbers.

Project 14.1 · Easy

Index the shop’s real queries

Find and fix the slow queries of the shop from the earlier chapters.

You will practise: realistic data, EXPLAIN ANALYZE, composite indexes, and the slow query log.

  1. Generate realistic data: 100,000 customers, 1,000,000 orders and their items.
  2. Turn on log_min_duration_statement = 50 in PostgreSQL and use the API for a while; collect the slow queries.
  3. Explain each, add the index that fits, and explain again.
  4. Load test the affected routes before and after, and write the numbers in a short report.

Hint: the pg_stat_statements extension ranks queries by total time spent, which is often more useful than the slowest single query.

Project 14.2 · Medium

A load test in the repository

Make performance checkable by anyone, before every release.

You will practise: scripted load tests, performance budgets, and comparing runs.

  1. Write a script that seeds production-like data, starts the server, and runs autocannon against the five main routes.
  2. Write the budget for each route (p95 and requests per second) in a file, and make the script fail when a route misses it.
  3. Introduce a regression on purpose (drop an index, add a slow loop) and show that the script catches it.

Hint: load tests on shared CI machines are noisy; compare against a budget with a margin, and run the full test on a dedicated machine before releases.

Project 14.3 · Challenge

Off the event loop

Move a CPU-heavy feature off the event loop, and prove the rest of the API stays fast.

You will practise: worker threads, measuring event loop delay, and comparing designs.

  1. Add a route that generates a PDF invoice (or resizes an image) synchronously, and measure /ping under load while it runs.
  2. Move the work to a pool of worker threads (node:worker_threads, or the Piscina library), and measure again.
  3. Move it instead to a background job (Chapter 10) with a status route, and compare the user experience of both designs.

Hint: perf_hooks.monitorEventLoopDelay() measures how late the loop runs; plot it during each test.

Exercises

  1. Easy A route handles 2,000 requests a second with a p50 of 5 ms and a p99 of 900 ms. What does that tell you, and where would you look first?
  2. Easy Which index would you add for SELECT * FROM orders WHERE customer_id = $1 AND status = 'paid' ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 10, and why in that column order?
  3. Medium Ten clients call a route that does JSON.parse on a 50 MB file read with readFileSync. Describe what happens to other routes, and two ways to fix it.
  4. Medium After adding two web servers, the API is no faster, and the database’s CPU is at 100%. Explain, and propose what to do instead.
  5. Challenge The product page makes 12 queries, takes 300 ms, and is the most visited page. Propose a plan, in the order of this chapter, with how you would measure each step.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 15

The code works, is secure, observable and fast; now it must stay easy to change as it grows. Architecture: layers, modules, and where business rules belong.

15

Chapter 15

Architecture: Keeping Code Easy to Change

In this chapter you will learn to

  • recognise the signs of code that is getting hard to change
  • separate business rules from HTTP, databases and frameworks
  • describe what a use case needs as ports, and plug adapters into them
  • wire everything in one composition root, without a framework for dependency injection
  • organise code by feature, and enforce the boundaries with a test
  • know when a monolith is the right answer, and when to split

Every chapter so far added something the shop needed: a route, a query, a lock, a job, a metric. Each was small. Together they make a codebase where one route handler validates input, reads three tables, applies pricing rules, sends an email and formats the response, and the pricing rule appears again, slightly different, in another route. Such code works; it is just harder to change every month. Architecture is the set of decisions about where each kind of code lives, so that the shop’s rules can be understood, tested and changed without touching everything else. This chapter rebuilds the order feature with clear layers, small enough to read whole.

Bit

The most expensive bug I have seen was a discount rule copied into four route handlers. Marketing changed the rule; we updated three of them. For two weeks, orders through the mobile app got a discount that the website refused. No single line was wrong. The rule simply had no home.

15.1 Signs that structure is needed

  • Fat route handlers. A handler that parses, decides, queries, sends and formats does five jobs; changing one risks the others.
  • Rules in several places. The same check (“at most 10 of one product”) written in two routes, or once in a route and once in a job.
  • Tests that need everything. To test a pricing rule you must start a server, fill a database and fake an email service.
  • Afraid to change the infrastructure. Replacing the mail provider, or moving one table, means editing business code everywhere.

The cure is the same idea at several sizes: separate what the business decides from how it is delivered and stored. The decisions, the domain, change when the business changes; HTTP, PostgreSQL and the email provider change for technical reasons. Keeping them apart lets each change without the other.

15.2 Layers, from the inside out

The order feature now lives in one folder, with one file per role:

Terminal
# The order module
$ ls src src/orders test
src:
db.ts
orders
server.ts

src/orders:
kysely-store.ts
order.ts
place-order.ts
ports.ts
routes.ts

test:
architecture.test.ts
place-order.test.ts

The domain holds the shop’s rules and words, in plain TypeScript. It imports nothing: no Fastify, no Kysely, not even node: modules:

src/orders/order.ts
// the domain: the shop's rules, in plain TypeScript; no database, no HTTP, no framework
export type Line = { productId: number; quantity: number; price: number };
export type Order = {
  id: number;
  email: string;
  lines: Line[];
  total: number;
  placedAt: Date;
};

// errors that speak the business's language; each adapter decides what they become
export class OrderError extends Error {}
export class TooMany extends OrderError {}
export class OutOfStock extends OrderError {}
export class UnknownProduct extends OrderError {}

// a rule of the business, not of the database or the API
export const MAX_PER_PRODUCT = 10;

export function checkQuantities(items: { productId: number; quantity: number }[]) {
  for (const item of items) {
    if (item.quantity > MAX_PER_PRODUCT)
      throw new TooMany("At most 10 of one product per order");
  }
}

export const totalOf = (lines: Line[]) =>
  lines.reduce((sum, l) => sum + l.price * l.quantity, 0);

Rules here are pure functions and values, testable in microseconds, and their errors use the business’s words: “too many”, “out of stock”, not “HTTP 422” or “constraint violation”.

The ports describe what the use case needs from the outside world, in the use case’s own terms. They are interfaces, written by the inside for the outside to implement:

src/orders/ports.ts
// ports: what the use case needs from the outside world, described by the use case
export type OrderStore = {
  /** Takes the stock and saves the order, all or nothing; prices come from the store. */
  place(
    email: string,
    items: Omit<Line, "price">[],
    placedAt: Date,
  ): Promise<Order>;
};
export type Mailer = { send(to: string, subject: string): Promise<void> };
export type Clock = { now(): Date };

The use case is one thing the shop does, from start to finish: place an order. It checks the rules, asks the store to save, then sends the email, through the ports only:

src/orders/place-order.ts
type Deps = { store: OrderStore; mailer: Mailer; clock: Clock };
type Input = { email: string; items: { productId: number; quantity: number }[] };

// the use case: one thing the shop does, start to finish, through its ports
export function makePlaceOrder({ store, mailer, clock }: Deps) {
  return async function placeOrder({ email, items }: Input) {
    checkQuantities(items);
    const order = await store.place(email, items, clock.now());
    // the email after the order is saved, never inside its transaction
    await mailer.send(email, `Order ${order.id}: ${order.total / 100} EGP`);
    return order;
  };
}

Read it as a description of the business process; there is no SQL, no status code and no library in it. makePlaceOrder receives its dependencies and returns the function that does the work: dependency injection with nothing more than a function argument.

15.3 Adapters

Adapters connect the ports to real technology. This design is often called ports and adapters, or hexagonal architecture. The store adapter implements OrderStore with Kysely and PostgreSQL, including the transaction and the atomic stock update of Chapter 6:

src/orders/kysely-store.ts
export function kyselyOrderStore(db: Kysely<DB>): OrderStore {
  return {
    place: (email, items, placedAt) =>
      db.transaction().execute(async (trx) => {
        const lines: Line[] = [];
        for (const { productId, quantity } of items) {
          const row = await trx
            .updateTable("products")
            .set((eb) => ({ stock: eb("stock", "-", quantity) }))
            .where("id", "=", productId)
            .where("stock", ">=", quantity)
            .returning("price")
            .executeTakeFirst();
          if (!row) {
            const exists = await trx
              .selectFrom("products")
              .select("id")
              .where("id", "=", productId)
              .executeTakeFirst();
            throw exists
              ? new OutOfStock("Not enough stock")
              : new UnknownProduct("Unknown product");
          }
          lines.push({ productId, quantity, price: row.price });
        }
        const total = totalOf(lines);
        const { id } = await trx
          .insertInto("orders")
          .values({ email, total, placed_at: placedAt })
          .returning("id")
          .executeTakeFirstOrThrow();
        await trx
          .insertInto("order_items")
          .values(
            lines.map((l) => ({
              order_id: id,
              product_id: l.productId,
              quantity: l.quantity,
              price: l.price,
            })),
          )
          .execute();
        return { id, email, lines, total, placedAt };
      }),
  };
}

The transaction belongs here, in the adapter: “take the stock and save the order, all or nothing” is the port’s promise, and how a database keeps that promise is the adapter’s business. The HTTP adapter works the other way round, translating a request into a call of the use case, and the use case’s result or error into a response:

src/orders/routes.ts
export const orderRoutes =
  (placeOrder: PlaceOrder): FastifyPluginAsync =>
  async (app) => {
    app.post<{
      Body: { email: string; items: { productId: number; quantity: number }[] };
    }>("/orders", async (request, reply) => {
      try {
        const order = await placeOrder(request.body);
        return reply.code(201).send({ id: order.id, total: order.total });
      } catch (error) {
        if (!(error instanceof OrderError)) throw error;
        const status =
          error instanceof TooMany
            ? 422
            : error instanceof OutOfStock
              ? 409
              : error instanceof UnknownProduct
                ? 422
                : 400;
        return reply.code(status).send({ title: error.message, status });
      }
    });
  };

Deciding that “out of stock” means 409 is an HTTP concern, so it lives in the HTTP adapter. A command-line tool or a queue worker could call the same use case and translate the same errors differently.

15.4 The composition root

Somewhere, real pieces must be chosen and connected. That place, the composition root, is the program’s entry point, and it is the only file that knows every concrete class:

src/server.ts
const db = connect();
const placeOrder = makePlaceOrder({
  store: kyselyOrderStore(db),
  mailer: { send: async (to, subject) => console.log(`mail to ${to}: ${subject}`) },
  clock: { now: () => new Date() },
});

const app = Fastify();
await app.register(orderRoutes(placeOrder));
app.get("/ready", async () => ({ ready: true }));
await app.listen({ port: 3000 });

Here the “mailer” only prints, which is enough for a demonstration; swapping it for a real email provider is a change to this one file. Running it shows the layers working together:

Terminal
# An order
$ curl -s localhost:3000/orders -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"sara@example.com","items":[{"productId":1,"quantity":2},{"productId":2,"quantity":4}]}' | jq -c
{"id":1,"total":114000}
# Eleven of one product
$ curl -s localhost:3000/orders -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"sara@example.com","items":[{"productId":2,"quantity":11}]}' | jq -c
{"title":"At most 10 of one product per order","status":422}
# More than the stock
$ curl -s localhost:3000/orders -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"sara@example.com","items":[{"productId":1,"quantity":4}]}' | jq -c
{"title":"Not enough stock","status":409}
$ psql shop -c 'SELECT id, stock FROM products ORDER BY id'
 id | stock 
----+-------
  1 |     3
  2 |    46
(2 rows)
$ npm start
mail to sara@example.com: Order 1: 1140 EGP

The rule refused eleven wheels before the database was touched; the store refused four kits, rolled back, and left the stock as it was; and one email went out, for the one order that was saved.

Quick check

  1. Why does the domain file import nothing at all?
  2. Who decides that an OutOfStock error becomes a 409, and why there?
  3. What has to change to send real emails instead of printing them?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

15.5 Testing by layer

The reward for this structure shows in the tests. The use case can be tested with in-memory fakes for its ports, without a database, a server or a mail provider:

test/place-order.test.ts
function fakes(store: OrderStore["place"]) {
  const sent: string[] = [];
  const placeOrder = makePlaceOrder({
    store: { place: store },
    mailer: { send: async (to) => void sent.push(to) },
    clock: { now: () => new Date("2026-11-20T10:00:00Z") },
  });
  return { placeOrder, sent };
}

test("places an order, saves it and sends one email", async () => {
  const saved: unknown[] = [];
  const { placeOrder, sent } = fakes(async (email, items, placedAt) => {
    const lines = items.map((i) => ({ ...i, price: 6000 }));
    saved.push({ email, placedAt });
    return { id: 7, email, lines, total: 12000, placedAt };
  });
  const order = await placeOrder({
    email: "sara@example.com",
    items: [{ productId: 2, quantity: 2 }],
  });
  assert.equal(order.total, 12000);
  assert.deepEqual(saved, [
    { email: "sara@example.com", placedAt: new Date("2026-11-20T10:00:00Z") },
  ]);
  assert.deepEqual(sent, ["sara@example.com"]);
});

test("refuses more than 10 of one product, before touching the store", async () => {
  const { placeOrder } = fakes(async () =>
    assert.fail("the store must not be called"),
  );
  await assert.rejects(
    placeOrder({ email: "a@b.c", items: [{ productId: 1, quantity: 11 }] }),
    TooMany,
  );
});

Each test sets up exactly the situation it needs: a store that succeeds, one that reports too little stock, one that must not be called at all. The adapter is tested against a real database, as in Chapter 12, and only for what it adds: SQL, transactions and the mapping of rows.

Boundaries that only exist in people’s heads erode under deadlines. An architecture test makes them part of the build:

test/architecture.test.ts
const rules: Record<string, RegExp> = {
  "order.ts": /^$/,
  "ports.ts": /^\.\/order\.ts$/,
  "place-order.ts": /^\.\/(order|ports)\.ts$/,
};

test("each layer imports only what it may", () => {
  const folder = "src/orders";
  for (const file of readdirSync(folder)) {
    const allowed = rules[file];
    if (!allowed) continue;
    const source = readFileSync(`${folder}/${file}`, "utf8");
    for (const [, from] of source.matchAll(/from "([^"]+)"/g)) {
      assert.match(from!, allowed, `${file} must not import ${from}`);
    }
  }
});
Terminal
# The tests: a use case without a database, and the rules
$ npm test
✔ each layer imports only what it may (…ms)
✔ places an order, saves it and sends one email (…ms)
✔ refuses more than 10 of one product, before touching the store (…ms)
✔ sends no email when the store reports too little stock (…ms)
ℹ tests 4
ℹ suites 0
ℹ pass 4
ℹ fail 0
ℹ cancelled 0
ℹ skipped 0
ℹ todo 0

When someone, in a hurry, imports the database into the domain, the build says so:

Terminal
# Someone imports the database into the domain
$ npm test -- test/architecture.test.ts
✖ each layer imports only what it may
AssertionError [ERR_ASSERTION]: order.ts must not import ../db.ts

This handmade check covers one folder; tools such as dependency-cruiser and ESLint’s import rules do the same for a whole codebase.

Watch out: architecture is a cost too

Every port and adapter is a file to read and an indirection to follow. For a route that reads one row and returns it, a use case and a repository are ceremony; keep such routes simple. Introduce layers where they pay: where business rules live, where several entry points share behaviour, where tests are slow because everything is tangled. The goal is code that is easy to change, not code that looks like a diagram.

15.6 Modules, monoliths and services

  • Organise by feature, not by kind. orders/, catalog/, accounts/, each with its domain, use cases and adapters, rather than controllers/, services/, repositories/ holding a bit of everything. A change to ordering then touches one folder.
  • Modules talk through their public functions. orders may call catalog’s exported use cases, but must not read catalog’s tables or import its internals. The same architecture test can enforce it.
  • A modular monolith first. One deployable application, well divided into modules, is the right shape for almost every team: one build, one database, transactions that span modules, and simple debugging.
  • Services when there is a reason. Splitting into separate services pays off when parts must scale very differently, be deployed by different teams independently, or be isolated for safety. The cost is real: network calls that fail, data spread across databases without shared transactions, and the tracing of Chapter 13 becoming a necessity. Clear modules make a later split easy; a tangle split into services is a distributed tangle.

Professional habit: write decisions down

When you make a structural decision (“orders do not read catalog tables”, “we use a modular monolith”), write a short architecture decision record: the context, the decision, the alternatives and the consequences, one page in docs/decisions/. A year later, the next developer, often yourself, will know why, and can change the decision on purpose instead of by accident.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Business rules in route handlersRules copied, tested only through HTTPDomain functions and use cases
Domain code importing the database or frameworkRules tied to technology; slow testsPorts owned by the inside; adapters outside
Status codes or SQL errors in the domainBusiness code speaks technical languageDomain errors, translated by adapters
Dependencies created inside functionsNothing can be replaced in testsInject them; wire in the composition root
Folders by kind for a large appEvery change touches every folderFolders by feature
Layers for every trivial routeCeremony that hides simple codeStructure where rules and reuse live
Microservices on day oneNetwork failures and distributed data for no gainA modular monolith, split when needed
Boundaries only in people’s headsThey erode under deadlinesArchitecture tests in the build

Summary

  • Architecture decides where each kind of code lives, so rules can change without touching delivery and storage.
  • The domain holds rules and words in plain code, importing nothing technical; errors speak the business’s language.
  • Use cases orchestrate a business action through ports; adapters implement the ports with real technology.
  • A single composition root chooses and wires the concrete pieces; dependency injection is a function argument.
  • Use cases are tested with fakes in milliseconds; adapters against real infrastructure; boundaries with an architecture test.
  • Organise by feature; prefer a modular monolith; split into services only for a concrete reason.

Quick check answers: 1. So that the shop’s rules depend on nothing that changes for technical reasons: they can be read without knowing Fastify or Kysely, tested in microseconds without setup, and reused by any entry point (HTTP, a job, a script). 2. The HTTP adapter, routes.ts: a status code is part of HTTP, not of the business. Another adapter, such as a command-line tool, would translate the same error into its own form, a message and an exit code. 3. Only the composition root, server.ts: create an adapter that implements Mailer with the provider’s library, and pass it to makePlaceOrder. The domain, the use case and the tests do not change.

Coming up in Chapter 16

The code is ready; now it must run the same everywhere. Docker: images, containers, Compose for the whole stack, and shipping to production.

16

Chapter 16

Docker: Shipping the Same Thing Everywhere

In this chapter you will learn to

  • explain images, containers and layers, and why they end “it works on my machine”
  • write a production Dockerfile for the Node service, small and safe
  • run the service and its database together with Docker Compose, with health checks
  • run one-off commands such as migrations in the same image
  • prepare containers for production: configuration, secrets, logs, workers and releases

The shop now needs a precise list of things to run: a particular version of Node, its dependencies, PostgreSQL, Redis, environment variables, a worker. On a developer’s laptop that list lives in someone’s memory; on a server, in a setup document that is always slightly out of date. Docker turns the list into a file that a machine follows exactly. The result, an image, runs the same on a laptop, in CI and in production. Every example in this book has quietly used it: PostgreSQL and Redis ran in containers from the first chapter. Now the shop itself goes into one.

Bit

My first deployment checklist had 41 steps, and a new server took a day. One step was “install the image library, version 2.x (not 3!)”. When we moved to Docker, the checklist became one command, and the “not 3” lived in a file that CI checked on every change. I have not missed the checklist once.

16.1 Images and containers

  • An image is a read-only package: a minimal operating system, the runtime, your code and its dependencies, and the command to start it. It is built once and copied anywhere.
  • A container is a running instance of an image, isolated from the host and from other containers: its own files, processes and network address, sharing the host’s kernel. Containers start in a second and are thrown away freely; nothing written inside one survives its removal unless it is in a volume.
  • An image is a stack of layers, one per instruction of its Dockerfile. Docker caches each layer and rebuilds only from the first instruction whose inputs changed, which is why the order of instructions matters.

16.2 A production Dockerfile

The service is a small Fastify app that reads the products from PostgreSQL. Its Dockerfile has two stages: the first installs the production dependencies, the second copies only what is needed to run:

Dockerfile
# stage 1: the production dependencies only, from the lock file
FROM node:22-slim AS deps
WORKDIR /app
# copied before the code: this layer is rebuilt only when the dependencies change
COPY package.json package-lock.json ./
RUN npm ci --omit=dev

# stage 2: the image that runs; nothing from stage 1 except node_modules
FROM node:22-slim
WORKDIR /app
ENV NODE_ENV=production
COPY --from=deps /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY package.json ./
COPY src ./src
# never run as root inside the container
USER node
EXPOSE 3000
# Docker asks the app itself whether it is healthy
HEALTHCHECK --interval=5s --timeout=2s \
  CMD node -e "fetch('http://localhost:3000/health').then(r => process.exit(r.ok ? 0 : 1)).catch(() => process.exit(1))"
CMD ["node", "src/server.ts"]
  • Dependencies before code. package.json and the lock file are copied first and npm ci runs on them alone; editing the code then rebuilds only the last layers, in seconds. npm ci installs exactly the lock file’s versions, and --omit=dev leaves out TypeScript, test tools and the rest.
  • No build step. Node 22 runs TypeScript files directly by stripping their types (Chapter 2), so the image runs src/server.ts as it is. Projects that compile add a build stage whose output is copied in the same way.
  • Not as root. The official image provides a node user; if an attacker found a hole in the app, they would not be root inside the container.
  • A health check. Docker asks the app’s own /health route (Chapter 13) every five seconds, and reports the container as healthy or not.
  • A .dockerignore keeps node_modules, .env and .git out of the build, so local files and secrets never end up in the image.
Terminal
# Build the image
$ docker build -t shop-node .
# What was built
$ docker image ls shop-node --format '{{.Repository}}:{{.Tag}}  {{.Size}}'
shop-node:latest  345MB

Size matters: every image is downloaded by every server that runs it, and every package inside is something that may need a security update. Slim base images, multi-stage builds and deleting build tools in the layer that used them all keep it down (exercise 3).

16.3 Compose: the whole stack

An app is rarely alone. Docker Compose describes several containers, their configuration and their network in one file, and starts them together:

compose.yaml
name: shop

services:
  db:
    image: postgres:18
    environment:
      POSTGRES_USER: shop
      # for a demo only: real passwords come from a secret store, never from this file
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: secret
      POSTGRES_DB: shop
    volumes:
      - db-data:/var/lib/postgresql
      - ./schema.sql:/docker-entrypoint-initdb.d/schema.sql:ro
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD", "pg_isready", "-U", "shop", "-d", "shop"]
      interval: 2s
      retries: 15

  app:
    build: .
    image: shop-node
    ports:
      - "3000:3000"
    environment:
      DATABASE_URL: postgres://shop:secret@db:5432/shop
    depends_on:
      db:
        # start the app only when the database answers
        condition: service_healthy

volumes:
  db-data:
  • Services find each other by name. Compose puts them on one private network, where the database’s address is simply db. Only the app’s port is published to the host; the database is reachable from the app, not from outside.
  • A named volume keeps the database’s files when its container is replaced. The first time the database starts, it runs the SQL files mounted in /docker-entrypoint-initdb.d.
  • Health checks drive the start order. condition: service_healthy starts the app only once PostgreSQL answers, and --wait returns only when every container is healthy.
Terminal
# The whole stack, with one command
$ docker compose up -d --wait
Volume shop_db-data Created
Network shop_default Created
Container shop-db-1 Created
Container shop-app-1 Created
Container shop-db-1 Healthy
Container shop-app-1 Healthy
# What is running, and is it healthy?
$ docker compose ps --format 'table {{.Service}}\t{{.Status}}\t{{.Ports}}'
SERVICE   STATUS                   PORTS
app       Up 5 seconds (healthy)   0.0.0.0:3000->3000/tcp
db        Up 8 seconds (healthy)   5432/tcp
# The app, from the host
$ curl -s localhost:3000/products | jq -c
[{"id":1,"name":"Robot kit","price":45000},{"id":2,"name":"Spare wheels","price":6000}]
# A one-off command in a new container
$ docker compose run --rm --no-deps app npm run --silent count 2>/dev/null
{ products: 2 }
# The app's logs
$ docker compose logs app
{"level":30,"msg":"incoming request","url":"/products"}
# Stop and remove everything
$ docker compose down -v
Container shop-app-1 Removed 
Container shop-db-1 Removed 
Volume shop_db-data Removed 
Network shop_default Removed

One command started the database, waited for it, started the app and waited for it too. The products came from PostgreSQL inside the stack. docker compose run started a new container from the same image for a one-off command, the way migrations and maintenance scripts run in production. The logs went to standard output, where Docker collects them (Chapter 13). And down -v removed everything, the volume included, leaving the machine as it was.

Quick check

  1. Why are the dependency files copied before the rest of the code?
  2. Why is the database’s port not published to the host?
  3. What happens to the products if the stack is stopped with down without -v, and started again?

The answers are at the end of the chapter, after the summary.

16.4 Containers in production

A container platform (a server with Compose, Kubernetes, or a cloud service that runs containers) takes the image and runs it. A few rules make that safe:

  • Configuration from the environment. The same image goes to staging and production; only environment variables differ (Chapter 9). Secrets come from the platform’s secret store, never from the image or a committed file.
  • One concern per container. The web app, the queue worker (Chapter 10) and the scheduler run as separate containers from the same image with different commands, so each can be scaled and restarted on its own.
  • Stateless containers. Anything that must survive goes to the database, Redis, object storage or a volume. A container can then be replaced at any moment, which is what deployments and autoscaling do.
  • Graceful shutdown. The platform sends SIGTERM and waits a little before killing; the app finishes its requests and the worker its jobs, as earlier chapters prepared.
  • Releases in steps. Build the image in CI, tag it with the commit (never only latest), scan it for known vulnerabilities, push it to a registry, run the migrations as a one-off container, then replace the running containers gradually while health checks confirm each new one.

Professional habit: the same image from CI to production

Build the image once, in CI, from a clean checkout, and promote that exact image through staging to production by its tag. Never build on the production server, and never change a running container by hand: the next deployment would silently undo it. If something must change, change the Dockerfile or the configuration, and ship a new image.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Copying all the code before installing dependenciesEvery code change reinstalls everythingDependency files first, code last
No .dockerignoreSecrets and local files baked into the imageIgnore .env, dependencies and .git
Running as rootA hole in the app gives root in the containerA non-root user
Secrets in the Dockerfile or the imageAnyone who pulls the image has themEnvironment variables from a secret store
Data inside the containerLost at the next deploymentVolumes and external services
Only the latest tagNobody knows what is running; no rollbackTags per commit or version
No health checksTraffic sent to containers that are not readyHealth checks and service_healthy
Several processes in one containerScaling and restarts affect everything togetherOne concern per container, same image

Summary

  • An image packages the runtime, code and dependencies; a container runs it, isolated and disposable; volumes keep data.
  • A good Dockerfile orders layers for the cache, installs production dependencies only, runs as non-root, and has a health check and a .dockerignore.
  • Compose starts the app and its services on a private network, in order, with health checks; run executes one-off commands in the same image.
  • In production: configuration and secrets from the environment, one concern per container, stateless containers, graceful shutdown.
  • Release by building once in CI, tagging, scanning, migrating, and replacing containers gradually.

Quick check answers: 1. Docker rebuilds from the first instruction whose inputs changed. Copying only the dependency files first means the expensive install layer is reused as long as dependencies do not change, so an ordinary code change rebuilds in seconds instead of reinstalling everything. 2. Only the app needs to reach it, through the private network; publishing it would expose the database to anything that can reach the host, and nothing outside the stack has a reason to connect. 3. They are still there: down removes the containers and the network, but keeps the named volume, so the new database container finds the same files (and does not run the initialisation scripts again, since the database already exists). -v removes the volume too.

Exercises
