  4. Medium After docker compose up, the app crashes because it cannot reach the database, but a minute later everything works. Explain, and fix it in the Compose file.
  5. Challenge Describe the steps of a release that adds a required column to a table, from merging the pull request to all containers running the new code, without downtime.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Coming up in Chapter 17

Everything is in place. The final project brings the whole book together: a complete, secure, tested and observable shop API, built from an empty folder to a running container.

17

Chapter 17

Capstone: A Production API

In this chapter you will

  • turn a short brief into the endpoints, tables and quality goals of a real API
  • build accounts, products and orders in one codebase, each with the techniques of its chapter
  • make an order happen exactly once, even when the client sends it twice
  • send the order’s email from an outbox, never from inside the transaction
  • prove it with tests against a real database, then run the whole system in containers
  • check the result against a production checklist, and know what is still missing

Each chapter of this book solved one problem on a small example: a route, a transaction, a lock, a token, a job, a metric. A real service needs all of them at once, and the hard part is not any single technique but how they fit together. In this last chapter we build Bit’s shop API from an empty folder to a system of four containers: about 600 lines of TypeScript, each line run, each test passed, and each answer below printed by the real server.

Bit

Every section says which chapter a decision comes from. When something feels new, that is your cue to go back and read that chapter again: you will understand it better now that you see where it fits.

17.1 The brief

The shop owner writes: “Customers should create an account, browse the products, and order. An order must never be charged twice, and they should get an email when it is placed. It has to stay up when we are busy.” As in every real project, the first job is to turn that into something testable:

As a customer, I want to…Done when…
create an account and sign inPOST /auth/register and POST /auth/login return a user and a token; a stolen database opens no account
browse the products, cheapest firstGET /products returns pages with a cursor; no product is skipped or repeated, however large the table
place an orderPOST /orders takes the stock and saves the order together, or does neither; the same request sent twice makes one order
get an email when I orderExactly one email per order, even if the email service or the worker is down for a while

And the qualities that every endpoint must also meet:

  • Safe: passwords hashed slowly, tokens stored only as hashes, login attempts limited (Chapters 8 and 9).
  • Consistent: stock never below zero, money in integers, no half-saved order (Chapters 4 and 6).
  • Clear: every error in the same Problem Details form, every input validated (Chapter 3).
  • Observable: structured logs, metrics and health checks from the first day (Chapter 13).
  • Tested and shippable: tests against a real database, and one command to run everything (Chapters 12 and 16).

17.2 The plan

The project is one Fastify application and one worker that share the same code and the same database:

Terminal
# The project
$ ls src test; ls
src:
app.ts
auth.ts
config.ts
db.ts
metrics.ts
orders.ts
password.ts
problem.ts
products.ts
server.ts
worker.ts

test:
api.test.ts
Dockerfile
compose.yaml
package.json
schema.sql
src
test
tsconfig.json
FileHoldsFrom chapter
schema.sqlSix tables, their constraints and one index4, 5, 14
config.ts, db.tsSettings checked at start-up; the typed database2, 5
problem.tsEvery error the API can give3
password.ts, auth.tsHashing, tokens, the login limit8, 9, 7
products.ts, orders.tsCursor pages; the order transaction3, 6
worker.tsSends the emails in the outbox10, 6
metrics.ts, app.ts, server.tsMetrics, health, error handling; the composition root13, 15

Each route module is a function that receives its dependencies, { db, redis }, and returns a Fastify plugin. Only server.ts creates real connections; the tests create their own. That is the composition root of Chapter 15, at the size this project needs: no use-case layer, because the routes here are short enough to read whole.

17.3 Settings and tables

A service that starts with a missing setting and fails at the first request is a service that fails at 3 a.m. So the settings are read from the environment once, checked with zod, and the app refuses to start without them:

src/config.ts
import { z } from "zod";

// every setting comes from the environment, checked once at start-up: a missing one stops the app here, not at the first request
export const config = z
  .object({
    DATABASE_URL: z.url(),
    REDIS_URL: z.url(),
    PORT: z.coerce.number().int().default(3000),
    LOG_LEVEL: z.enum(["silent", "error", "warn", "info", "debug"]).default("info"),
  })
  .parse(process.env);

The tables carry the rules that must hold whatever the code does: an email is unique, stock cannot go below zero, a price is positive, and a customer cannot use the same idempotency key twice. The index serves the product pages, sorted by price:

schema.sql
CREATE INDEX products_price_id ON products (price, id);
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES users (id),
  idempotency_key text NOT NULL,
  request_hash text NOT NULL,
  total integer NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  created_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  UNIQUE (user_id, idempotency_key)
);

request_hash remembers what was asked with each key, so a key sent again with a different body can be refused. The outbox table, further down the file, holds messages that must be sent after a commit; we come back to it in section 17.7.

17.4 Accounts and tokens

Registration and login are those of Chapter 8: scrypt hashes with a salt, and an opaque random token of which only the SHA-256 hash is stored. Every protected route runs one function first:

src/auth.ts
// who is calling? (Authorization: Bearer <token>)
export const authenticate = ({ db }: Deps) =>
  async function (request: FastifyRequest) {
    const [scheme, token] = (request.headers.authorization ?? "").split(" ");
    if (scheme !== "Bearer" || !token) throw problems.unauthenticated();
    const session = await db
      .selectFrom("sessions")
      .select("user_id")
      .where("token_hash", "=", digest(token))
      .where("expires_at", ">", sql<Date>`now()`)
      .executeTakeFirst();
    if (!session) throw problems.unauthenticated();
    request.userId = session.user_id;
  };

The login route adds the limit of Chapter 9, with one change that matters in production: the counts live in Redis, passed to @fastify/rate-limit in app.ts. With the counts in memory, three copies of the app behind a load balancer would allow three times the attempts, and a restart would forget them all:

src/auth.ts
    app.post(
      "/auth/login",
      {
        // 5 tries a minute per address and email, counted in Redis: every copy of the app shares the count
        config: {
          rateLimit: {
            max: 5,
            timeWindow: "1 minute",
            hook: "preHandler",
            keyGenerator: (request) =>
              `login:${request.ip}:${(request.body as { email?: string }).email}`,
          },

17.5 Products, page by page

The product list uses keyset pagination from Chapter 3: the cursor is the last row’s price and id, and the next page starts after that pair. With the index (price, id) PostgreSQL jumps straight to the right place, so page 1000 costs what page 1 costs (Chapter 14). The cursor is opaque to clients, and a cursor that does not decode is a 422, not a 500:

src/products.ts
      async (request) => {
        const { limit, cursor } = request.query;
        let query = db
          .selectFrom("products")
          .select(["id", "name", "price", "stock"])
          .orderBy("price")
          .orderBy("id")
          .limit(limit + 1);
        if (cursor) {
          // the cursor is the last row's price and id; the index (price, id) finds where to start
          const [price, id] = decode(cursor);
          query = query.where((eb) =>
            eb(eb.refTuple("price", "id"), ">", eb.tuple(price, id)),
          );
        }
        const rows = await query.execute();
        const page = rows.slice(0, limit);
        const last = page.at(-1);
        return {
          data: page,
          next_cursor:
            rows.length > limit && last ? encode(last.price, last.id) : null,
        };
      },

The id in the cursor is not decoration: two products may share a price, and without a second, unique key a page boundary between them would skip one or show it twice.

17.6 Orders that happen once

The order is the heart of the shop, and the place where most of the book meets. Phones lose their connection mid-request; the app retries; the customer taps twice. If each request made an order, the customer would pay twice. The client therefore sends an Idempotency-Key header, a value it chooses once per order and repeats on every retry. The whole order is one transaction (Chapter 6), and its first statement claims the key:

src/orders.ts
        const result = await db.transaction().execute(async (trx) => {
          const created = await trx
            .insertInto("orders")
            .values({ user_id: userId, idempotency_key: key, request_hash: hash })
            .onConflict((c) =>
              c.columns(["user_id", "idempotency_key"]).doNothing(),
            )
            .returning("id")
            .executeTakeFirst();
          if (!created) {
            // the key was used before: return that order, unless the body is different
            const old = await trx
              .selectFrom("orders")
              .select(["id", "request_hash"])
              .where("user_id", "=", userId)
              .where("idempotency_key", "=", key)
              .executeTakeFirstOrThrow();
            if (old.request_hash !== hash) throw problems.keyReused();
            return { id: old.id, replayed: true };
          }

          let total = 0;

The unique constraint does the hard work. If two copies of the same request arrive together, the second INSERT waits for the first transaction, then finds the key taken and returns that order. No lock in the code, no race: the database already guarantees it. If the key comes back with a different body, the client has a bug, and saying so is better than guessing. (The refine on the body refuses the same product twice in one order, which the table’s primary key would refuse less politely, with a 500.)

Then each line takes its stock with the conditional update of Chapter 6. If any line is short, the error rolls the whole transaction back: the order row, the lines already taken and the claimed key all disappear, so the client may fix the order and retry with the same key:

src/orders.ts
          let total = 0;
          for (const { product_id, quantity } of request.body.items) {
            // the stock is taken only if it is enough, in the same statement
            const product = await trx
              .updateTable("products")
              .set((eb) => ({ stock: eb("stock", "-", quantity) }))
              .where("id", "=", product_id)
              .where("stock", ">=", quantity)
              .returning("price")
              .executeTakeFirst();
            if (!product) {
              const left = await trx
                .selectFrom("products")
                .select("stock")
                .where("id", "=", product_id)
                .executeTakeFirst();
              throw left
                ? problems.outOfStock(product_id, left.stock)
                : problems.unknownProduct(product_id);
            }
            total += product.price * quantity;
            await trx
              .insertInto("order_items")
              .values({
                order_id: created.id,
                product_id,
                quantity,
                price: product.price,
              })
              .execute();
          }
          await trx
            .updateTable("orders")
            .set({ total })
            .where("id", "=", created.id)
            .execute();
          // the email is a row in the same transaction; the worker sends it after the commit
          await trx
            .insertInto("outbox")
            .values({
              topic: "order.placed",
              payload: JSON.stringify({ order_id: created.id }),
            })
            .execute();
          return { id: created.id, replayed: false };

Bit

Look at what the order does not do: it does not send the email. That one missing line is the difference between a shop that sometimes emails customers about orders that do not exist, and one that never does.

Quick check

  1. Why is the key claimed with INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING rather than by first checking whether it exists?
  2. An order has two lines, and the second is out of stock. What is left in the database afterwards?
  3. Why does the login limit keep its counts in Redis instead of memory?

17.7 The outbox and the worker

Sending the email inside the transaction is wrong twice: the transaction may still roll back after the email has gone, and a slow email service would hold the order’s locks. Sending it after the commit is better, but if the process dies between the commit and the send, the email is lost. The outbox removes both problems: the order writes a row into the outbox table in the same transaction, so the message exists if and only if the order does. A separate worker sends what the outbox holds:

src/worker.ts
// the worker: send what the outbox holds, mark it sent, repeat
while (running) {
  const sent = await db.transaction().execute(async (trx) => {
    const rows = await trx
      .selectFrom("outbox")
      .select(["id", "payload"])
      .where("sent_at", "is", null)
      .orderBy("id")
      .limit(10)
      // SKIP LOCKED: two workers never take the same row
      .forUpdate()
      .skipLocked()
      .execute();
    for (const row of rows) {
      const { order_id } = row.payload as { order_id: number };
      const { email } = await trx
        .selectFrom("orders")
        .innerJoin("users", "users.id", "orders.user_id")
        .select("users.email")
        .where("orders.id", "=", order_id)
        .executeTakeFirstOrThrow();
      console.log(JSON.stringify({ msg: "email sent", to: email, order_id }));
      await trx
        .updateTable("outbox")
        .set({ sent_at: sql`now()` })
        .where("id", "=", row.id)
        .execute();
    }
    return rows.length;
  });
  if (sent === 0) await sleep(500);
}
await db.destroy();

FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED lets you run several workers: each takes rows that no other worker holds. And the row is marked sent in the same transaction that sent it, so a worker that crashes halfway releases its rows to the next one. That makes delivery at least once: in a rare crash after sending but before the commit, the email goes twice. A real email provider accepts an idempotency key too, and the outbox row’s id is the natural one to give it. The queues of Chapter 10 work the same way; here PostgreSQL is the queue, which is enough for a few thousand messages an hour and needs nothing else to run.

17.8 Errors, metrics and health

app.ts puts the pieces together. One error handler turns everything into Problem Details: our own Problems as they are, validation errors with the field that failed, the rate limit’s 429 with how long to wait, and anything else as a 500 whose details go to the log, never to the client (Chapter 9):

src/app.ts
  app.setErrorHandler((error: FastifyError, request, reply) => {
    let problem: Problem;
    if (error instanceof Problem) {
      problem = error;
    } else if (hasZodFastifySchemaValidationErrors(error)) {
      problem = problems.invalid(
        error.validation.map((issue) => ({
          field: [error.validationContext, ...String(issue.instancePath).split("/")]
            .filter(Boolean)
            .join("."),
          message: issue.message ?? "",
        })),
      );
    } else if (error.statusCode === 429) {
      problem = problems.tooMany(Number(reply.getHeader("retry-after") ?? 60));
    } else {
      request.log.error(error);
      problem = problems.server();
    }
    reply.code(problem.status).type("application/problem+json");
    return reply.send(problem.body(request.url));
  });

Two endpoints answer different questions (Chapter 13). /health says the process is alive, and Docker restarts the container when it stops answering. /ready says the process can do its work, and a load balancer stops sending traffic while it is false:

src/app.ts
  // ready means the database and Redis both answer
  app.get("/ready", async (_request, reply) => {
    try {
      await sql`SELECT 1`.execute(deps.db);
      await deps.redis.ping();
      return { ready: true };
    } catch {
      return reply.code(503).send({ ready: false });
    }
  });

And server.ts is the only file that knows the real connections. On SIGTERM, which Docker sends before stopping a container, it finishes the requests in flight before closing the database pool:

src/server.ts
// the composition root: the only file that creates connections
const db = connect();
const redis = new Redis(config.REDIS_URL, { maxRetriesPerRequest: 1 });
const app = await buildApp({ db, redis });
await app.listen({ host: "0.0.0.0", port: config.PORT });

// on SIGTERM: stop taking requests, finish the ones in flight, then close the connections
process.once("SIGTERM", async () => {
  await app.close();
  await db.destroy();
  await redis.quit();
});

17.9 Tests against a real database

The tests use app.inject from Chapter 12: real requests through the real routes, without opening a port. They run against PostgreSQL and Redis, on a separate database named in .env.test, and each test rebuilds the schema from the same schema.sql that production uses:

test/api.test.ts
// each test starts from the schema file, on its own database
beforeEach(async () => {
  await sql
    .raw(`DROP SCHEMA public CASCADE; CREATE SCHEMA public; ${schema}`)
    .execute(db);
});

Each test states one promise from the brief. The most important is the first, which sends the same order twice and checks that there is one order, one stock deduction and one outbox row:

test/api.test.ts
test("places an order once, however many times the same key is sent", async () => {
  const auth = await signIn();
  const items = [{ product_id: 2, quantity: 3 }];
  const first = await order(auth, "key-0001", items);
  const again = await order(auth, "key-0001", items);
  assert.equal(first.statusCode, 201);
  assert.equal(again.statusCode, 200);
  assert.equal(again.json().id, first.json().id);
  assert.equal(await stock(2), 47);
  const outbox = await db.selectFrom("outbox").selectAll().execute();
  assert.equal(outbox.length, 1);
});
Terminal
# The tests, against PostgreSQL and Redis
$ npm test
✔ places an order once, however many times the same key is sent (…ms)
✔ refuses a key reused with another body (…ms)
✔ takes no stock when one line is short (…ms)
✔ refuses an order without a token (…ms)
✔ pages follow each other with no gaps and no repeats (…ms)
ℹ tests 5
ℹ suites 0
ℹ pass 5
ℹ fail 0
ℹ cancelled 0
ℹ skipped 0
ℹ todo 0

Watch out: tests that pass against the wrong database

Rebuilding the schema in every test is fast and makes tests independent, but it drops every table. That is why the tests read their own .env.test, pointing at shop_test. Never let tests fall back to the development settings, and never give a test runner credentials that reach production: one wrong environment variable must not be able to empty a real database.

17.10 Shipping it

The Dockerfile is the multi-stage file of Chapter 16: dependencies from the lock file, no development tools, a user that is not root, and a health check:

Dockerfile
FROM node:22-slim AS deps
WORKDIR /app
COPY package.json package-lock.json ./
RUN npm ci --omit=dev

FROM node:22-slim
WORKDIR /app
ENV NODE_ENV=production
COPY --from=deps /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY package.json schema.sql ./
COPY src ./src
USER node
EXPOSE 3000
HEALTHCHECK --interval=5s --timeout=2s \
  CMD node -e "fetch('http://localhost:3000/health').then(r => process.exit(r.ok ? 0 : 1)).catch(() => process.exit(1))"
CMD ["node", "src/server.ts"]

Compose runs the four parts. The app and the worker are the same image with different commands, so they can never run different versions of the code. Both wait until the database and Redis report healthy, and the YAML anchors (&env, *env) keep their shared settings in one place:

compose.yaml
  app:
    build: .
    image: shop-api
    ports:
      - "3000:3000"
    environment: &env
      DATABASE_URL: postgres://shop:secret@db:5432/shop
      REDIS_URL: redis://redis:6379
    depends_on: &ready
      db:
        condition: service_healthy
      redis:
        condition: service_healthy

  worker:
    image: shop-api
    command: ["node", "src/worker.ts"]
    environment: *env
    depends_on: *ready
    # the worker serves no HTTP, so the image's health check does not apply
    healthcheck:
      disable: true
Terminal
# The image
$ docker build -t shop-api .
$ docker image ls shop-api
shop-api:latest  371MB
# The whole system: database, Redis, the API and the worker
$ docker compose up -d --wait
Container shop-redis-1 Started
Container shop-db-1 Started
Container shop-redis-1 Healthy
Container shop-db-1 Healthy
Container shop-worker-1 Started
Container shop-app-1 Started
Container shop-worker-1 Healthy
Container shop-app-1 Healthy
$ docker compose ps --format 'table {{.Service}}\t{{.Status}}'
SERVICE   STATUS
app       Up 5 seconds (healthy)
db        Up 8 seconds (healthy)
redis     Up 8 seconds (healthy)
worker    Up 5 seconds

17.11 Proving that it works

Now the customer’s journey, against the running containers. An account, a token, and the products, two per page:

Terminal
# An account
$ curl -s localhost:3000/auth/register -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"sara@example.com","password":"a long password"}' | jq -c
{"id":1,"email":"sara@example.com"}
# A token
$ TOKEN=$(curl -s localhost:3000/auth/login -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"sara@example.com","password":"a long password"}' | jq -r .token)
# The first page of products, cheapest first
$ curl -s 'localhost:3000/products?limit=2' | jq -c
{"data":[{"id":2,"name":"Spare wheels","price":6000,"stock":50},{"id":4,"name":"Battery pack","price":9000,"stock":30}],"next_cursor":"WzkwMDAsNF0"}
# The next page, from the cursor
$ curl -s 'localhost:3000/products?limit=2&cursor=WzkwMDAsNF0' | jq -c '.data[].name, .next_cursor'
"Soldering iron"
"Sensor set"
"WzI1MDAwLDVd"

An order, then the same order again, as a phone would send it after losing the first answer. The second answer is a 200, not a 201: the same order, marked as a replay. Then the failures, each a clear Problem:

Terminal
# An order, with a key the client chose
$ curl -s -i localhost:3000/orders -H 'content-type: application/json' -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'idempotency-key: order-0001' -d '{"items":[{"product_id":1,"quantity":2},{"product_id":2,"quantity":4}]}' | grep -iE '^HTTP|^idempotent|^\{' | sed 's/\r//'

# The network failed and the client sends it again, with the same key
$ curl -s -i localhost:3000/orders -H 'content-type: application/json' -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'idempotency-key: order-0001' -d '{"items":[{"product_id":1,"quantity":2},{"product_id":2,"quantity":4}]}' | grep -iE '^HTTP|^idempotent|^\{' | sed 's/\r//'

# More than the stock
$ curl -s localhost:3000/orders -H 'content-type: application/json' -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'idempotency-key: order-0002' -d '{"items":[{"product_id":1,"quantity":4}]}' | jq -c

# Someone guesses a password
$ for i in 1 2 3 4 5 6; do curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code} ' localhost:3000/auth/login -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"email":"sara@example.com","password":"guess"}'; done; echo
401 401 401 401 429 429

Four wrong passwords answer 401, then the fifth attempt in the minute (the successful login counted as the first) is refused before the password is even checked. Finally, what the operators see. The metrics count every answer by route and status, the worker’s log shows exactly one email for the one order, and the API’s log has one JSON line per request:

Professional habit: a production checklist

Before a service takes real traffic, walk a short written list with the team: settings checked at start-up and secrets outside the image; migrations run as a separate step, never by the app on start; health and readiness probes wired to the platform; logs collected and an alert on the error rate and the latency; backups taken and a restore tried; rate limits on everything that costs money or guesses secrets; and a rollback that has been practised. The list is boring on purpose: incidents rarely come from clever bugs, mostly from a forgotten line on a list like this.

17.12 What you built

In about 600 lines: accounts with properly hashed passwords and revocable tokens; product pages that stay fast at any size; orders that are atomic, idempotent and never oversell; emails that are sent if and only if the order exists; Problem Details for every error; logs, metrics and two kinds of health check; tests that prove each promise against a real database; and one image, run by Compose, with its database, Redis and worker.

What a production version would add, each from a chapter you have read: migrations with a tool instead of one schema.sql (Chapter 5); secrets from the platform, not from compose.yaml (Chapter 16); HTTPS and security headers in front (Chapter 9); tracing across the worker (Chapter 13); and a CI pipeline that runs the tests, builds the image and deploys it. None of them changes the code you wrote; each one sits around it. That is the sign of a structure that is ready to grow.

Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensFix
Retried orders without an idempotency keyCustomers charged twice on a bad networkA client key, claimed by a unique constraint
Check, then insert, for the keyTwo requests both pass the checkINSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING
Email sent inside the transactionEmails about orders that rolled back; locks held while waitingAn outbox row in the same transaction
Rate-limit counts in memoryEach copy and each restart gives new attemptsCounts in Redis, shared by every copy
Offset pages on a growing tableSlow deep pages; rows skipped or repeatedA cursor on an indexed, unique sort key
Settings read when first usedThe service starts, then fails at the first requestValidate the environment at start-up
Tests sharing the development databaseLost data, and tests that depend on each otherA separate test database, rebuilt per test
App and worker built separatelyTwo versions of the code running against one databaseOne image, two commands

Summary

  • Turn a brief into user stories with a test for “done”, and write the quality goals before the code.
  • Let the database guarantee what must always be true: unique keys, stock never below zero, one order per idempotency key.
  • Make retries safe with an Idempotency-Key: claim it in the order’s transaction, replay the same answer, refuse a changed body.
  • Never do side effects inside a transaction: write an outbox row, and let a worker with SKIP LOCKED send it.
  • Keep shared state, such as rate-limit counts, outside the process, so any number of copies behaves as one.
  • Give every error one Problem Details shape, and every service logs, metrics, health and readiness.
  • Prove each promise with tests against a real database, and ship one image for the app and its worker.

Quick check answers: 1. Because checking first leaves a gap: two copies of the same request can both find no order with that key, and both create one. The insert claims the key and checks it in one step, and the unique constraint makes the second insert wait for the first transaction, then do nothing. 2. Nothing from this order: the error rolls back the transaction, so the order row, its first line, the stock taken for it and the claimed key are all undone, and no outbox row exists. The client may correct the order and send it again, even with the same key. 3. Because memory belongs to one process: with three copies of the app, an attacker gets three times the attempts, and every restart or deployment resets the count. Redis gives all copies one shared count that survives restarts.

Practice projects

These projects grow the API the way a real one grows. For each one, write the user story and its test first, put the rule where the database can guarantee it when possible, and run the tests and the whole system in Compose before calling it done.

Project 17.1 · Easy

My orders

Add GET /orders: the signed-in customer’s orders, newest first, in cursor pages.

You will practise: authentication on a route, keyset pagination, and an index that matches the query.

  1. Write a test first: two customers each place orders; each sees only their own, newest first.
  2. Add the route to orders.ts, with limit and cursor as in products.ts, on (created_at, id).
  3. Add an index on orders (user_id, created_at, id) and check with EXPLAIN that it is used.
  4. Test that a cursor from one customer gives the other customer nothing of the first one’s.

Hint: the filter on user_id must stay in the query on every page; the cursor only says where to start, never whose orders to show.

Project 17.2 · Medium

Cancel an order

Add POST /orders/:id/cancel: within 30 minutes of placing it, a customer may cancel, and the stock comes back.

You will practise: conditional updates, transactions, the outbox, and testing rules that depend on time.

  1. Add a status column (placed, cancelled) with a check constraint.
  2. In one transaction: set the status to cancelled only if it is placed, belongs to the user and is recent enough; then return each line’s quantity to the stock; then write an order.cancelled outbox row.
  3. Cancelling twice must be harmless: the second call changes nothing and says so.
  4. Teach the worker to send a different email for order.cancelled.
  5. Test the 30-minute rule by inserting an order with an older created_at, not by waiting.

Hint: put the whole rule in the WHERE of the update and check how many rows changed; then two cancels at once cannot both return the stock.

Project 17.3 · Challenge

Survive a busy day

Show, with numbers, that the API keeps its promises under load and when parts of it fail.

You will practise: load testing, concurrency, failure testing, and reading metrics.

  1. With autocannon (Chapter 14), send 2000 orders for a product with a stock of 100, from many connections, each with its own key. Check that exactly 100 orders exist and the stock is 0.
  2. Send 50 copies of one order with the same key at once, and check there is exactly one order.
  3. Run three workers (docker compose up --scale worker=3) and check that every outbox row was sent exactly once.
  4. Stop Redis while the app runs. What do /ready, /health and a login answer? Decide what they should answer, and make it so.
  5. Write what you measured: requests per second, the slowest 1%, and anything that surprised you.

Hint: for step 4, a limit that cannot be counted must still fail safely. Decide whether a login without Redis is refused (safer) or allowed (friendlier), and write the reason in a decision record.

Exercises

  1. Easy In the journey, the second order request answered 200 with the header idempotent-replayed: true, and the first answered 201. Why give them different status codes, when the body is the same?
  2. Easy Why does the product cursor contain the id as well as the price? Give an example with two products where a cursor without the id goes wrong.
  3. Medium The worker marks a row as sent in the same transaction in which it sends the email. Describe what happens if the worker crashes (a) before sending, (b) after sending but before the commit. Which of the two can cause a duplicate, and how can the email provider help?
  4. Medium A customer sends an order with key k1; the product is out of stock and the answer is 409. Ten minutes later the stock is refilled, and the client retries with k1. What happens, and why is that the right behaviour?
  5. Challenge Idempotency keys stay in the orders table forever. Propose how to let them expire after 24 hours without breaking the guarantee for retries within that time, and write the change to the schema and the query.

Answers to the exercises are at the end of the book and in the book’s code repository.

Where to go from here

You started this book able to build a small API, and you finish it able to design, secure, test, measure and ship a service that keeps its promises when the network fails, the traffic grows and the code changes. The next book in the series joins the two halves: a front end and a back end built, tested and shipped together as one full-stack product.

Appendix

Answers to the Exercises

Try every exercise yourself before you look here. When your answer differs from this one, that is not necessarily wrong: most problems have several good solutions. If yours handles normal and unusual cases, and you can explain your choices to a colleague, it is a good answer. The full, tested code for every answer is in the book’s code repository.

Chapter 1

  1. The status line is HTTP/1.1 200 OK; the headers are Content-Type, Content-Length, Date and Connection; then comes the empty line, and the body is the JSON object of 41 bytes. Without Host, Node answers 400 Bad Request by itself, before our code runs, so not even our log line appears: HTTP/1.1 requires the header, and Node enforces it.
  2. GET /products?max_price=100: safe and idempotent. PUT /products/2 with a full product: not safe, idempotent (the product ends up the same however many times it is sent). POST /products/2/reviews: neither; two requests make two reviews. DELETE /cart/items/7: not safe, idempotent. PATCH /products/2 with {"stock_change": -1}: neither, because each request takes one more item from the stock; a PATCH that sets a value ({"stock": 4}) would be idempotent.
  3. In handle, after the line that matches /products/:id, add: if the method is DELETE, find the product’s index with findIndex; answer 404 if it is -1, otherwise remove it with splice and answer send(res, 204). The first delete answers 204 and the second 404; that is still idempotent, because the server is in the same state after both. The new product, though, gets id 3 again, because the id is “number of products + 1”. A client holding an old link to product 3 now gets a different product, and if you had deleted product 1 instead, the new product would get the id of product 3, which still exists. Ids must never be reused: keep a counter, let nextId = products.length + 1, and use nextId++. With that change, the run in the code repository creates product 4. A database does this for you, with a sequence.
  4. The product list is public: anyone can read it with curl, so a page on another site reading it in a visitor’s browser learns nothing secret. CORS only stops other sites’ JavaScript from reading answers with the visitor’s browser. The danger is an endpoint that answers with private data because of the visitor’s cookies, such as GET /me/orders: there, letting any origin read the answer would let any site read the visitor’s orders. What protects it is not CORS alone, but the server checking who is asking (authentication), what they may see (authorisation), and cookies with SameSite, together with a short list of trusted origins.
  5. The answer must carry Content-Encoding: gzip, so the client knows to decompress; Content-Length with the size of the compressed bytes; and Vary: Accept-Encoding, so a cache never gives a compressed copy to a client that cannot read it. In send, check res.req.headers["accept-encoding"], compress with gzipSync from node:zlib, and send the buffer. With the three products of the run, the list is 128 bytes plain and 105 bytes compressed. That is a small saving, because gzip needs repetition to work with; on a list of a hundred products, the same JSON shrinks to a fraction of its size. That is also why servers do not compress tiny bodies: below about 1 KB, the work costs more than it saves. curl --compressed sends the header and decompresses the answer for you.

Chapter 2

  1. After about 50 ms: the timer cannot run before the busy loop ends, and the loop then finds it overdue. Without the busy loop it runs after about 10 ms, never earlier, and often a millisecond or two later.
  2. Add HOST: z.string().min(1).default("127.0.0.1") to the schema and call server.listen(config.PORT, config.HOST, ...). 127.0.0.1 accepts connections from the same machine only. 0.0.0.0 means “every network interface”: you need it inside a Docker container or on a server that must be reached from outside, and it is exactly what you do not want on a laptop in a café.
  3. Promise.all rejects as soon as the first promise rejects, at about 200 ms, with that error. The stock request is not cancelled: it keeps running and finishes at about 300 ms, and its result is ignored. The program in the code repository (answers/all-fails.ts) prints “Promise.all rejected at 201 ms: price service down” and then “stock finished anyway at 301 ms”. To really stop the other calls, give them an AbortController’s signal and call abort() in the catch.
  4. Read Number(url.searchParams.get("rows") ?? 100000), answer 422 unless it is a whole number from 1 to 1,000,000, and pass it to orderRows. Memory stays flat because the generator makes one row at a time, and pipeline only asks for the next row when the response can take more: at no moment do all rows exist together. When the client disconnects, the response is destroyed, pipeline rejects, and it calls the generator’s return(), so the generator stops at its current yield (any finally block in it runs). The handler’s catch sees the error and, since the headers were already sent, destroys the response instead of writing a 500.
  5. The pool in answers/pool.ts keeps a list of idle workers and a queue of jobs. run puts a job in the queue and calls #next, which gives the first job to an idle worker; when the worker answers, it goes back to the idle list and #next runs again. A worker that crashes is replaced with a new one, because a crashed thread cannot be reused. The worker file listens with parentPort.on("message", ...) instead of reading workerData once. In the run in the code repository, 100 counts with a new thread each took about 6 seconds, and with a pool of four about 150 ms: starting a thread costs far more than these small counts.

Chapter 3

  1. GET /v1/customers/3/orders (or GET /v1/orders?customer_id=3, which also works when orders are listed in other ways); POST /v1/carts/5/items; POST /v1/webhooks/payments, an endpoint the provider calls, which must check the provider’s signature and be idempotent, because providers resend webhooks; GET /v1/products?q=kit, a filter on the collection, not a new resource; POST /v1/orders/7/refunds, which creates a refund resource that can be listed and read later.
  2. 409 Conflict for the email, since the request is valid but clashes with existing data (some APIs use 422 to avoid revealing which emails exist; for a sign-up form, the security chapter discusses this choice); 428 Precondition Required; 412 Precondition Failed; 400 Bad Request, because the body cannot even be read; 422, because the body is readable and one value breaks a rule.
  3. Add q: z.string().trim().min(2).max(50).optional() to the query schema, read it with the other values, and add one filter: .filter((p) => !q || p.name.toLowerCase().includes(q.toLowerCase())). The cursor still works, because it records a position in the sorted order, not in the filtered list: the client sends the same q with the cursor, and the next page continues after that position among the matching products. In the run in the code repository, q=se with limit=1 returns “Servo motor”, then “Sensor set” with the cursor, and q=x is refused with a 400 problem. In a database, a search like this needs a suitable index, which Chapter 4 covers.
  4. One way: make cancel idempotent in its result. If the order is already cancelled, answer 200 with the order, because the client’s goal (a cancelled order) is reached. Another way: accept an Idempotency-Key on cancel too, and replay the first answer. The first is simpler and right here, because the second cancel changes nothing; keep 409 for orders that can no longer be cancelled because they were shipped. The general lesson: design actions so that repeating them is harmless, whenever the domain allows it.
  5. The class in answers/idempotency.ts keeps entries in a Map under the key client:key, so the same key from two clients is two entries. Each entry stores the time it was saved, read from a now function given to the constructor (by default Date.now); get deletes and ignores an entry older than the time to live. The tests pass a fake clock, () => now, and move now forward instead of waiting a day: a key is still there after 999 ms of a 1000 ms life and gone at 1000 ms. In production the client comes from authentication (Chapter 8), and the store is a database table or Redis keys with an expiry time, which also clears old entries on its own.

Chapter 4

  1. SELECT name, email FROM customers WHERE city = 'Cairo' AND id <= 7 ORDER BY name; returns Lina, Sara and Youssef. Both conditions must be true, so AND; the ORDER BY makes the order part of the answer instead of an accident of storage.
  2. Karim’s city is NULL, and NULL != 'Cairo' is NULL, not true, so his row is dropped. Write WHERE city IS DISTINCT FROM 'Cairo', which treats NULL as a value that is simply different, or WHERE city <> 'Cairo' OR city IS NULL. For the named customers, the result is Omar, Karim, Mona and Hana. Whether Karim belongs in the answer is a business question; the query must answer it on purpose, not by accident.
  3. SELECT p.name FROM products p LEFT JOIN order_items i ON i.product_id = p.id AND i.order_id > 100000 GROUP BY p.id, p.name HAVING count(i.order_id) = 0; gives Battery pack, Sensor set, Remote control and Gear box. The condition on order_id belongs in the ON clause: in the WHERE clause, it would remove the rows with NULL and turn the left join back into an inner join. WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM order_items i WHERE i.product_id = p.id AND i.order_id > 100000) says the same more directly.
  4. A partial index contains only the rows that match its WHERE: CREATE INDEX orders_pending_recent_idx ON orders (created_at) WHERE status = 'pending';. Since few orders are pending at any time, the index stays tiny however large orders grows, and EXPLAIN shows Index Scan using orders_pending_recent_idx. The query must include status = 'pending' for PostgreSQL to use it.
  5. Group by month in a CTE, then use two window functions over the months in order: sum(revenue) OVER (ORDER BY month) for the running total, and revenue - lag(revenue) OVER (ORDER BY month) for the change. lag reads the previous row, so the first month’s change is NULL. The query in the code repository shows the first months of the generated data; the first is small because the data starts on 19 November, part of the way through the month.

Chapter 5

  1. up: await db.schema.alterTable("customers").addColumn("city", "text").execute();; down: await db.schema.alterTable("customers").dropColumn("city").execute();. Name the file with a date after the existing ones, run npm run migrate, npm run migrate -- down, and npm run migrate again, then regenerate the types so Customers gains city: string | null.
  2. The generator sees a text column; the CHECK is a rule, not a type, so it cannot turn it into a union by itself. Tell it with --overrides: '{"columns":{"orders.status":"\"pending\" | \"paid\" | \"shipped\" | \"cancelled\""}}'. The run in the code repository generates status: "pending" | "paid" | "shipped" | "cancelled". Because the column has a default, wrap it as Generated<…> in the override, so inserts may still leave it out. A PostgreSQL ENUM type would be read as a union automatically, at the cost of a migration for every new value.
  3. (1) Expand: add title, optional; the old code runs unchanged. (2) Deploy code that writes both name and title and still reads name; old and new code can run together. (3) Backfill title from name in batches. (4) Deploy code that reads title (and still writes both, in case of a rollback). (5) Deploy code that no longer writes name. (6) Contract: make title NOT NULL, drop name. At every step, the versions running side by side agree on the schema.
  4. db.selectFrom("products as p").leftJoin("order_items as i", "i.product_id", "p.id").select(["p.id", "p.name", (eb) => eb.fn.coalesce(eb.fn.sum<number>("i.quantity"), eb.lit(0)).as("units")]).groupBy(["p.id", "p.name"]).execute(). The left join keeps products that never sold, and coalesce turns their NULL sum into 0. One query, whatever the number of products.
  5. Start a fresh database (pg-start --fresh in the book’s repository, or a CI service container), call migrateToLatest(), then call migrateDown() in a loop until it returns no results, then migrateToLatest() again, and set process.exitCode = 1 if any step returns an error. Run it in CI on every pull request that touches migrations/.

Chapter 6

  1. Nothing of the three changes is kept. The connection drops, PostgreSQL sees the session end without a COMMIT, and rolls the transaction back; its row locks are released at the same moment, so the transactions waiting for those rows continue. A crash of the database server itself has the same result: on restart, an uncommitted transaction never happened.
  2. Page views: an atomic update, UPDATE pages SET views = views + 1 WHERE id = $1, with no read in code; nothing to check, so nothing can race. A numbered seat: an atomic conditional update (UPDATE seats SET booked_by = $1 WHERE id = $2 AND booked_by IS NULL, and look at the number of changed rows) or a unique constraint on the seat in a bookings table. A unique username: a unique constraint (or unique index), and turn its error into a 409; checking first in code is only for a friendly message, never the guarantee.
  3. Make both transactions take the rows in the same order, lowest id first: B runs run("B", 1, 2) like A. Then B waits at product 1 until A commits, and continues; no cycle is possible. The programs take an ordered argument for this, and the run in the code repository prints “A: done” and “B: done” in both editions. With several products, sort them by id before locking or updating.
  4. The lock is held for the whole duration of the network call, which can take seconds or time out, and every other buyer of that product waits in the meantime; a slow payment service becomes a slow shop. Worse, if the charge succeeds and the commit then fails, money is taken for an order that does not exist. A better order: (1) a short transaction that reserves the stock with an atomic update and creates the order as “pending”; (2) the charge, outside any transaction, with an idempotency key made from the order id; (3) a short transaction that marks the order “paid”, or releases the stock if the charge failed. A worker cancels pending orders that stay pending too long.
  5. async function retrying<T>(work: () => Promise<T>, attempts = 5): Promise<T> {
  for (let attempt = 1; ; attempt++) {
    try {
      return await work();
    } catch (error) {
      const code = (error as { code?: string }).code;
      if (attempt === attempts || (code !== "40001" && code !== "40P01")) throw error;
      const pause = Math.random() * 20 * 2 ** attempt; // grows with each attempt
      await new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, pause));
    }
  }
}
// await retrying(() => db.transaction().setIsolationLevel("serializable").execute(work));
    The pause is random so that two transactions that collided do not retry at the same instant and collide again; it grows so that a busy moment gets time to pass. Only the whole transaction is retried, never a single statement, because everything it read may have changed.

Chapter 7

  1. The product price: a few minutes, with invalidation on every change, because a wrong price shown for minutes is tolerable only if changes also delete the key at once. The exchange rate: as long as its source updates it, often an hour, since every page reads it and it changes on a schedule. The list of countries: a day or more; it almost never changes, and a deploy can clear it. The login session: its idle limit, such as 30 minutes, renewed on every request; here the TTL is the rule itself (log out after 30 idle minutes), not a safety net.
  2. If the process stops between the two commands (a crash, a deploy, a lost connection), the key exists with no expiry. It is never deleted, the count only grows, and that client is refused forever. Send both in one MULTI/EXEC transaction, or use EXPIRE key 60 NX in the same transaction, as the chapter’s program does, or a Lua script that does both.
  3. Every cached product page that shows the category must be invalidated. Solution one: when a category is renamed, find its products and delete each product:{id}; precise, but it costs one query and many deletes, and every new place that shows a category name must remember it. Solution two: cache the product without the category name, cache categories separately under category:{id}, and assemble the page from both; a rename then deletes one key. The second is simpler to keep correct, at the cost of two cache reads per page (one round trip with MGET).
  4. Process A took the lock and paused for 7 seconds (a long query, a garbage-collection pause, a slow network). At 5 seconds the lock expired; process B took it and started the same work. For 2 seconds both worked at once, and when A finished, its release script saw B’s token and correctly left B’s lock alone. Protections: (1) make the work safe to run twice, with database constraints, conditional updates or idempotency keys, so a duplicate does no harm; (2) a TTL with a wide margin, and a watchdog that renews the lock (only if the token is still ours) while the work continues; (3) a fencing token: an increasing number given with the lock and stored with every write, so the database refuses a write carrying an older number than one it has already seen.
  5. node/sliding.ts
    // exercise 5: a sliding window, one sorted-set member per request, scored by its time
async function allow(client: string) {
  const key = `sliding:${client}`;
  const now = Date.now();
  const [, , count] = await redis
    .multi()
    .zRemRangeByScore(key, 0, now - WINDOW_MS)
    .zAdd(key, { score: now, value: `${now}-${Math.random()}` })
    .zCard(key)
    .pExpire(key, WINDOW_MS)
    .exec();
  return Number(count) <= LIMIT;
}
    Terminal
    $ node node/sliding.ts
request 1: allowed
request 2: allowed
request 3: allowed
request 4: refused, 429
(2 seconds later)
request 5: allowed
    Each request adds a member scored by its time in milliseconds (with a random suffix, so two requests in the same millisecond stay distinct). The transaction first removes members older than the window, then counts what is left. This version also records refused requests, so a client that keeps hammering stays blocked; to count only accepted requests, check the count before adding, in a Lua script.

Chapter 8

  1. The salt makes every user’s hash different, so identical passwords do not show as identical hashes, one computation cannot attack every row at once, and precomputed tables are useless. It does not make each guess slower: SHA-256 is still billions of guesses a second, so a weak password in any single row falls in seconds. The fix is a slow, memory-hard function (scrypt, Argon2, bcrypt), with a salt.
  2. No header: 401. An expired token: 401, since the server can no longer tell who is calling; the client should sign in again. A customer deleting a product: 403, as the customer is known and lacks the role. Another customer’s address: 404, as for an order, so that the response does not confirm that the address exists.
  3. Upgrade on sign-in, when the plain password is available for a moment: after a successful verifyPassword, read the cost from the stored string (its second part), and if it is lower than COST, compute hashPassword(password) and update the user’s row. Users who sign in get the new cost; for accounts that never sign in, you can wait, or force a reset after a long time.
  4. The route deletes every session of the current user:
    src/routes/auth.ts
      // exercise 4: sign out on every device
  app.post(
    "/auth/logout-all",
    { onRequest: authenticate },
    async (request, reply) => {
      await db
        .deleteFrom("sessions")
        .where("user_id", "=", currentUser(request).id)
        .execute();
      return reply.code(204).send();
    },
  );
    Terminal
    # Signed in on two devices, then out of both
$ curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}\n" -X POST localhost:3000/auth/logout-all -H "authorization: Bearer $PHONE"
204
$ curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}\n" localhost:3000/me -H "authorization: Bearer $LAPTOP"
401
    The request came from the phone’s token, and the laptop’s token, which was never used in it, now answers 401.
  5. Sign-in returns two tokens: an access token, a JWT valid for 10 minutes carrying the user id and role, and a refresh token, random bytes stored as a hash in the database with a long expiry. Every API request checks only the JWT’s signature and expiry, without a database query. When the access token expires, the client sends the refresh token to POST /auth/refresh, which checks it in the database, replaces it with a new one (so a stolen refresh token works only once), and returns a new JWT. Sign-out deletes the refresh token; the access token stays valid for up to 10 minutes, which is the price. The gain is no database lookup on most requests, which matters when many services check tokens; for a single API with a database already involved in every request, this chapter’s sessions are simpler.

Chapter 9

  1. A parameter can only stand for a value; ORDER BY $1 would sort by a constant string, the same for every row, and column names are part of the SQL itself. Check the input against a fixed list and use your own constant: const columns = { name: "name", price: "price" } as const, take columns[sort] only if sort is one of its keys (a zod z.enum(["name", "price"]) does exactly this), and refuse anything else with 422. In Kysely, orderBy(sort) with sort typed as that union is checked by the compiler too.
  2. CORS is enforced by browsers, and only for reading responses. Anyone can call the route with curl, a script or another server, none of which care about CORS; and even a browser on another site can still send many requests. Protection of data and actions comes from authentication and authorization on the server. CORS only decides which websites’ scripts may read the answers in a user’s browser.
  3. The browser first sends OPTIONS with the origin, the method it wants and the headers it wants to send; only if the answer allows all three does it send the real PATCH:
    Terminal
    # What a browser asks before a PATCH from the shop's website
$ curl -s -i -X OPTIONS localhost:3000/users/2 -H 'Origin: https://shop.example' -H 'Access-Control-Request-Method: PATCH' -H 'Access-Control-Request-Headers: content-type' | grep -i '^HTTP\|^access-control'
HTTP/1.1 204 No Content
access-control-allow-origin: https://shop.example
access-control-allow-methods: GET, PATCH
access-control-allow-headers: content-type
    204: no body, only permission. Access-Control-Allow-Origin names the shop’s site, so this origin may read the answers. Access-Control-Allow-Methods lists GET and PATCH, the methods configured. Access-Control-Allow-Headers allows content-type, which a JSON body needs. A Max-Age header could let the browser remember this answer and skip the preflight for a while.
  4. v-html inserts the name as HTML, so the browser creates an image with an invalid source, its onerror handler runs in the admin’s session, and the cookies (if scripts can read them) go to the attacker; a script could also act as the admin directly. Each defence stops part of it: rendering with {{ }} instead of v-html shows the name as text (the real fix); a Content Security Policy without 'unsafe-inline' refuses inline handlers, and connect-src refuses the request to another site; an HttpOnly cookie cannot be read by scripts; and validation on the server could refuse < and > in product names. Layers, because any one of them might be missing one day.
  5. (1) An internal address (http://localhost:6379, http://10.0.0.5/admin, the cloud metadata address http://169.254.169.254/) makes the server reach services that are not public, and may return their answers or secrets: allow only https, resolve the host name yourself and refuse private, loopback and link-local addresses, and do not follow redirects (or check every hop the same way). (2) A huge or endless file ties up the server and fills the disk: a timeout, a maximum size counted while downloading, and a check that the content really is an image. (3) A URL to a slow server keeps connections open to exhaust your workers: short timeouts, and the download done in a background job (Chapter 10) rather than inside the request. Better still, have admins upload the file instead of giving a URL.

Chapter 10

  1. Checking stock: in the request, because the answer (accepted or refused) depends on it, and it must happen in the same transaction as the order. The confirmation email: a job; the customer does not wait for it, and it needs retries. Charging the card: usually in the request, because the customer needs to know at once whether the payment worked; if the provider is slow or works asynchronously, the order is saved as “awaiting payment” and a job (or the provider’s webhook) completes it. The monthly invoice PDF: a scheduled job; nobody is waiting, and it is heavy.
  2. After the first failure the job waits 1 second, then 2, 4 and 8 seconds between the following attempts: the fifth attempt starts 1 + 2 + 4 + 8 = 15 seconds after the first failure, plus the time the attempts themselves took.
  3. Redis never heard that the job finished, so after the worker’s lock on it expires the job is considered stalled and is given to a worker again: the email would be sent a second time. The job must be idempotent: before sending, check whether the confirmation is already marked as sent (a confirmation_sent_at column set with a conditional update), and pass an idempotency key such as order-1001-confirmation to the mail provider so that it ignores a repeated request. Mark it sent right after sending; the remaining gap (crash between the provider’s answer and the update) is covered by the provider’s key.
  4. Update the data of each failed job, then retry it; the worker picks it up again, and its attempt counter continues:
    demos/retry.ts
    for (const job of await mail.getFailed()) {
  await job.updateData({ ...job.data, email: "karim@example.com" });
  await job.retry();
  console.log(job.id, "retried");
}
await mail.close();
await connection.quit();
    Terminal
    # The address is fixed; retry the failed job
$ node demos/retry.ts
order-1004 retried
$ node src/worker.ts --until-empty
 0.0s sending the confirmation for order 1004 (attempt 2)
 0.3s order-1004 sent
    In a real shop the address would be corrected on the customer’s record, and a job that carries only the order id would read it from there, so a plain job.retry() would be enough.
  5. Design one: separate queues, exports and emails, each with its own workers (or at least its own worker processes), so emails never wait behind exports; simple to reason about, and each queue scales on its own, at the cost of more processes. Design two: one queue with priorities (BullMQ’s priority option), giving emails a higher priority; fewer moving parts, but an email still waits for every export already running, and a busy export flood still slows everything. Separate queues are usually the better answer for work with very different urgency; priorities suit small differences within the same kind of work.

Chapter 11

  1. Delivery tracking: SSE (or polling every 30 seconds); updates flow only from the server, a few times an hour. Support chat: WebSockets; both sides send messages often, and typing indicators make it chattier still. Today’s sales refreshed every minute: polling; the data changes constantly but nobody needs it faster than the refresh, and a plain request is cacheable. Multiplayer quiz: WebSockets; answers go up and questions, scores and timers come down, with low delay both ways.
  2. The route on server 3000 would find no socket for order 1001, because the client is connected to server 3001, and would send nothing; the client would never hear about the change. With one server everything would look fine, which is why the bug reaches production.
  3. Keep recent events per order with increasing ids, for example in a Redis Stream (XADD order:1001 * status shipped, whose entry ids grow) or a database table, trimmed to the last hour or the last 100 events. When a client connects with Last-Event-ID, the route first sends every stored event after that id, then switches to live events, using the stored ids in its id: lines so that the next reconnect continues from the right place. If the id is older than what is kept, send the current state instead.
  4. It receives only the connection comment, nothing about the change:
    Terminal
    # A change while nobody is listening, then a client that connects afterwards
$ curl -s -X POST localhost:3000/orders/1001/status -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"status":"delivered"}'
$ curl -sN --max-time 1 localhost:3000/orders/1001/events
: connected to server 3000
    The message published while nobody was subscribed went nowhere: pub/sub does not store messages. This is why a connecting client must first get the current state from the database, and treat live messages as updates on top of it.
  5. Client: reconnect after a random delay that grows with each failed attempt (for example a random time between 0 and 2attempt seconds, capped at a minute), instead of at once; after reconnecting, load the current state with one request. Server: deploy gradually (one server at a time, so only part of the clients reconnect at once), close connections during shutdown with a “reconnect later” signal (SSE’s retry: field sets the browser’s delay), limit new connections per second at the load balancer, and keep the reconnect path cheap: authentication cached, and the initial state served from a cache rather than a heavy query.

Chapter 12

  1. The three table rows fail and the two other tests pass. The rows are exactly the cases where VAT is charged on a positive amount; the “fixed discount never goes below zero” case has nothing left to tax (VAT of 0 at any rate), and the unknown code throws before VAT is computed. So the bug is in the VAT step, not in the discounts or the codes, and the diff (8550 instead of 7980, which is 15% instead of 14% of 57000) points at the rate. Good tests narrow a bug down by which of them fail.
  2. The branch where process.env.BUG is set: the tests never switch the bug on. It should not be covered by a test; it is not a feature of the shop but a switch put there for this exercise, and real code would not contain it. That is why coverage is a flashlight: an uncovered branch is a question (“is this tested, should it exist?”), not an order to write a test.
  3. (1) Shared data: another test file, or a previous test, inserts orders into the same table; check whether the test resets its data and whether files share a schema. (2) Order of execution: the test counts orders that a test running earlier or at the same time created; run it alone with --test-name-pattern many times, and then with the whole suite. (3) Something asynchronous still running: an order created by a request or job that was not awaited lands after the reset; look for missing awaits and jobs or timers left running. Running the test alone a hundred times and checking whether it ever fails separates (3) from (1) and (2).
  4. The second integration test, “refuses an order larger than the stock, and changes nothing”, already asserts mailer.send.mock.callCount() is 0 after a 409. It cannot catch an email sent inside a transaction that later rolls back, because the double only records that send was called; in that bug the email is sent (the double records one call) and the order disappears afterwards, so the test that would catch it is the opposite one: force the transaction to fail after the point where the email is sent (for example with a failing insert), and assert that send was not called. The better fix is in the design: send after the commit, or through an outbox.
  5. Unit level: a pure function isUnpaidTooLong(order, now) tested with a table of times on both sides of 15 minutes, without any clock. Integration level: the job or scheduled command that cancels orders, run against a real database, with orders whose created_at is set in the past by the test (rather than waiting), checking that only the unpaid old ones are cancelled and that stock is returned. The clock is controlled by passing now in (or with mock.timers for Date), never by sleeping. One end-to-end check at most, if the cancellation is visible to customers, using the same past timestamps. The queue itself needs no test of its timing: trust the library, test your code.

Chapter 13

  1. {"level":"info","msg":"server started","port":3000}: the event, with what makes it useful. {"level":"info","msg":"signed in","user_id":42}: the user’s id instead of the email, and never the password (it must not appear anywhere; if it was logged, change the logging and treat the logs as a leak). {"level":"error","msg":"order failed","order_id":1001,"reason":"payment declined","trace":"…"}: one honest level instead of capitals and exclamation marks, and the order and reason as fields.
  2. The p95 is the time under which 950 of the 1,000 requests finish. 940 finished within 100 ms and 990 within 500 ms, so the 950th falls between 100 and 500 ms; Prometheus interpolates inside the bucket and would report about 180 ms. The p99 (the 990th request) is about 500 ms. With buckets this wide, add one at 250 ms if the exact value matters.
  3. Every customer creates a new series for every route and status, so the number of series grows with the customer base: after a month, hundreds of thousands of series, memory and storage in the metrics system explode, and queries become slow or fail. The question (“which customers get errors?” or “how many requests does this customer make?”) belongs to the logs or traces, which carry the customer id on each event and can be searched and counted when needed; or to a dedicated business table if it is asked often.
  4. Without a timeout, a database that hangs (rather than refusing connections) makes every readiness check hang; the platform’s check times out, but the server piles up open checks and connections, and recovery is slower. Checking a slow partner API is worse: when the partner is slow, every server reports “not ready”, the load balancer removes all of them, and the whole shop goes down because one optional feature is degraded. Readiness should check only what this server cannot work without, with short timeouts; partners are watched with metrics and alerts instead.
  5. SLO: over 30 days, 99.5% of checkout requests succeed (any status below 500) and 95% complete within 800 ms. Paging alert: the error budget is burning fast, for example more than 5% of checkouts failing over the last 5 minutes and over the last hour (both, so a single blip does not wake anyone); this means customers are losing orders now. Ticket alert: the budget is burning slowly, for example the p95 above 800 ms for three hours, or 1% of checkouts failing over a day; this needs attention during working hours, not at night. The difference is urgency for users: page only when people are affected now and someone must act at once.

Chapter 14

  1. Most requests are fast, so the route itself is not slow in general; one request in a hundred takes nearly a second. Something affects only some requests: a slow query for certain parameters (a big customer, a category without an index), a lock that some requests wait for, a cache miss that recomputes, an occasional call to a slow service, or garbage collection pauses. Start from the traces of the slow requests (Chapter 13) and compare them with fast ones: the difference shows where the time goes.
  2. CREATE INDEX ON orders (customer_id, status, created_at). The two equality conditions come first, so that the index narrows straight to one customer’s paid orders; within them the entries are sorted by created_at, so the database reads the last 10 entries (an index can be read backwards for DESC) and stops, without sorting. With created_at first, the index would be ordered by time across all customers, and the conditions would have to be checked entry by entry.
  3. Both readFileSync and JSON.parse run on the event loop: while each of the ten requests reads and parses 50 MB, which takes hundreds of milliseconds, no other request in the process can run, so every route becomes slow at once, and memory spikes as ten copies are held. Fixes: do not parse it per request (parse once at start-up, or keep the data in a database and query what is needed); or move the work off the loop, reading with the asynchronous fs.promises.readFile and parsing in a worker thread, or in a background job that stores the result.
  4. The bottleneck is the database, not the web servers: every web server sends the same expensive queries to the same database, so adding servers only sends it more work at the same time (and more connections). Find the expensive queries (the slow query log, pg_stat_statements, EXPLAIN ANALYZE), fix them with indexes and better queries, remove N+1 patterns, cache what is shared, and only then consider read replicas or a bigger database server.
  5. Measure first: a load test of the page with realistic data, p50/p95/p99, and a trace of one request showing the 12 queries and their times. (1) Do less: combine the queries with eager loading or joins, add indexes for the slow ones, select only the columns shown; measure again. (2) Do later: anything not needed to render the page (view counts, recommendations computed per visit) goes to a background job or is loaded separately. (3) Do once: cache the product data shared by all visitors in Redis with invalidation on change, and set Cache-Control so a CDN can serve the page or its API for a few seconds; measure the hit rate and the latency. (4) Only if the page is still too slow under the target load, add servers, checking the database stays healthy. Each step is kept only if the numbers improved.

Chapter 15

  1. The zod schema: the HTTP adapter, because it describes the shape of a request (another entry point would validate its own input differently). “At most 3 unpaid orders”: the domain, as a pure rule such as checkUnpaidLimit(count). The SQL that counts unpaid orders: an adapter, behind a port method such as store.countUnpaid(customerId), called by the use case. Sending a push notification: an adapter behind a port (Notifier), called by the use case after the order is saved.
  2. Because the transaction may still fail or roll back after the email is sent, and an email cannot be unsent: the customer would be told about an order that does not exist (Chapter 6). Sending after store.place returns means the order is committed. If the process dies between the two, no email is sent; when that matters, the use case writes to an outbox inside the store’s transaction instead.
  3. The rule (“members get 5% off the total”) is a pure function in the domain, next to totalOf, with unit tests for members and non-members and for rounding. Whether a customer is a member is data, so the use case asks a port (Customers.isMember(email)) implemented by an adapter that queries the database, and passes the answer to the rule; the store saves the discounted total. Tests: domain unit tests for the arithmetic; a use-case test with a fake Customers port returning true and false; an adapter test for the membership query; and one HTTP integration test that a member’s order total is discounted.
  4. Costs: network calls that fail or time out between orders and the rest; no shared transactions, so stock and orders can disagree and need sagas or outboxes; separate deployments, monitoring, tracing and on-call; data duplicated or fetched across the network; slower development while the boundary settles. Questions first: Which concrete problem would the split solve? Does orders need to scale differently from the rest, or be deployed by a separate team? Is the boundary already clean inside the monolith (could we split it tomorrow without rewriting)? If the answers are vague, keep a well-divided module and revisit when there is a measured need.
  5. Add a rule that walks every file under src/ and fails when a file imports kysely-store.ts unless it is src/server.ts. It matters because the store adapter is a detail: if routes, jobs or other modules import it directly, they bypass the use case and its rules, couple themselves to Kysely, and make it impossible to replace the adapter or test them with fakes. Keeping the concrete adapter visible only to the composition root keeps every other file depending on the port.

Chapter 16

  1. Set the working directory; copy the dependency files; install the dependencies; copy the source code. The working directory comes first because every later path is relative to it. The dependency files and the install come before the code so that their layers are reused whenever only code changes; copying the code last means an ordinary change rebuilds only that final, cheap layer.
  2. Everything in a Dockerfile ends up in the image, and anyone who can pull the image (CI, other teams, a leaked registry token) can read it with docker history or by running the image; the password is also committed to the repository with the Dockerfile, and the same image cannot be used with a different password in another environment. Pass it at run time instead, from the platform’s secret store: environment entries filled from secrets in Compose or Kubernetes, or an .env file that is never committed.
  3. (1) A smaller base image, such as an Alpine variant or a “distroless” one: often half the size, but Alpine uses a different C library that some native modules or extensions do not support, and distroless images have no shell for debugging. (2) Remove build tools in the same layer that used them (install native build tools only in the dependency stage): smaller and fewer packages to patch, but a slightly more complex Dockerfile. (3) Fewer dependencies: check what node_modules holds with npm ls --omit=dev and remove packages the app does not need; the trade-off is time spent, and sometimes features of the bigger option.
  4. Compose starts the containers in dependency order but, with a plain depends_on, it waits only until the database container has started, not until PostgreSQL accepts connections, which takes a few seconds; the app starts, fails to connect, and crashes (it works later after a restart or a retry). Give the database a health check (pg_isready) and make the app wait for it with depends_on: db: condition: service_healthy, as this chapter’s Compose file does; the app should also retry its connection at start-up, since databases restart in production too.
  5. Expand and contract (Chapter 5). Release 1: a migration adds the column as nullable (or with a default), and the code writes it for new rows while still working without it; CI builds, tests and scans the image; the migration runs as a one-off container (old code is unaffected by a new nullable column); the new containers replace the old ones gradually behind health checks. Then a backfill fills the column for existing rows, in batches. Release 2: a migration makes the column required (now safe, since every row has a value), and the code starts relying on it; deployed the same way. At every moment, the running code and the database schema are compatible, so no request fails.

Chapter 17

  1. Because they tell the client different things. 201 Created says “this request made a new order”; 200 OK with idempotent-replayed: true says “nothing new happened, this is the order your earlier request made”. A client normally treats both as success, but its logs, its metrics and any developer reading the traffic can see that a retry happened, and how often. Counting replays is also a good signal of network trouble between the app and the API.
  2. Because the price alone does not say where a page ended when several products share it. Say products 7 and 8 both cost 6000 and the page size is 1. Page 1 ends at product 7, and a cursor of only 6000 asks for price > 6000, which skips product 8. Asking for price >= 6000 instead would show product 7 again. With (6000, 7) the next page asks for (price, id) > (6000, 7), which is exactly product 8. The id makes the sort key unique, so every row has one place in the order.
  3. (a) Before sending: the transaction rolls back when the connection closes, the row lock is released, and the row is still unsent, so the next worker (or the same one after a restart) sends it. Nothing is lost and nothing is doubled. (b) After sending, before the commit: the email has gone, but the update to sent_at rolls back, so the row is sent again: a duplicate. Only (b) duplicates. The provider helps when it accepts an idempotency key: send the outbox row’s id with each email, and the provider recognises the second send of row 42 and drops it. Without such a key, a rare duplicate is the price of never losing an email.
  4. The second request makes a new order, and that is right. The 409 rolled back the whole transaction, including the INSERT that claimed k1, so no order with that key exists and the retry runs as if it were the first. The promise of an idempotency key is “the same key never makes two orders”, not “the same key always gives the same answer”: a request that failed and changed nothing may be retried, and the customer gets the order they wanted once the stock is back.
  5. Move the keys to their own table, CREATE TABLE idempotency_keys (user_id bigint NOT NULL, key text NOT NULL, request_hash text NOT NULL, order_id bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES orders (id), created_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(), PRIMARY KEY (user_id, key)), and drop the key columns from orders. The order inserts its key row in the same transaction, with ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING as now. A scheduled job deletes old keys: DELETE FROM idempotency_keys WHERE created_at < now() - interval '24 hours', in batches with an index on created_at. The guarantee holds for any retry within 24 hours, because the row exists until then; tell clients (in the API documentation) that a key is only remembered for 24 hours, so they must not retry with an older one.
